# SINGAPORE HEALTH DATA SHARING — CORE BUNDLE

### Legislative and normative instruments governing the sharing of health data in Singapore
### State of the law as at **15 August 2026**

# 1. HOW TO USE THIS BUNDLE

### 1.1 What this covers

Every legislative and other normative instrument identified as governing the sharing, disclosure, access to, or compelled surrender of health data in Singapore: Acts, subsidiary legislation, Bills, draft Bills and open consultations, licence conditions, ministerial circulars and written directions, codes of practice, professional ethical codes, advisory guidelines, national standards, certification frameworks, data access policies, grant conditions, programme terms, treaty commitments and case law.

It covers instruments that are **in force**, instruments that are **enacted but not commenced**, instruments **in passage or in consultation**, instruments **announced but never made**, and — separately in §6 — instruments that are **operative but unpublished**, which cannot be read by anyone outside the distribution list.

### 1.2 Retrieval date and the "as at" convention

**Every status statement in this bundle is as at 15 August 2026.** Where a source agent recorded a status "as at 30 July 2026" (the project's original nominal date), that has been normalised to 15 August 2026 and the underlying evidence re-checked against the later date. **Where a later retrieval strengthens a negative, this bundle says so rather than flattening it to the anchor date.** In particular, the commencement negatives in §4.15.4 rest on an e-Gazette index verified fresh through **17 August 2026**, so they are *stronger* than the 15 August anchor, not weaker. Nothing relied on anywhere in this bundle is dated after 17 August 2026.

The 1–15 August 2026 window was swept deliberately and separately; it is the richest recency window in the project and produced, among other things, the Genetic Information Bill consultation (7 August), Bill 17/2026 (4 August), the Implementation Guide v2.0 (14 August), GL-10-R1 (14 August), the FAQ v1.1 (13 August), and MOH's own statement that the Health Information Act's requirements "have yet to take effect" (14 August).

### 1.3 What "status" means in this bundle

Singapore's legislative process has five distinct stages, and the difference between them is the single most important thing in this bundle. The terms are used as follows and never interchangeably:

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Passed** | Voted through Parliament. No legal effect follows from this alone. |
| **Assented** | Signed by the President. Still no legal effect. |
| **Gazetted** | Published in the Government Gazette Acts Supplement. Still no legal effect. |
| **Commenced / in force** | A commencement notification has been gazetted (or the Act commences on a fixed date on its face). **This is the only stage at which an Act has legal effect.** |
| **Uncommenced** | Passed, assented and gazetted, but no commencement notification exists. The Act is on the statute book and has no legal effect whatever. |

For non-legislative instruments — codes, guidelines, licence conditions, circulars, certification frameworks, contracts — "status" means whether the document is current, superseded, or not yet effective, and the entry states separately **what makes it binding**, because in this field the answer is frequently "contract" or "licence condition" rather than "law".

### 1.4 Citation conventions and four traps

**(a) The Acts Supplement number is NOT the Act number.** This is the commonest citation error available in this area and it has already produced errors in the underlying research. The gazetted Acts Supplement **leaves the Act number blank on its title page**; the Act number appears only in the running header from page 2 onward and in the assent block. An extraction from page 1 alone yields the *supplement* number and nothing else.

- The **Health Information Act 2026** is **Act 1 of 2026**, published in **Acts Supplement No. 2 of 2026**.
- The **Public Sector (Governance) (Amendment) Act 2026** is **Act 2 of 2026**, published in **Acts Supplement No. 3 of 2026**.
- The mapping is not year-aligned (Acts Supplement 1 of 2026 carries Act 20 of **2025**) and not monotonic (Supplement 18 of 2025 = Act 17 of 2025; Supplement 19 of 2025 = Act 16 of 2025), because Act numbers follow assent order and supplements follow publication order.
- **The trap runs in both directions.** "Act 3 of 2026" (National Council of Social Service (Amendment) Act 2026) and "Act 17 of 2026" (Skills and Workforce Development Agency Act 2026) both look like supplement numbers and are **correct**. Both amend the Public Sector (Governance) Act 2018. Verify; do not assume in either direction.

**(b) The e-Gazette `notificationNum` field is not the Act number either.** It is the supplement notification number. The same caution applies.

**(c) Two dates appear on every Acts Supplement, and they mean different things.** The masthead caption is the *supplement's* date; the publication line gives the date and time of first publication in the Gazette. **Cite the publication line.** For the Health Information Act 2026 the caption reads Friday 13 February 2026 and the publication line reads 12 February 2026 at 5 pm: **cite 12 February 2026**. The same pattern applies to every Acts Supplement (e.g. the Communicable Diseases Agency Act 2025 — caption 14 February 2025, published 12 February 2025).

**(d) There are no Hansard column numbers for any modern Singapore debate.** Online Hansard has carried **no column numbering since 10 September 2012**. Every one of the 7,209 Hansard sections retrieved across 79 sitting days for this project has a page field of zero, and no PDF is published; the same was verified again, section by section, for the specific sitting this bundle needs — all 138 sections of 12 January 2026 carry start and end page numbers of zero and `onlinePDFFileName` is null. **Any "Vol X col Y" citation for a Singapore debate after 10 September 2012 is invented.**

A regex scan of all 45 findings files for `col N`, `cols N`, `col. N`, `column N` and `Vol …col` returned **zero offenders** — every hit is a table column. The corpus is clean. The risk is not an existing bad citation but the **open instruction to go and find one**, which appeared in two files and has been closed by supplying the correct form below.

**The defensible citation form, used throughout this bundle:**

> *Singapore Parliamentary Debates, Official Report* (12 January 2026) **vol 96, sitting no 12** (15th Parliament, First Session), Health Information Bill, second reading.

The Bill was moved by **Mr Tan Kiat How, Senior Minister of State for Health, at 4.05 pm** on behalf of the Minister for Health; debate resumed at 6.29 pm and he replied at 8.08 pm. The Public Sector (Governance) (Amendment) Bill appears as a Bill Proceedings section in the same sitting, so the same citation serves both.

A related caution: MOH's newsroom reproductions of Parliamentary Questions carry a running "PQ nnnn" reference which does **not** correspond to Hansard's within-sitting "Q n" numbering. The two are not alternative citations for the same thing. This bundle uses the Hansard sitting-date convention and gives the MOH newsroom reference separately where it is the source.

**(e) The 2025 Revised Edition trap.** HOTA subsidiary legislation **was** republished in the 2025 Revised Edition (in force 2 June 2025) and must be cited to it. HCSA subsidiary legislation — including the blood, cord-blood and human-tissue-banking Regulations — was **not** republished and must still be cited by S-number. Getting this backwards produces wrong citations in either direction.

**(f) Retrieval route.** The Government e-Gazette asset host is directly retrievable and is now the authoritative text (the Electronic Gazette and Legislation Act 2025, Act 5 of 2025, makes the electronic Gazette prima facie evidence). The working pattern for a subsidiary-legislation instrument is `https://assets.egazette.gov.sg/<year>/Legislative%20Supplements/Subsidiary%20Legislation%20Supplement/<number>.pdf`, where **the number is zero-padded to a minimum of three digits** — `066.pdf`, not `66.pdf`; `001.pdf`, not `1.pdf`; four-digit numbers such as `1030.pdf` are unchanged. ⚠️ A wrong path fails **silently and misleadingly with HTTP 403 AccessDenied**, not 404, which reads like a permissions problem rather than a bad path. Acts Supplement numbers are zero-padded to **two** digits (`02.pdf`, `09.pdf`); Bills Supplement 2026 uses a different scheme again (`26bills17.pdf`). The `subCategory:"Revised Acts"` facet of the e-Gazette index gives complete consolidated statute text as a gazette PDF and is a full substitute for Singapore Statutes Online.

**(g) The Hansard JSON API distinguishes "no sitting" from "error" by status code.** A date on which Parliament did not sit returns **HTTP 500**, not 404 and not an empty 200. That is a reliable negative test for whether a sitting occurred, and it settled one dating conflict in this bundle (§4.7.3).

### 1.5 Source marking

- **P** — primary source: the instrument itself, or the issuing body's own publication, was retrieved and read.
- **P (index only)** — the gazette or agency index record (title, number, date) was retrieved; the document's contents were not read.
- **P (described)** — the existence and described contents come from a primary document, but the instrument itself is not public.
- **[SECONDARY]** — the proposition rests on a law-firm alert, press report, commentary or search summary. Every such item is flagged at the point of use.
- **[CITATION UNCONFIRMED]** — a citation that could not be verified against the instrument and must not be relied on.
- **[VERIFY QUOTATION]** — the quotation was obtained through a text-extraction proxy rather than a direct reading, and must be re-verified before use in advice or filing. This applies to the whole of the special-category statutes chapter (§4.9) and is explained there.

### 1.5A How the negative findings in this bundle were derived

Verified negatives carry weight in this bundle, so the method matters. **Every keyword negative — "the word X appears zero times in instrument Y" — rests on a scripted count over locally extracted text, not on a shell search.**

⚠️ **This is not pedantry.** During the research a shell `grep` for "confidential" **crashed rather than returning a no-match result**, which would have produced a **false negative** on the very provision (Blood Banking Service Regulations reg 20(3)) that turns out to be the affirmative statutory obstacle in §4.15.4. A crash and a genuine absence look identical if the exit status is not checked. Every negative was re-derived with scripted counts.

### 1.6 Quotations

Quotations are kept short and are attributed to the instrument and provision. Where a longer passage matters, the entry gives the provision number and describes its effect rather than reproducing it.

### 1.7 How to navigate

- **§2** is the headline position: one page, read it first.
- **§3** is the **pipeline register** — everything not yet in force. It is placed before the in-force material deliberately.
- **§4** is the in-force material, by domain. Each domain opens with a summary table.
- **§5** is the data-access map for research and secondary use.
- **§6** names the operative instruments that are not public.
- **§7** states the gaps honestly.

# 2. THE HEADLINE POSITION

### 2.1 Singapore has a dedicated health information statute. It has no legal effect.

**The Health Information Act 2026 (Act 1 of 2026) is wholly uncommenced as at 15 August 2026. Not one of its 114 sections is in operation.** The Public Sector (Governance) (Amendment) Act 2026 (Act 2 of 2026) — the sibling instrument passed at the same sitting and containing the most expansive data-sharing power in the package — is also **wholly uncommenced**.

This is not an inference from a status banner. It rests on four independent proofs:

1. **The gazette record.** The complete Subsidiary Legislation Supplement for 2025 (S 1–898) and 2026 (S 1–564, to 14 August 2026) was retrieved, verified gap-free, and searched in full text. Across all 94 commencement notifications gazetted in the two years, **none is under either Act**. No regulations, no orders, no notifications, nothing. The phrase "health information" occurs **zero times** in the entire 2025–2026 subsidiary legislation corpus.
2. **A title-restricted search of the whole e-Gazette index** for "Health Information" returns exactly two documents in Singapore's entire gazette history: the Bill (Bills Supplement No. 20, 5 November 2025) and the Act (Acts Supplement No. 2, 12 February 2026).
3. **The commencement provision itself.** Health Information Act 2026, s 1: the Act "comes into operation on a date that the Minister appoints by notification in the Gazette". There is no default date and no backstop. Act 2 of 2026, s 1, is in identical form.
4. **MOH's own statement, one day before the retrieval date.** The Ministry's cyber and data security page, last updated **14 August 2026**, states that "the HIA requirements have yet to take effect" and that further communications will follow on when mandatory reporting begins (MOH, *About Cybersecurity and Data Security*, `healthinfo.gov.sg/csds/`).

The responsible Ministry's own affirmative statement, 24 hours old at the retrieval date, is the strongest available citation and should be used alongside the gazette proof.

**Target commencement is "early 2027"**, stated three times by the Senior Minister of State on the floor of the House at Second Reading (Vol 96, Sitting No 12, 15th Parliament First Session, Monday 12 January 2026). That is a stated intention, not a legal date. Mandatory contribution obligations then land in batches from **1 September 2027**.

### 2.2 What therefore actually governs health data sharing today

> **Reading the Singapore statute book will not tell you what governs health data sharing today.** The dedicated statute has no legal effect. The operative rules sit in codes that say on their face they have no legislative effect, in licence conditions changed by circular, in professional codes made enforceable only through disciplinary findings, and in contracts that are not published. Where the state wants data *from* you, the duty is in an Act with a criminal penalty. Where it authorises data to flow *between* institutions, the rule is somewhere else — and in several load-bearing cases, nowhere you can read.

The pattern is **statutory design, not regulatory drift**. Health Information Act 2026 **s 90** is a purpose-built codes-of-practice power. Its three subject-matter limbs — conduct (s 90(2)(a)), confidentiality/integrity/availability (s 90(2)(b)), and portability (s 90(2)(c)) — correspond to the three MOH documents already published. **s 90(6)**: a code "does not have legislative effect". **s 90(7)** makes compliance mandatory in terms, subject to a Ministerial waiver (s 90(8)). **s 90(9)(b)** makes breach admissible in any proceedings "to establish or negate any liability". The operational rulebook was drafted never to appear in the Subsidiary Legislation Supplement at all — searching for S-numbers will always return nothing, by construction.

**How a s 90 code is actually enforced, stated precisely.** Enforcement is **indirect and narrower than it first appears**: inspection and information-gathering under ss 91–95, and evidential admissibility under s 90(9)(b) — which cuts **both ways**, since a code may be relied on to *negate* liability as well as to establish it. The Act's only directions power triggered by breach of a code is **s 88**, and it is confined to the portability limb (s 90(2)(c)) and runs only against a health data intermediary — not against a contributor or user. There is no direction, no fine and no licence consequence for breaching the conduct or security limbs of a code as such.

**⭐ And there is a doctrinal mechanism behind all of this, which explains *why* the pattern exists rather than merely describing it.**

**Interpretation Act 1965 s 23(1)(a)** requires subsidiary legislation to be published in the Gazette. **s 2** defines subsidiary legislation as an instrument made under an Act **"and having legislative effect"**. Parliament has then, in **five** statutes bearing on health data, expressly placed the operative normative instrument **outside that definition**, so that the gazetting duty never bites:

| Provision | Effect |
|---|---|
| **HBRA s 40(3)** | a code of practice "does not have any legislative effect and need not be published in the Gazette" |
| **PDPA s 26(3)(b)** | transfer exemptions need not be gazetted |
| **HIA s 90(6)** | a code of practice "does not have legislative effect" |
| **PSGA s 9(2)** | a data sharing direction is removed from the gazetting duty |
| **MediShield Life Scheme Act 2015 / CareShield Life and Long-Term Care Act 2019** | the Part 5 and Part 8 ministerial certifications carry **no publication requirement of any kind** — by silence, against six express Gazette formulae elsewhere in the same Acts |

**This is not a construction imposed on the drafting. The drafter says so.** The Public Sector (Governance) Bill's own Explanatory Statement states that a direction "does not have legislative effect" and "**It is therefore not subsidiary legislation.**"

🔴 **The sharpest consequence, and the strongest single line available to the client: PSGA s 7(1) makes unauthorised disclosure a criminal offence carrying up to $5,000 or 2 years where the disclosure "is not authorised by any data sharing direction" — and s 9(2) means no statute requires anyone to publish that direction. Whether a public official's conduct is criminal turns on an instrument nobody outside the recipient agency can read.**

A Government Gazette full-text sweep confirms that **no MediShield Life or CareShield Life certification has ever been gazetted**, and a **462-hit full-text gazette search for "data sharing direction" returns no gazetted direction, ever** — only Acts using the phrase.

⭐ **A worked example of what that means in practice, and the single sharpest illustration in this bundle.** From 27 June 2026 blood donors receive reward points, which requires identified donor data to move from the blood service to the Health Promotion Board and onward to a private rewards operator. **The private operator's collection and use of that data is squarely authorised without consent by PDPA s 17(1) and Second Schedule, Part 1, paragraph 1. The disclosure to it has no located authorisation whatsoever**, because the power to share with a "non-public sector person" was created by the Public Sector (Governance) (Amendment) Act 2026 and **is not in force**. **So the statute book authorises the private company to receive the data while authorising nobody to send it** — and an unqualified, criminally enforced confidentiality duty sits over exactly those records. See §4.15.4.

**And no s 90 code can bind today in any event.** s 90(5) is a hard gate: nothing has force or effect until the s 90(4) publication notice is given. The Act is uncommenced, so s 90(4) cannot be exercised. No such notice exists anywhere in the gazette.

### 2.3 Six limits on that proposition — where it does not hold, and must not be pushed

The pattern is real but **domain-specific, not general**. Six genuine counter-examples:

**Limit 1 — Where the state compels data *in*, it legislates. Precisely, and with criminal sanction.** This should be stated as *part of* the position, not as an exception: Infectious Diseases Act ss 6, 25, 59A with prescribed 24-hour and 72-hour deadlines and a prescribed electronic system; National Registry of Diseases Act ss 6–7 (compelled notification) and ss 8–12 (confidentiality and gated disclosure) with eight sets of regulations, disease by disease; Misuse of Drugs Regulations reg 19 (compulsory 7-day report of suspected addicts to the Director of Medical Services and the Central Narcotics Bureau); Workplace Safety and Health Act s 27 with Incident Reporting Regulations reg 7(3) (10-day duty on the certifying doctor or dentist); Registration of Births and Deaths Act 2021 ss 7, 8, 22, 23 (compelled reporting of births, deaths, stillbirths and cause of death); Healthcare Services (Blood Banking Service) Regulations 2021 **reg 20(3)–(6), as inserted by S 388/2023 reg 12 with effect from 26 June 2023** (criminal confidentiality duty under reg 24, up to $20,000 / 12 months plus $1,000 a day continuing).

**Limit 2 — The PDPA layer is fully statutory, adjudicated and published.** 387 published PDPC decisions with reasons; financial penalties; a transfer obligation in gazetted regulations; health data prescribed as a deemed-significant-harm breach category by S 64/2021; a parallel public-sector offence regime in Part 9B. It produces genuinely normative rulings — *Institute of Mental Health* [2025] SGPDPC 1 and the SGH secure-AI-inferencing guidance of 7 July 2026 are operative norms delivered through a statutory mechanism.

**Limit 3 — CSA's codes bind; MOH's do not.** The **Cybersecurity Code of Practice for Critical Information Infrastructure 2026**, issued and in force 29 July 2026, is a code of practice under **Cybersecurity Act 2018 s 35A(1)(a)(i)** and is **binding** on CII owners, healthcare CII included, with compliance dates of 29 July 2027 and 31 December 2027. Contrast Health Information Act s 90(6). Two codes of practice, two statutes, opposite legal force. **The pattern is MOH's, not Singapore's.**

**Limit 4 — The Health Information Act is a dense statute and in one respect is *tougher* than the administrative layer.** 114 sections, four Schedules, offences up to **$1 million** for a body corporate (ss 68(5), 82(1)). And the decisive instance: MOH's administrative assurance that a licensee choosing a whitelisted clinic management system "will not be held accountable" for that system's failures **has no statutory basis** — the Act imposes the **opposite** duty. **ss 66(4)(b) and 68(2)(b)** require the contributor or user to *ensure* that its health data intermediary complies, with criminal liability attached. Here the statute is harsher than the guidance, and a client relying on the guidance is exposed. See §4.1.6.

**Limit 5 — The Act reserves proper legislative form for the parts that matter most.** **s 111** lets the Minister amend the four Schedules "by order in the Gazette" — gazetted subsidiary legislation, not circular. **s 110** exemptions are likewise by Gazette order. **s 90(4)–(5)** impose a publication gate on codes. And the most expansive sharing power in the whole package — the new **s 6(3)** inserted by the Public Sector (Governance) (Amendment) Act 2026, overriding common-law confidentiality subject only to privilege and contract — was put in **primary legislation**, debated and passed. The Government legislated when it wanted the strongest power.

**Limit 6 — The special-category statutes are statutory, with criminal sanction.** "Confidentiality lives in codes" is true of the seven **professional registration** Acts. It is not true of the **subject-matter** Acts: Infectious Diseases Act s 25, Termination of Pregnancy Act s 7, Voluntary Sterilisation Act s 5 and Human Organ Transplant Act s 28 are statutory confidentiality provisions with criminal penalties. (Caveat: the underlying research on these is proxy-derived — see §4.9.1.)

### 2.4 What must not be said

For the avoidance of overstatement, the following formulations are **not supported** by the evidence base and should not be used:

- ❌ "MOH is avoiding legislation" / "regulatory drift" / "a governance gap". The mechanism is **designed**; s 90 is a purpose-built channel with its own publication gate, waiver power and inconsistency rule.
- ❌ "Singapore governs health data by guidance." CSA's binding s 35A codes, the PDPA's adjudicated regime and the compelled-notification statutes refute it.
- ❌ "The codes are unenforceable." They are indirectly enforceable, and §2.2 states precisely how.
- ❌ "The Act is a shell." It is a dense statute that is unstarted, and in the vendor-liability respect it is *stricter* than the guidance it will replace.
- ❌ Any version implying the pattern uniformly disadvantages the regulated party. Sometimes it advantages them (a voluntary AIHGle 2.0; a non-regulatory GL-10-R1); sometimes the guidance promises protection the statute withholds (the whitelisting assurance). **The consistent feature is not severity — it is that the operative norm is not in a form that can be relied on, challenged, or found by reading the statute book.**
- ❌ "The clusters fall between two regimes." They do not. They are governed as ordinary organisations under the PDPA, plus the HCSA and the Cybersecurity Act. See §4.4.4.
- ⭐ ❌ **"This inter-agency data flow is unauthorised" / "there is no legal basis for it", said on the strength of gazette silence.** This is the single most tempting overstatement in the bundle and it must not be made. **PSGA s 4 — the provision under which data sharing directions are given — carries no publication requirement at all**; the word "Gazette" does not appear in it, and s 44's laying duty reaches only subsidiary legislation. **The absence of a gazette hit for any inter-agency flow is therefore structurally guaranteed, and proves nothing about authorisation.** Always write **"no *published* legal basis"** or **"no *located* authorisation"**. The permissible sharp claims are different and narrower, and §4.15.4 models all three: that **no published basis exists**; that a direction **could not cure a *statutory* confidentiality duty** in any event, because s 6(1) lifts only common-law confidentiality, s 6(2) preserves privilege and contract, and s 11(1) forbids a direction inconsistent with any other written law; and that a direction **cannot reach a private-sector recipient at all**, because s 6(1) is confined to sharing with another Singapore public sector agency.

**And a time limit.** The position in §2.2 is total *as at 15 August 2026* because the health-data-specific statutory layer is inert. **That is time-bounded and changes on commencement.**

### 2.5 The one thing every confidentiality provision in this bundle is subject to

**Criminal Procedure Code 2010 s 20(5)** permits a police officer or authorised person to exercise the s 20 production power "despite any provision in any other law relating to the production of, or the giving of any access to, any document or thing, or data" (CPC s 20(5)). It is current: the Criminal Procedure (Miscellaneous Amendments) Act 2024 (Act 5 of 2024) amended s 20(2), inserted s 20(2A) and replaced s 20(9), but **did not touch s 20(5)**.

**Every statutory health-confidentiality provision catalogued in this bundle is subject to it** — Infectious Diseases Act s 25 (HIV and sexually transmitted disease), Termination of Pregnancy Act s 7, Human Organ Transplant Act s 28, blood-donor confidentiality under the Healthcare Services (Blood Banking Service) Regulations 2021 reg 20(3)–(6) (as inserted by S 388/2023), National Registry of Diseases Act s 8, HCSA s 51(2), and the professional-code duties enforced through Medical Registration Act s 59D(1) and its analogues. **None of them contains a clause preserving itself against the CPC.** Those provisions must never be presented as absolute, and this bundle flags the point at each of them.

⭐ **A concrete comparison worth putting in front of the client.** CPC s 20(2) already required an order for a **financial institution's** customer information to be made by an officer of or above the rank of **inspector**; the 2024 Act inserted **s 20(2A)** imposing a tighter rank-and-authorisation gate still for a new category of "specified institution". **There is no equivalent elevated threshold anywhere in s 20 for medical records.** A patient's clinical record is reachable on a **sergeant's** written order. Parliament revisited that asymmetry in 2024 and made it larger.

There is a second universal override: **Cybersecurity Act 2018 s 23(3)(b)**, which saves legal professional privilege but **expressly defeats professional-conduct and contractual confidentiality**. See §4.21.

### 2.6 The reviewability point

MOH confirmed in a Parliamentary Question answered **6 August 2026** that it maintains **no public register of regulatory actions against HCSA licensees**. Because the operative norms sit in the licence-condition and circular layer, and because roughly four out of five numbered MOH circulars are never published (§4.2.6), that layer cannot be checked against enforcement practice at all. This bounds what any roundup of this kind can honestly claim, and it is stated here rather than buried.

---

# 3. ⭐ PIPELINE REGISTER — everything not yet in force, as at 15 August 2026

This is the section the client weighted most heavily. It is placed first among the substantive material.

The register is built from an independent re-verification performed on 15 August 2026 in which every status line rests on a document retrieved and parsed in that session — gazette PDFs, Bill PDFs, Order Paper PDFs, MOH circular PDFs and agency pages. It is ordered by proximity to legal effect.

**Corpus completeness, verified twice independently:** Subsidiary Legislation Supplement 2026 = **564 unique S-numbers, S 1/2026 to S 564/2026, no gaps, no duplicates**, latest gazetted 14 August 2026. Acts Supplement 2026 = 19, latest 15 June 2026. Bills Supplement 2026 = 18, latest 4 August 2026. Nothing was gazetted on 15 August 2026.

**Parliament:** the last sittings before the retrieval date were **4 and 5 August 2026** (Order Papers Nos 33 and 34). No Order Paper exists for any later date, so **no sitting occurred between 6 and 15 August 2026**, and the date of the next sitting is not publicly determinable from Parliament's own site (`/parliamentary-business/sitting-dates` returns 404).

## 3A. ENACTED BUT NOT COMMENCED

| # | Instrument | Citation | Stage | Date of that stage | What remains outstanding | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Health Information Act 2026** | **Act 1 of 2026** (Acts Supplement No. 2 of 2026) | **Passed, assented, gazetted — WHOLLY UNCOMMENCED** | Passed 12 Jan 2026; assented 3 Feb 2026; gazetted **12 Feb 2026, 5 pm** | A commencement notification under s 1. None exists. No subsidiary legislation of any kind. No s 90(4) code notice. **No System Operator designated under s 8(1).** No approved contributor (s 13(2)) or approved user (s 20(2)) notification. Minister's stated target: **early 2027** | Acts Supp 2/2026 PDF; complete SLS 2026 sweep; `healthinfo.gov.sg/csds/` (14 Aug 2026) — **P** |
| 2 | **Public Sector (Governance) (Amendment) Act 2026** | **Act 2 of 2026** (Acts Supplement No. 3 of 2026) | **Passed, assented, gazetted — WHOLLY UNCOMMENCED** | Passed 12 Jan 2026; assented 3 Feb 2026; gazetted **16 Feb 2026, 12 noon** | A commencement notification under s 1. None exists | Acts Supp 3/2026 PDF; complete SLS 2026 sweep — **P** |

Not read a second time on 5 August either — Order Paper No. 34 does not list it | Parliament Bills Introduced listing; Bill PDF (66 pp); Order Papers Nos 33 and 34 — **P** |
| 21 | **Scams (Countermeasures) and Other Matters Bill** | **Bill No. 16/2026**; Bills Supplement No. 16 of 2026 | **First reading done; not passed** | First reading 4 August 2026 | Second reading onwards. ### 3B.1 What Bill 17/2026 actually does — read in full (66 pages)

**Long title:** an Act to amend the Health Sciences Authority Act 2001, the Biological Agents and Toxins Act 2005, the Human Biomedical Research Act 2015 and the Healthcare Services Act 2020, and to make related and miscellaneous amendments to certain other Acts.

**The substantive content is the transfer of HBRA and BATA administration to the Health Sciences Authority, not the HIA amendment.** Six data-relevant points:

**(a) Administration of the HBRA moves from the Director-General of Health to the Chief Executive of HSA** (Part 3, cll 23–32). **Data-relevant:** clause 32 expressly lists **HBRA s 28(2)(e)** (re-identification in the public interest), **s 29(1)(e)** (disclosure of a research subject's individually-identifiable information in the public interest), **s 39(1)(e)** (the same for tissue donors), and **s 40(1)–(2)** (codes of practice, including the code for protecting identity). If enacted and commenced, **the public-interest override on health-data confidentiality under the HBRA moves from the Director-General of Health to the Chief Executive of HSA.**

**(b) A new First Schedule to the HSA Act (clause 13) lists ELEVEN Acts** that HSA will assist in administering or enforcing: CareShield Life and Long-Term Care Act 2019; Geneva Conventions Act 1973; Healthcare Services Act 2020; Human Cloning and Other Prohibited Practices Act 2004; Human Organ Transplant Act 1987; Infectious Diseases Act 1976; Medical and Elderly Care Endowment Schemes Act 2000; MediShield Life Scheme Act 2015; National Registry of Diseases Act 2007; Termination of Pregnancy Act 1974; Voluntary Sterilisation Act 1974. **Eleven, not nine.** A press report of "nine other Acts" is contradicted by the Bill's own First Schedule and should not be used. **Clause 11 inserts a new HSA Act s 40A permitting the Minister to amend that First Schedule by order in the Gazette** — the list is expandable by subsidiary instrument without further primary legislation.

**(c) "Auxiliary authorised officers" — private individuals appointed as inspectors, expressly barred from medical records.** New HBRA s 45A(2)(a) and new HCSA s 41A(2)(a) permit them to inspect and copy documents and records, "but not any book, document, record or electronic material that is a medical record" (Bill 17/2026, new HBRA s 45A(2)(a)). The bar is carried into the electronic-data powers by the parenthesis "(but not any data that is a medical record)" in new HBRA s 45A(3)(b)–(c) and new HCSA s 41A(3)(b)–(c), so it cannot be circumvented by copying data rather than documents.

**(d) 🚩 The Explanatory Statement contradicts the operative clauses — verified in full.** The Explanatory Statement says auxiliary authorised officers enter "with consent" and that "refusal to give consent is not an offence". The word "consent" occurs **seven times** in Bill 17/2026 — once in the enacting formula, once in new HBRA s 45(2)(b), once in new HBRA s 59(5A), and four times in the two Explanatory Statement passages — and it appears **nowhere in new HBRA s 45A or new HCSA s 41A**. New HBRA s 45A(1) simply permits entry and inspection during normal business hours.

Refusal of access **does** appear to be an offence on the face of the Bill, and the two penalties are different — **do not state a single combined figure**:

| Route | Provision | Penalty |
|---|---|---|
| HBRA | **new s 45B(1)(a)** — refusing access to, or obstructing, an authorised officer **or auxiliary authorised officer** | **fine up to $20,000 or imprisonment up to 2 years or both** |
| HCSA | **s 42(1)**, extended to auxiliary authorised officers by **clause 40** | **fine up to $20,000 or imprisonment up to 12 months or both** |

New HBRA s 45A(5) and new HCSA s 41A(4) additionally impose a positive duty on any individual present to render all necessary assistance and cooperation. On the text as introduced, the real constraints are the **"normal business hours"** limit and the **absence of a search power** — **not** a consent requirement. An Explanatory Statement is an aid to construction and not operative; where it and the enacting words diverge, the enacting words govern. **This is exactly the kind of point that attracts a committee-stage amendment and should be re-checked against the Bill as passed.**

**(e) The electronic-data powers, and where the password compulsion actually sits.** ⚠️ **The clause map matters and is easy to get wrong:**

- **Clause 26** replaces HBRA s 45 and inserts new ss 45A and 45B. **New HBRA s 45 is the fullest version of the power** and is where the **password-compulsion limb sits — new s 45(10)(d)**.
- **Clause 38** amends the HCSA to give operate-the-system and bulk-export powers — **but it contains no password power**.
- **Clause 36** inserts **new HCSA s 36A**, the auxiliary officer's information notice (see below).
- **Clauses 39 and 40** insert new HCSA s 41A and extend s 42.

New HBRA s 45(2)(b) preserves for *authorised* officers (not auxiliary ones) the power to inspect and copy any medical record of a person treated or examined at the premises, "even though the prior consent of such person has not been obtained" (Bill 17/2026, new HBRA s 45(2)(b)). Auxiliary authorised officers get no equivalent paragraph.

**(g) ⭐ The medical-record carve-out is genuinely well drafted — and new HCSA s 36A defeats it.** Both halves should be stated, because the drafting credit makes the point stronger rather than weaker.

The carve-out at new HCSA s 41A(2)(a) and (3)(b)–(c) (and its HBRA twin) is **not** a token: by carrying the exclusion into the electronic-data limbs with the parenthesis "(but not any data that is a medical record)", it closes the obvious copy-the-data-instead loophole. Somebody thought about it.

**But clause 36's new HCSA s 36A lets the same auxiliary authorised officer require, by written notice, "all documents and information" the Director-General considers necessary — with no medical-record carve-out at all.** So the officer who may not copy a medical record on the premises may demand it by notice. The safeguard is defeated on the Bill's own terms, and this should be raised at second reading alongside the consent point.

Note also that **"medical record" is undefined** in the Bill and in both parent Acts, yet the entire safeguard rests on that term.

**(h) Onward use.** New HBRA s 45(12), new HBRA s 45A(7) and new HCSA s 36A(5) each provide that the officer or the Director-General "is entitled without payment to keep" the information, copies and extracts. **None of the three imposes any restriction on onward use.** See §4.21.

**(f) The HIA amendment is housekeeping.** Clause 47 amends Health Information Act 2026 ss 91(1)(a)(iii), 92(1)(a)(iii) and 92(2)(c). The Bill's own Explanatory Statement says clause 47 amends those sections "to clarify the scope of the provisions and to correct a typographical error". The typographical error is that s 92 governs *investigation* officers throughout but subsection (2)(c) as enacted says "authorised officer". **This should not be presented to the client as a significant substantive development.** The genuinely noteworthy point survives and is narrower: clause 47 amends an Act that has never been in force, and because both instruments commence by Ministerial notification, the *sequencing* of the two notifications determines whether the Health Information Act ever operates in its uncorrected form.

Bill 17/2026 also touches, for officer-appointment and personal-liability purposes only, the National Registry of Diseases Act 2007, the MediShield Life Scheme Act 2015, the CareShield Life and Long-Term Care Act 2019, the seven healthcare-profession registration Acts, and the **Termination of Pregnancy Act 1974 and Voluntary Sterilisation Act 1974**. ⚠️ Those last two amendments permit officers of a public authority to be appointed so that HSA officers can be appointed. **They have nothing to do with the medical-confidentiality provisions in those Acts** and must not be conflated with them. Clause 48 separately inserts a new s 66A into the Health Products Act 2007 giving personal-liability protection to the HSA Chief Executive and officers acting in good faith and with reasonable care.

### 3C.1 The Genetic Information Bill — the most genuinely mid-passage item in the bundle

**Origin, traced to primary source.** Announced by Minister Ong Ye Kung at the launch of National Precision Medicine Programme Phase III, **14 November 2025**: MOH is "working on legislation to enshrine the proper use of genetic information", intends "to provide legislative backing to the current moratorium on insurance", and intends "to expand the coverage to include employment". Foreshadowed again on 19 January 2026 at the opening of the SingHealth Genomic Assessment Centre, and in a written Parliamentary answer of 24 February 2026. The earliest located ministerial announcement is the Singapore Health & Biomedical Congress keynote of 10 October 2024. The policy lineage runs back to the Bioethics Advisory Committee's *Genetic Testing and Genetic Research* report of November 2005, Recommendation 7.

**What the consultation paper proposes** (MOH, *Proposed Bill on Protecting Genetic Information — Public Consultation Policy Document*, August 2026, 9 pp, retrieved in full):

- **Who is restricted:** all **MAS-licensed insurers** (not merely members of the Life Insurance Association), and **employers** "[a]cross all employment stages and decisions, including recruitment, hiring, promotion, performance evaluation, termination, dismissal or retrenchment" (para 10b).
- **What is protected:** all genetic information and genetic test results "regardless of the reason for the genetic test", covering clinical, non-clinical and **research** purposes (para 9a(ii)), **including the genetic information of the individual's biological relatives** (para 9).
- **What remains disclosable:** current medical conditions and diagnoses; and family history of medical conditions, "as per current practice" (para 9b(ii)). Note the tension: relatives' *genetic information* would be protected but relatives' *medical history* would not. The paper's own worked examples make the diagnosis point explicit — an individual who is diagnosed must disclose the diagnosis but not the genetic test result.
- **Retrospective and anti-avoidance:** obligations apply even where the insurer or employer obtained the information before commencement (para 13c) and even where it "was voluntarily disclosed by the individual for his advantage" (para 13b) — i.e. the Bill **removes** the favourable-voluntary-disclosure route the moratorium preserves.
- **The single exception:** a financial limit for insurance, to be set "on a per-policy basis" (para 17) — narrower than the moratorium's aggregation across all insurers in Singapore. **MOH is working with MAS and the industry to set it and it has not been set.** ⚠️ A worked example in the paper uses **$1 million**, but expressly as an assumption. **Do not report $1 million as a proposal.**
- **Flexibility clauses:** power to include additional sectors in future (para 11) and to accommodate additional exceptions (para 18).
- **🚩 Enforcement, offences, penalties and the regulator are NOT ADDRESSED anywhere in the published paper.** This is a real gap in what is known, not an omission from this bundle.
- **"Singapore Residents" is used in the paper without definition**, which materially affects coverage of foreign workers and non-residents.
- The paper does **not** discuss interaction with the PDPA.
- **Insurance-side scope is underwriting only** (para 10a limits the insurance obligations to "insurance underwriting activities and decisions"). Claims assessment appears to fall outside, matching the moratorium's post-acceptance carve-out. The paper does not spell this out — **treat it as an open question, not a finding.**

**Effect on the existing instrument.** The moratorium is drafted to be extinguished by the Bill automatically, without any repealing instrument: 2025 Moratorium para 11 provides that it is open-ended "unless it is replaced by any legislation on the use of genetic test information in insurance underwriting at its effective date". No transitional instrument is needed on the moratorium side. See §4.13.

**Two forward-dated programme items announced in the same release:** the National Familial Hypercholesterolaemia Genetic Testing Programme (launched June 2025, already running) and **subsidised testing for Hereditary Breast and Ovarian Cancer from December 2026** (register item 47).

### 3C.2 The Digital Infrastructure Bill — route it correctly

The draft Bill was retrieved in full and reports a clean negative: it contains **no personal-data or health-data provisions at all**. Its reach is purely infrastructural — licensing of data centres and IaaS/PaaS cloud services above thresholds, with security, resilience and incident-notification duties — and government and public-authority data centres are carved out of licensing (draft s 3(3)). It belongs in an adjacent bundle with that finding stated, rather than being presented as governing health data. Its relevance here is convergence: see §3D.1. Note the drafting detail that the draft carries "Bill No. /2026" and "DIGITAL INFRASTRUCTURE ACT 2026 (No. of 2026)" with the numbers left blank — the same blank-number convention that produces the Acts Supplement citation trap in §1.4(a).

---

### 3D.1 A convergence worth a paragraph of its own

**Cloud and data-centre services are now the subject of three separate not-fully-operative regimes**: (i) the uncommenced Cybersecurity Act Third Schedule and Part 3D (register items 4–5), (ii) the un-introduced Digital Infrastructure Bill (item 22), and (iii) the un-issued CCoP (Cloud) (item 29).

Part 3D is **doubly inoperative**: the Part is uncommenced, and the Third Schedule that supplies the content of its central defining term is also uncommenced. Because the s 3 definition of "foundational digital infrastructure service" is drafted as requiring specification in the Third Schedule, and the Third Schedule does not yet exist, the regime has no content even conceptually. This is the sharpest way to put it to the client.

There is a further live gap inside the *commenced* part of that Act. **s 4 of Act 19 of 2024 is in force** and inserted new ss 3(2E)–(2H) into the Cybersecurity Act — the application provisions telling you to whom Parts 3C and 3D apply. **Those application provisions are in force; the Parts they apply are not.** And because s 20 (the civil-penalty regime) is uncommenced even though it is drafted to cover "any provision in Part 3, 3A, 3B, 3C or 3D", **Parts 3A and 3B, which are in force, have no civil-penalty alternative to prosecution available.**

One cloud provision *is* already in force, in a different instrument: **CCoP for CII 2026 §3.9** requires notification to the Commissioner of any plan to host CII on cloud regardless of deployment or service model, a prior risk assessment, and the appointment by the cloud service provider of a person in Singapore to accept service of legal process. The standalone CCoP (Cloud) will be additional to it.

### 3F.1 The point to make about the batch dates

Every date in items 56–59 is **administrative**. They are announced deadlines under an Act that has not commenced. The National Contribution Grant window for GPs opened on **1 July 2026** and public money is already moving against a statute with no legal effect.

More strikingly: **none of MOH's three principal HIA implementation documents states when the Act commences, or that it is not yet in force.** Machine-searched in full:

| Document | Characters extracted | occurrences of "commence" | occurrences of "in force" |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOH Circular MOH-MHC-0018-2026 (6 Mar 2026) | 16,758 | **0** | **0** |
| Cybersecurity and Data Security Essentials, first edition (Mar 2026) | 32,127 | **0** | **0** |
| HIA Implementation Guide v2.0 (Aug 2026) | 122,017 | **0** | **0** |

(Extraction verified sound: 142 hits for "HIA" and 290 for "NEHR" in the Implementation Guide.) MOH is running a funded compliance programme — grants open, deadlines set, security controls mandated — against a statute that has never commenced, and the documents driving that programme are silent on the point. Only the website says it.

**🚩 And MOH's citizen-facing page reads the other way.** `healthinfo.gov.sg/hia/`, updated 12 August 2026, opens by saying the Act was "Enacted on 3 February 2026" — the assent date — and describes in the present tense what it "enables", with no statement anywhere on the page that no provision is in force. A reader would reasonably conclude the Act is operative. This is worth showing the client as the reason the commencement point has to be stated explicitly in every advice.

### 3F.2 The watch list — the earliest visible signs that the Act is starting

Because commencement will be signalled in the Gazette, the following are the specific instruments to monitor. All are by order in the Gazette or by gazetted notification, and **none exists today**:

1. A **commencement notification** under Health Information Act s 1.
2. **Designation of a System Operator** by order under s 8(1).
3. **Approved contributor** notifications under s 13(2) and **approved user** notifications under s 20(2) — these function as individualised licences and are the principal instruments by which the access perimeter will expand.
4. **s 30(3) regulations** prescribing types of accessible health information that access restrictions cannot touch — this defines the irreducible core the opt-out cannot reach, and its size is currently unknown and entirely in the Minister's gift.
5. **s 110 exemption orders** and **s 111 Schedule-amendment orders**.
6. **s 98(4) regulations** prescribing "prescribed written laws" — the set is currently empty.
7. The **s 90(4) notice** publishing a code of practice, which is the s 90(5) gate.
8. **s 77(2)–(4) regulations** defining what counts as a notifiable data breach, including the deemed-significant-harm categories and the prescribed number of affected individuals.
9. **s 58(1) regulations** requiring data sharing agreements under Part 3 to be notified — absent such regulations, **DSAs need not be filed with anyone.**

⚠️ Watch the Government Gazette "Notices under other Acts" category as well as the Subsidiary Legislation Supplement. The Cybersecurity (Symbols) Notification 2026 (Notice No. 520, 26 January 2026) shows that notifications can land outside the SL Supplement.

**Verified negative, stated for citation:** a machine scan of the full text of all 564 subsidiary-legislation instruments gazetted in 2026 for commencement dates falling after 15 August 2026 found **no health-data, health-services, biomedical-research, data-protection or health-cybersecurity instrument gazetted with a future commencement date**. The forward-dated statutory pipeline in this domain is entirely at the level of *Acts awaiting a commencement notification*, not of gazetted-but-pending subsidiary legislation.

Everything in this domain is either **uncommenced** (the Act) or **non-statutory** (everything else). Nothing in it binds as law today. What binds today is contract, licence condition and programme requirement.
