### 4.1.1 Health Information Act 2026 — the Act itself

**Exact title:** Health Information Act 2026 | **Type:** Act of Parliament | **Issuing body:** Parliament of Singapore | **Citation:** **Act 1 of 2026**; Government Gazette Acts Supplement No. 2 of 2026 | **Date:** passed 12 January 2026; assented 3 February 2026 (President Tharman Shanmugaratnam); first published in the Gazette 12 February 2026 at 5 pm | **Status as at 15 August 2026: WHOLLY UNCOMMENCED** | **Source:** `https://assets.egazette.gov.sg/2026/Legislative%20Supplements/Acts%20Supplement/02.pdf` (157 pp) — **P**

**What it does for health data sharing.** It creates a national electronic records system and a compulsory duty on named classes of healthcare provider to contribute health information to it; a closed-list access regime under which only specified users may access that information for specified purposes; a mechanism by which individuals may restrict access; a second, separate channel for bilateral sharing of individually identifiable information outside the national system under data sharing agreements; a data-security and cybersecurity regime with incident and breach notification duties; and a portability regime. It systematically displaces PDPA consent as the basis for any of this.

**Structure:** 114 sections, **nine Parts** and **four Schedules** — First Schedule (s 12) contributors and data types; Second Schedule (ss 18(1)(a)(i), 19(1)(c)) users and authorised individuals; Third Schedule (s 19(3)) written laws under which "specified examinations" are carried out; **Fourth Schedule (ss 47, 51(1)(a), 51(2)(c)) — the non-NEHR data-sharing matrix**. ⚠️ Some sources report three Schedules. It is four, verified by grep of the enacted text. **The Fourth Schedule is the discloser/recipient/use-case table that scopes Part 3, and a reader told there are only three Schedules will not go looking for it.**

**Terminology caution:** the Act never uses "NEHR" or "National Electronic Health Record". Its statutory object is the **"national electronic records system"** (s 7(1)). Cite the statutory term.

**Long title** (verbatim, and the only place the HOTA amendment is signalled): an Act to provide for a national electronic records system for the contribution, collection, storage and disclosure of health information, to facilitate collection, disclosure and use of health and other information to maintain and improve health and wellbeing, "and to make a related amendment to the Human Organ Transplant Act 1987".

**Key provisions.**

*Definitions.* **s 3(1)** — "health information" means administrative and clinical information, "but does not include any administrative information or clinical information that may be prescribed" (note the negative delegation: the Minister can carve categories *out*). **s 3(2)** administrative information; **s 3(3)** clinical information; **s 3(4)** — **"accessible health information"** is health information made available on the national system by a System Operator. This is the pivotal concept: most of Part 2's prohibitions and offences bite on the copy in the national system, not on a provider's own records. **s 5** applies the Act to the Government. **s 7(1)** establishes the system; **s 8(1)** provides for designation of a System Operator by order in the Gazette.

*Contribution.* **s 12** and the First Schedule — 13 categories of specified contributor and the types of health information each must contribute. **s 13** approved contributors, by Gazette notification. **s 14** contributors' obligations, including prescribed accuracy and completeness requirements (s 14(2)(b)) and prescribed timing (s 14(2)(c) — **the Act imposes no deadline of its own**). **s 15** directions to enforce the duty, offence at s 15(4).

*Access.* **s 16** the gateway rule; **s 19** and the Second Schedule — 17 categories of specified user; **s 18** authorised individuals; **s 20** approved users by Gazette notification; **s 21** onward disclosure; **s 22** users' obligations; **s 23** directions.

*Derived information — the secondary-use channel.* **s 24** defines **type 1 derived information** as derived information in individually identifiable form and **type 2** as aggregated or anonymised. **s 25(2)**: the Minister may approve an application for **type 1** derived information "if the application is made for a purpose that relates to or promotes public health". This matters enormously for the opt-out — see below.

*Access restrictions (the opt-out) — Division 5 is **ss 29–33**, not ss 29–31.* s 29 (two classes); s 30 (effect); s 31 (imposition and revocation); **s 32 (access restrictions relating to health information about deceased individuals)**; **s 33 (transitional — an existing NEHR opt-out notified to the Government before s 31 commences is deemed to become a class 1 or class 2 restriction on commencement)**.

**The opt-out is materially narrower than it appears.** It is **mandatory on request** — s 31(7) requires the System Operator to impose or revoke on request, with no discretion and no grounds of refusal. But:

- it does **not** stop contribution of your data to the national record (**s 30(7)**);
- it does **not** survive emergencies (**s 30(4)** — access is permitted where necessary to respond to an emergency threatening life or health, "whether or not P consents or is able … to consent");
- it does **not** exclude you from derived-information and secondary use (**s 30(5)(a)**) — and because the s 25(2) route yields **type 1** derived information, **an individual who has imposed the maximal class 1 restriction can still have individually identifiable information about them supplied to an approved requestor**;
- it does **not** restrict access "permitted or required under any other Act or by an order of court" (**s 30(5)(b)**);
- it does **not** reach any type of accessible health information **prescribed** under **s 30(3)** — nothing is prescribed today, so the size of this hole is currently unknown and entirely in the Minister's gift.

All three carve-outs are complete defences to the improper-access offence: **s 38(3)(b)**.

*Consent is displaced, not relied on.* **s 11(1)**: "Despite any other written law or rule of law, or any obligation under contract", consent is not required for contribution, access, collection, disclosure or use under Part 2. **s 11(2)**: nothing in Part 2 allows access on the basis that the individual consents. **s 16(2)**, **s 30(6)**, **s 38(7)**, **s 39(3)**, **s 40(7)** and **s 50** all repeat the formula that PDPA consent neither makes access "required or permitted under any written law" nor operates as a defence. **Anyone reasoning from PDPA consent principles about this statute will be wrong.**

*Excluded purposes — insurers and employers.* ⚠️ The words "insurer" and "employer" appear **zero times** in the Act. A keyword search will produce a false negative. The operative concepts are **s 6 "excluded purpose"** (employment, insurance and platform-work purposes), **s 19(3)** and the **Third Schedule** whitelist of statutory medical examinations. Access or collection for an excluded purpose is the most heavily punished offence in the Act: **s 38(5)** — $100,000 / 4 years, rising to $200,000 / 7 years on repeat.

*Part 3 — the second sharing channel, easy to miss.* ss 45–60. **s 48(1)**: Part 3 does not apply to accessible health information — it is a parallel channel, not an extension of Part 2. **s 46(1)** "relevant information" extends to individually identifiable information relating to **a caregiver** as well as to the patient. **s 50** displaces consent and confidentiality: "Despite any other written law or rule of law or any obligation of confidentiality imposed by contract", consent is not required. **s 52** requires a written data sharing agreement specifying parties, use case, information types and authorised personnel, with a **minimisation rule on the face of the agreement** at s 52(4). **ss 53–57** impose discloser, recipient, collection, use and no-onward-disclosure duties. **s 58** provides that DSAs need be notified only if regulations so require — **no such regulations exist, so DSAs need not be filed with anyone.** Offences at s 60: $50,000 / 2 years, doubling on repeat.

**The Fourth Schedule as enacted is narrow — four use cases, all serving Healthier SG and Age Well SG**, with the Agency for Integrated Care Pte Ltd or one of the three cluster headquarters (National Healthcare Group Pte Ltd, National University Health System Pte Ltd, Singapore Health Services Pte Ltd) as a party in every case. **But it is expandable by Ministerial order under s 111 without returning to Parliament**, and "relevant information" for any new use case is defined by regulations under s 46(1)(b).

*Security, breach and portability.* Part 4 (ss 61–71) data security, retention and disposal (s 67), cybersecurity (s 68), policies (s 69), incident management framework (s 70), remedial directions (s 71). Part 5 (ss 72–82) assessment and notification of cybersecurity incidents and data breaches. Part 6 (ss 83–85) Ministerial emergency powers by certificate. Part 7 (ss 86–88) portability duties on health data intermediaries.

*Enforcement.* There is **no new regulator** — the Act is administered by the Minister and MOH, with authorised officers and investigation officers appointed under Part 8. Administrative action against contributors and users at ss 34–37; inspection and search at ss 91–95; corporate and personal liability at ss 105–106; composition at s 109.

*Penalties — the top of the range.* **s 82(1)** (failure to assess or notify a cybersecurity incident or data breach): individual $200,000 / 2 years; otherwise **$1,000,000**. **s 68(5)** (cybersecurity contraventions, including the duty to ensure a vendor complies): individual $200,000 / 2 years; body corporate **$1,000,000**. **s 70(4)** (no incident management framework): $100,000 / 12 months. **s 38(5)** (excluded purpose): $100,000 / 4 years, repeat $200,000 / 7 years. **s 39(2)** and **s 40(4)** (access or disclosure by a person who is not a user): $100,000 / 4 years. **s 112(3)**: regulations may create offences punishable with $50,000 / 12 months plus $1,000 a day.

**Significant negative findings in the Act, verified by full-text search.**

- **No right of correction or rectification.** Accuracy is framed as a duty on contributors (s 14(2)(b)), not a right of the individual.
- **No complaint mechanism.** The word "complaint" does not appear in the Act.
- **No individual right of appeal.** The s 36 appeal is available only to a contributor, user or authorised individual of a user. A data subject has no appeal route.
- **No statutory private right of action and no compensation provision.** The opposite: Part 9 (ss 98–104) confers broad statutory immunities.
- **"Sensitive" appears 0 times.** "Sensitive health information" is **not a defined term** — verified twice, by two independent retrieval routes. It is policy vocabulary from commentary. **Never attach it to a section number.**
- **"Research" and "biomedical" appear 0 times.** There is no research carve-out and no HBRA carve-out. See §4.5.
- **"School" appears 0 times; "veterin" appears 0 times.** "Prison" appears 49 times; "dental" 14; "optometr" 7; "deceased" 8. ("Pharmac" appears repeatedly — two independent extractions counted 46 and 53; retail pharmacy is a live contributor class in Batch 3.)
- **"Communicable Diseases Agency" appears 0 times.** The CDA is not named anywhere in the Act. It may still be caught as an HCSA clinical-laboratory licensee (it is one, by S 118/2026) through the First Schedule contributor classes, but the Act does not name it. ⭐ **And it is caught — see §4.10.8.** First Schedule Part 1 item 4 makes an HCSA clinical-laboratory licensee a specified contributor of "Laboratory test reports", and clinical laboratories are in **Batch 1, by 1 September 2027**. **So the National Public Health Laboratory is on the face of the Act a compulsory NEHR contributor**, without ever being named.
- 🔴 **"genom", "pathogen" and "microb" each appear 0 times** in the whole enacted text. **So the Act neither includes nor excludes genomic results**, and **"laboratory test report" is itself undefined** — First Schedule Part 2 defines ten other terms and not that one (the whole of Part 2 was read to confirm it). ⭐ **Whether a genome sequence forms part of a "laboratory test report" is the single most consequential unresolved question in the pathogen-genomics domain** (§4.10.8, §4.10A.4), and it is resolvable by regulation under **s 3(1)** or by amendment of the First Schedule under **s 111**.

**📌 The one statute the Act does expressly touch — and it is not the one usually named.** The long title's "related amendment" is delivered by **s 113**, which inserts into **Human Organ Transplant Act s 28(2)** a new paragraph: "(ba) as required or permitted under the Health Information Act 2026". That is the Act's **only** amendment to another statute, it is a **direct amendment adding a permitted-disclosure gateway**, and it is **uncommenced** along with the rest of the Act — so HOTA s 28(2) has no paragraph (ba) today.

⚠️ **Do not call s 113 a "consequential amendment"** — the Act calls it a *related* amendment and it sits in the body of the Act, not in a schedule.

**⚠️ Three different mechanisms, three different statuses. Merging any two produces a false statement:**

| Mechanism | What it is | Status |
|---|---|---|
| **HIA s 98(4)** | a *prescription power* over confidentiality obligations imposed by "prescribed written law" | enacted, uncommenced, **and the prescription has never been made — the set is empty** |
| **HIA s 113** | an *enacted amendment* to HOTA s 28(2) adding gateway (ba) | enacted, **uncommenced** |
| **Bill 17/2026, new HSA Act First Schedule** | a *list of eleven Acts* on which HSA may assist MOH — including the IDA, TPA, VSA, HOTA and NRDA | **not law at all** until the Bill passes |

So the four special-category confidentiality provisions fall into **three groups**, not one: **HOTA s 28** (expressly amended by HIA s 113); **IDA s 25** (the Infectious Diseases Act appears twice in the HIA Third Schedule for s 19(3) specified-examination purposes, but is not amended); and **TPA s 7 and VSA s 5** (absent from the Act entirely, and dependent on a s 98(4) prescription that has not been made).

**🚩 And a gap in the Act that is worth raising while it can still be fixed.** The Act confers the widest inbound access powers in Singapore health law — health information without the individual's consent (ss 93(2)(d), 94(2)(d)), **compelled disclosure of passwords** (s 92(2)(d)), warrantless entry (s 94), bulk export (ss 93(3), 94(3)), and retention of copies "without payment" (ss 91(2), 92(3)) — and pairs them with **no restriction on the regulator's onward use at all.** There is no equivalent of HCSA s 51: no secrecy duty on authorised or investigation officers, no gateway list, and **"not compellable" appears zero times in the Act.** Every other health statute in the field — the HCSA, the repealed PHMCA, the NRDA, the PDPA, the Cybersecurity Act, the Health Products Act — has *some* secrecy provision binding its own officers. The dedicated health information statute has none. The one exception is **s 84(9)**, which does restrict onward use, but only of emergency-measure information, and even then permits disclosure to any investigation officer, police officer or law enforcement authority of information disclosing an offence under any written law; breach carries $50,000 / 2 years (s 84(10)). **Because the Act is uncommenced and commencement is targeted for early 2027, this is still fixable — which is the point worth making.** See §4.21.

**The immunities — ss 98–104, and exactly where the confidentiality override sits.**

All of ss 98–103 share one architecture: a gateway subsection (1) applying the protection "if, and only if" the actor acts in good faith and with reasonable care, in accordance with the Act, and (for authorised individuals and personnel) in the ordinary course of duties. Then:

| s | Protected class | Confidentiality override | Carve-out? |
|---|---|---|---|
| **98** | contributors | **(3)** | **YES — s 98(4), "prescribed written law"**; copyright at (5) |
| **99** | users | **(3)** | **NO**; (4) is copyright |
| **100** | requestors | **(3)** | **NO**; (4) is copyright |
| **101** | System Operator, officers, employees, contractors | **none** | n/a — (2), (3), (4) are all no-liability limbs; copyright at (5) |
| **102** | disclosers under a Part 3 DSA | **(3)** | **NO**; (4) is copyright |
| **103** | recipients under a Part 3 DSA | **(3)** | **NO**; (4) is copyright |
| **104** | authorised and investigation officers | **none** | s 104 is a single unnumbered sentence conferring personal immunity |

📌 **The correct statement for use in advice:** the confidentiality override appears in **ss 98(3), 99(3), 100(3), 102(3) and 103(3)**; **only s 98(3) is qualified by a carve-out** (s 98(4), "prescribed written law"); and **ss 50, 51 and 54 override confidentiality for Part 3 sharing with no carve-out mechanism at all.** Two widely circulated versions of this point — "ss 99–104" and "ss 98–103" — are both wrong at one end or the other.

📌 **s 98(4) does not name any statute.** A claim in circulation that s 98(4) preserves Infectious Diseases Act s 25, Termination of Pregnancy Act s 7 and Voluntary Sterilisation Act s 5 is **refuted**: "Termination of Pregnancy Act", "pregnancy", "Voluntary Sterilisation Act", "steril" and "section 25" each appear **zero times** in the enacted text. The mechanism is real but the list is empty, and it will remain empty until regulations prescribe written laws.

**Structural consequence.** s 98(4) is a narrow lever confined to contributors' contributions. **Once information is in the system, the confidentiality overrides protecting users, requestors, disclosers and recipients operate with no reserved statutory-confidentiality carve-out at all.**

📌 **Single day or phased commencement — now settled.** **s 1 appoints a single date on its face** and contains **no** "different dates may be appointed for different provisions" formula. But the Act's own drafting contemplates provision-by-provision commencement — s 33 refers to "the date of commencement of section 31"; s 44(1) to s 13; s 44(2) to s 20; s 44(3) to ss 19 and 20; and **s 114** runs from "the date of commencement of any provision of this Act". MOH's published timeline plainly assumes phasing. **The reconciliation is Interpretation Act 1965 s 21(2)**, which supplies the general power to appoint different days for different provisions. So the Minister may commence the Act provision by provision even though s 1 speaks of a single appointed date. **The correct formulation is: the Act appoints a day on its face; the phasing power comes from the Interpretation Act, not from the Act's own commencement clause; and MOH's published timeline assumes phasing.**

### 4.1.2 The codes-of-practice architecture — s 90

**Verified verbatim against the gazetted text.** s 90 sits in Part 8 and is headed "Codes of practice".

- **s 90(1)** — three powers: **issue** a code; **approve as a code a document prepared by a person other than the Minister**, if considered suitable; or amend or revoke. ⚠️ The approval limb matters: it lets MOH convert existing industry or agency material into a code without drafting it.
- **s 90(2)** — three subject-matter limbs: (a) conduct of contributors, users, disclosers, recipients, relevant health data intermediaries and health data intermediaries; (b) measures to ensure the **confidentiality, integrity and availability** of health information and relevant information; (c) **portability** of health information in electronic form processed by a relevant HDI.
- **s 90(3)** — a code provision inconsistent with the Act has effect subject to the Act, or not at all.
- **s 90(4)–(5)** — publication machinery, and the hard gate: nothing has force or effect until the s 90(4) notice is published.
- **s 90(6)** — a code "does not have legislative effect".
- **s 90(7)** — subject to s 90(8), "a person must comply with any code of practice that is applicable to that person".
- **s 90(8)** — Ministerial waiver, generally or for a specified period. 🔴 **An asymmetry inside a single statute:** s 90(7) makes compliance with a code mandatory, and **s 90(8) lets the Minister waive it "to any person" with no publicity requirement at all** — while **s 110** requires an exemption *from the Act* to be "by order in the Gazette". Exemption from the Act is gazetted; waiver from the mandatory code is invisible.
- **s 90(9)** — breach "does not of itself render the person liable to criminal proceedings" but "may be relied on by any party" in any proceedings, criminal or otherwise, as tending to **establish or negate** any liability.

**The claim that the three s 90(2) limbs map one-to-one onto the three MOH documents already published is a characterisation, not a verified statutory fact.** The limbs exist as stated; the mapping should be attributed as an inference.

**An open interpretive question, flagged and not resolved.** Because s 90(7) is itself a provision of the Act, a code breach is arguably a contravention of s 90(7). But **s 34** — the only power to suspend a contributor's or user's access — is confined by s 34(1)(a) to contraventions "under this Part" (Part 2) and by s 34(1)(b) to contraventions that are offences. A s 90(7) breach is neither. The residual hook would be s 34(1)(d), "the public interest so requires". This is an open question of construction, not a finding.

### 4.1.3 HIA Implementation Guide for Healthcare Providers, Version 2.0 — the real rulebook

**Type:** implementation guide, non-statutory | **Issuing body:** MOH | **Citation:** none | **Date:** August 2026; PDF internal creation timestamp `D:20260814122335+08'00'` — **authored 14 August 2026 at 12:23 SGT**, the day before the retrieval date; 74 pages | **Status:** current; supersedes v1.0 (April 2026, 18 pp) | **Source:** `healthinfo.gov.sg` (Isomer-hosted PDF) — **P**

**What it does.** It is the operative detail of the whole HIA programme: batch timelines and the National Contribution Grant schedule (Table 4), contribution requirements, access rules, the cyber and data security measures, the incident-reporting table (Annex E, Table 12), model contract clauses for cloud vendors, and Annexes A–F.

**The v1.0 → v2.0 delta matters and is documented:**
1. delivers the promised Annexes A–F;
2. **changes Batch 1 from "Private Hospital" to "Acute and Community Hospital"** — a scope change with direct consequences for private hospital groups;
3. **reverses** National Contribution Grant eligibility for Early Contribution Incentive Grant recipients;
4. **deletes the line "Administrative staff generally should not have access rights."**

That last deletion is worth the client's attention on its own terms: **a guidance document changed an access-control norm with no instrument and no notice.**

### 4.1.4 Cybersecurity and Data Security Essentials

**Type:** standards document; on MOH's description, the security rulebook for all HIA-regulated entities; in form, a prospective s 90 code | **Issuing body:** MOH, in consultation with CSA, IMDA and PDPC | **Citation:** none; face reads "Date of Publication: 03-2026 (First edition)"; 16 pages | **Date:** March 2026 | **Status: published; NOT legally binding, because the Act is uncommenced and no s 90(4) notice exists** | **Source:** `healthinfo.gov.sg/csds/` → `isomer-user-content.by.gov.sg` — **P**

**What it does.** 13 measures in 3 categories; the operative security control set MOH expects of HCSA licensees, NEHR contributors and users, and prospective HIA entities. It also carries a **2-hour initial MOH notification** obligation running concurrently with the CSA and PDPC channels, and a 14-day detailed report.

**⚠️ How to characterise its legal force — the contested point, settled.** One source in the research described it as a "mandatory standards document issued by MOH under the HIA framework". **That characterisation should not be used.** The document's own drafting register is advisory: across 16 pages it uses **"should" 95 times, "must" twice and "shall" once**. Its predecessor (December 2023) used "shall" 90 times. MOH's own website says "All HIA-regulated entities **must** comply with the security controls set out in the Cybersecurity and Data Security Essentials". **The website's characterisation and the document's own drafting do not match**, and three of the four source agents agree the drafting governs.

**The accurate formulation:** contractually binding today, through the HIA-Compliant certification framework and programme requirements (§4.16); not legally binding until the Act commences and, probably, until it is adopted as a s 90 code — at which point s 90(6) says it *still* has no legislative effect.

**A date inconsistency to footnote rather than resolve.** Its face reads "03-2026 (First edition)"; a second reading of it reports the document states "Revised in January 2026"; and MOH's Forum Reply of 25 February 2026 promised it would be revised "by the end of February 2026". **Cite it as "First edition, published March 2026" and footnote the internal inconsistency.**

### 4.1.5 The regulations the Act expects but that do not exist

The word "prescrib-" appears **99 times** in the Act. The following are places where the statute expressly requires something to be prescribed, MOH has already published a number or rule in guidance, and **no instrument prescribes it**:

| What the Act requires prescribed | Provision | What guidance already asserts | Instrument? |
|---|---|---|---|
| Period for notifying a **cybersecurity incident** | s 75(1)–(2) | **"within 2 hours of confirmation"** (IG v2.0 Table 12); initial notification within 2 hours, detailed report within 14 days (`/csds/`) | **None** |
| Period for notifying a **data breach** | s 79(1) | as above | **None** |
| Health information **deemed to cause significant harm** | s 77(2)(a)–(b) | FAQ v1.1 gives sexually-transmitted-infection data as an example, then states the details "will be set out in subsidiary legislation to be issued" | **None** |
| Number of affected individuals constituting **significant scale** | s 77(3)(a) | **"500 or more affected individuals"** (IG v2.0 Table 12; FAQ v1.1 Q13) | **None** |
| Types of **class 2 access restriction** | ss 29(1)(b), 29(2) | select-your-provider restriction; a fixed always-visible basic dataset; doctor break-glass — all described as arriving "from 2027" | **None** |
| Form, manner and **standards** of contribution | s 112(2)(b)–(c) | NEHR Contribution Requirements (27 Jul 2026); six data-quality standards in IG v2.0 Ch 2B | **None** |
| Form and manner of approved contributor / user applications | s 112(2)(a), (d) | Synapxe participation form plus a mandatory 30-minute onboarding session | **None** |
| Notification of **data sharing agreements** | s 58(1) | nothing published | **None** |
| Types of information immune from access restriction | s 30(3) | nothing published | **None** |

MOH twice concedes the gap in primary text: FAQ v1.1 (13 August 2026), p 6 n 1, says the breach thresholds "will be set out in subsidiary legislation to be issued"; and IG v2.0 Table 12, in place of a reporting link, says the link "will be added here in 2027, when the incident reporting framework is launched".

**Announced but unpublished instruments in this domain:** an **Alternate Contribution Channel** for pen-and-paper clinics licensed before 2027 (FAQ v1.1 Q22 — "More details will be provided in due course"; no document exists); the **MOH incident reporting form and framework** (due 2027); an updated Third Schedule form list (the 3 July 2026 restatement says MOH "will update this list"); and the **complete technical specifications for contribution** — the NEHR Contribution Requirements deck says the full specification is not published and directs providers to "check with your HIMS vendors", so **the authoritative data dictionary is held by vendors, not by MOH publicly.**

### 4.1.6 🚩 THE WHITELISTING SAFE HARBOUR DOES NOT EXIST IN LAW — and the Act does the opposite

This is the most client-actionable finding in the bundle.

**What MOH said.** MOH Circular No. 13/2025 (21 March 2025), repeated in the Feedback Report (Table 1, item 5): if licensees have exercised due diligence in choosing a whitelisted clinic-management or EMR system, "they will not be held accountable for lapses or cybersecurity non-compliance arising solely from these CMS/EMR solutions".

**What the Act says.** Searched in full (239,689 characters, ss 1–114, four Schedules):

| Search term | Occurrences in Act 1 of 2026 |
|---|---|
| "whitelist" / "white-list" / "white list" | **0** |
| "held accountable" | **0** |
| "accountab" (any form) | **0** |
| "safe harbour" | **0** |
| "due diligence" | **0** |
| "indemnif" (any form) | **0** |

**There is no whitelisting safe harbour in the Health Information Act 2026.** Nothing in the Act keys any liability consequence to the selection or use of an approved or listed health information management system.

**The Act goes the other way.** **s 66(4)(b)**: where health information is processed by a relevant health data intermediary of a contributor or user, the contributor or user "must ensure that A complies with all the requirements under paragraph (a)". **s 68(2)(b)** is in materially identical terms for cybersecurity safeguards. **s 68(5)** makes contravention of s 68(2) — which includes the licensee's own duty under (2)(b) — an offence carrying up to **$1 million** for a body corporate. **s 69(5)** adds a duty to implement processes ensuring the intermediary and its personnel adhere to the licensee's policies. **s 71(1)** exposes the licensee to Ministerial directions for contravening s 66(4)(b), s 68(2)(b) or s 69(5), and **s 71(3)** makes non-compliance with such a direction an offence.

**So on the enacted text, a licensee whose whitelisted system fails to implement reasonable safeguards is itself in breach of s 68(2)(b) and exposed to a fine of up to $1 million — the precise scenario the assurance says will not be held against it.**

**Do the enacted immunities help? No.** ss 98–104 are a genuine cluster of statutory immunities, but s 98 protects against liability *for the act of contributing* — it says nothing about data-security or cybersecurity non-compliance, which is governed by Parts 4 and 5 with its own offences. And the s 98(1) gateway requires that the contribution be "in accordance with this Act": a licensee whose intermediary has failed its s 66(4)(a) / s 68(2)(a) duties is, by force of s 66(4)(b) / s 68(2)(b), *not* in compliance with the Act, so the protection would fall away in exactly the scenario in question.

**The only statutory routes to the promised protection, and neither has been taken:**
1. a **s 110 exemption order** — an exhaustive title-restricted search of the whole e-Gazette index returns no such order and no subsidiary legislation of any kind under the Act; or
2. a **s 90 code of practice** containing a vendor-selection provision, which would then be admissible under **s 90(9)(b) to negate liability**. **This is the single most useful thing MOH could do, and it has not done it.**

**Two mitigating points, in fairness.** First, **none of this is in force** — the Act is wholly uncommenced. Second, MOH's own 6 March 2026 circular already **softened** the wording from "will not be held accountable" to a formulation about the vendor-selection exercise being "considered in determining if there will be liability". The softened version is consistent with the Act, because both a code of practice and a documented vendor-selection process are admissible under s 90(9)(b) as tending to negate liability. The original wording is not.

**Recommended action for a licensee relying on the assurance:** seek (a) a s 110 exemption order, (b) a s 90 code provision expressly addressing vendor-attributable failures, or (c) contractual indemnity from the vendor. The Act contains no indemnity provision of its own.

### 4.1.7 The guidance layer was not consulted on

Guidance in this domain was consulted on exactly **once** — a Draft NEHR Guidelines bundled into the December 2023 Bill consultation (item 23 above). Everything published since — the CS/DS Essentials, the final NEHR Guidelines, the Code of Practice for Data Portability v2.0, both Implementation Guides, both FAQ versions and the Contribution Requirements — was **published without consultation**. That is not an omission: **a s 90 code requires no consultation at all**, so this is what the design contemplates. The Feedback Report on the Bill consultation itself was published on **5 November 2025**, the day of First Reading and 21 months after the consultation closed.

---

## 4.2 HEALTHCARE SERVICES LICENSING AND THE CIRCULAR LAYER

**Terminology correction.** Under the Healthcare Services Act 2020 the normative instrument on a licence is a **"Licence Condition" ("LC")**, imposed under **s 13(1)** (or, for mode-of-service-delivery approvals, s 11B(3)) and modified under **s 14**. "Licensing Terms and Conditions (LTCs)" was the **Private Hospitals and Medical Clinics Act era** term and MOH's archive still carries PHMCA-era items under it. Use "Licence Conditions".

### 4.2.1 Healthcare Services Act 2020 — the statute

**Citation:** No. 3 of 2020 | **Status:** in force in phases; **s 28 has never been commenced** — six and a half years — and there has been **no amendment Act and no amendment regulations in 2025 or 2026**; the Act has been static since 18 December 2023 | **Source:** SSO `/Act/HSA2020` — **P**

**The three HCSA commencement notifications — all three verified against the gazetted instruments.** These were at one stage quarantined in the research as the corpus's highest fabrication risk, because they had come through a search-engine summary and appeared to be contradicted by adjacent numbers. **All three were subsequently downloaded and read. They are correct.**

| S-number | Full title — cite exactly | First published | Made | What it commenced |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **S 1030/2021** | Healthcare Services Act 2020 (Commencement) Notification 2021 | 30 December 2021 at 7 pm | 29 Dec 2021, Chan Yeng Kit, Permanent Secretary, MOH | The Act "except sections 26, 28, 32, 33, 34, 35, 58 and 59(2) and (8)", **wef 3 January 2022** |
| **S 381/2023** | Healthcare Services Act 2020 (Commencement) Notification 2023 | 20 June 2023 at 5 pm | 11 Jun 2023 | s 26, **Part 4** and s 59(2), **wef 26 June 2023** |
| **S 799/2023** | Healthcare Services Act 2020 (Commencement) **(No. 2)** Notification 2023 | 11 December 2023 at 5 pm | 8 Dec 2023 | s 58, **wef 18 December 2023** |

All three carry the file reference `[MH 78:44/1; AG/LEGIS/LEG/B/2015/20 Vol. 17]`.

⚠️ **Two citation points.** First, **S 799/2023 is a "(No. 2)" Notification** — a citation omitting "(No. 2)" is wrong. Second, the gazette **enumerates ss 32, 33, 34 and 35 individually**; it does not say "Part 4". The two are the same thing — **HCSA Part 4 is headed "Step-in arrangements for designated licensees" and comprises ss 32–35** — but quote the gazette's own enumeration rather than the paraphrase.

📌 **The apparent conflict that caused the quarantine was never a conflict.** The near-miss numbers `S 1/2022`, `S 387/2023` and `S 800/2023` are also real instruments — they are **Amendment of Schedules Orders**, a parallel series gazetted within a day or two of the commencement notifications. Both sets of numbers are correct; they are different instruments doing different jobs on nearly the same dates. The next reader will see the near-miss numbers and assume one set is a typo, so this is stated expressly.

🔴 **NEW FINDING — two HCSA provisions have never commenced.** A title-restricted sweep of the complete e-Gazette index for "Healthcare Services Act 2020 (Commencement) Notification", filtered to the Subsidiary Legislation Supplement, returns **exactly three hits across all years — the three above and nothing else.** Reading them together:

| Provision | Excepted by S 1030/2021 | Later commenced? |
|---|---|---|
| s 26 | yes | ✅ S 381/2023, wef 26 Jun 2023 |
| **s 28** ("Approval of employment of individuals by certain licensees") | yes | ❌ **never** |
| ss 32–35 (Part 4, step-in arrangements) | yes | ✅ S 381/2023, wef 26 Jun 2023 |
| s 58 | yes | ✅ S 799/2023, wef 18 Dec 2023 |
| s 59(2) | yes | ✅ S 381/2023, wef 26 Jun 2023 |
| **s 59(8)** | yes | ❌ **never** |

⚠️ **Cite this as "no commencement notification for HCSA ss 28 or 59(8) appears in the e-Gazette", not as an absolute negative** — the sweep was title-restricted, and a commencement effected by an instrument with a different title would not be caught.

📌 **s 59(8) is a different case, and a widely circulated statement about it is wrong.** It was excepted from commencement in 2021 and **then deleted outright** by the **Statutes (Miscellaneous Amendments) Act 2022 (Act 31 of 2022), s 24(1), with effect from 1 November 2022** (S 850/2022). **So HCSA s 59(8) never came into force and has not existed since 1 November 2022.**

⚠️ **Do not repeat the statement that "HCSA s 59(8) remains in force today because PDPA s 44 has never commenced."** The reasoning was that s 44 of the PDPA (Amendment) Act 2020 would have deleted s 59(8), was never commenced, and therefore s 59(8) survived. The premise is true and the conclusion does not follow: the very same omnibus provision — **Act 31 of 2022 s 24** — deleted **both** HCSA s 59(8) (s 24(1)) **and** ss 42, 44 and 45 of the PDPA (Amendment) Act 2020 (s 24(3)). Parliament tidied away the dormant amending provision and its target in one stroke. **The question "what does HCSA s 59(8) say" is moot.**

**s 28, by contrast, remains genuinely uncommenced** and its text is live on the statute book.

**Key data provisions:** **s 13(1)** (licence conditions) and **s 14** (modification of licence conditions, by the two-step in §4.2.3); **s 16** (duty to provide information); **s 17**; **s 27(1)** (monitoring and evaluation records to be kept "for the prescribed period" — ⚠️ **no period is prescribed in the General Regulations**, a live gap); **s 28** (employment-screening records, uncommenced); **s 36**; **s 37**; **s 38** (codes of practice); **s 51** (information sharing); **s 57** (regulation-making); **s 59(8)** (⚠️ **never commenced, and deleted with effect from 1 November 2022** by the Statutes (Miscellaneous Amendments) Act 2022 (Act 31 of 2022) s 24(1), S 850/2022 — the statement that it "remains in force because PDPA s 44 has never commenced" is wrong and is corrected above in this same section; see also §3A item 10).

**Records duties in the Regulations.** The General Regulations 2021 are the primary records instrument: **regs 25, 37, 38, 39 and 45**. Each service-specific set repeats a "keeping of other records" pattern — CLS/RS Regulations regs 42–45 and 44A (traceability); Outpatient Medical reg 33; Acute Hospital reg 44; Nursing Home reg 39.

⚠️ **Two nouns run in parallel across this Act and its Regulations, and only one of them is defined.** The **Regulations** speak of the "**patient health record**", which **reg 2 of S 1035/2021 defines** — but expressly only for those Regulations. The **Act** speaks of the "**medical record**" (**s 41(2)(b)** inspection, **s 51(2)** confidentiality), which **is defined nowhere in Singapore legislation at all**, including the Interpretation Act. **s 51(6) defines "medical information" — the contents — while leaving the container undefined.** This is a 1980 PHMCA term that survived the 2020–2023 modernisation at Act level, and **Bill 17/2026 builds its central safeguard on it**. Worked through in full at **§4.21.6**, which also corrects the citation trap around S 387/2023, S 414/2023 and reg 2 versus reg 37.

### 4.2.2 Retention periods — the consolidated answer

Drawn from the Licence Conditions on the Retention Periods of Patient Health Records (v1.0, 19 July 2022), which is the operative instrument:

| Record type | Retention period |
|---|---|
| Computerised / electronic patient health records | **Lifetime + 6 years** ("Lifetime" = actual lifetime, or 110 years if the date of death is unknown) |
| Paper inpatient records — adults | **15 years** from the last day of stay or of consultation or treatment, whichever is later |
| Paper inpatient records — minors | until age **24**, or 15 years, whichever is later |
| Paper inpatient records — persons lacking mental capacity | **Lifetime + 6 years** |
| Paper outpatient records | **6 years** from the last day of consultation or treatment |
| Assisted reproduction records where a child was born | **Child's lifetime + 6 years** |
| Culling | Paper records may be culled after **4 years**, but 15 listed categories must be kept for the remainder of the period (para 8) |
| Digitised paper records | treated as electronic → **Lifetime + 6 years** (para 9) |
| Litigation hold | until conclusion of proceedings or the standard period, whichever is later; **High Risk cases minimum 15 years** (paras 13–16) |
| Employment-screening records | during employment **+ 2 years** after cessation — **HCSA s 28(4), UNCOMMENCED** |
| Monitoring and evaluation records | "for the prescribed period" — **HCSA s 27(1); no period prescribed** |

⚠️ **These duties are in tension with general-law duties and with the PDPA disposal duty, and nobody has reconciled them.** Companies Act 1967 s 199(2) requires 5 years for accounting records; Income Tax Act 1947 s 67(1)(a) requires 5 years from the year of assessment; Employment Act 1968 s 95 with the Employment (Employment Records, Key Employment Terms and Pay Slips) Regulations 2016 (S 148/2016) reg 5 requires 2 years for entries and 1 year after employment ends. PDPA **s 25** (Retention Limitation Obligation — **in force**) requires cessation of retention when the purpose is served and retention is no longer necessary for legal or business purposes. A separate **"National Guidelines for Retention Periods"** existed under MOH Cir 84/2022; that circular is tagged Rescinded but its Annex A substance survives verbatim in the Licence Conditions.

### 4.2.3 How the operative rules on genetic testing are actually changed — the observable two-step

This is the completed, documented form of the mechanism, retrieved end to end:

**s 14(2) proposal circular → 14-day representation window → s 14(4) written direction circular → revised Annex A on each individual licence.**

Worked example, both circulars retrieved: MOH-MHC-0051-2026 (29 June 2026) proposed adding eight conditions to the allowable PGT-M/SR list; **MOH-MHC-060-2026 (22 July 2026)**, signed by the Deputy Director-General of Health (Health Regulation), directed licensees to give effect to the modification "immediately". The additions were PGT-M for BROVCA3 (RAD51C), Charcot-Marie-Tooth Disease type 2A (MFN2), SETD2-related neurodevelopmental disorder and Tuberous sclerosis 1 (TSC1); and PGT-SR for four named chromosomal rearrangements. **The cycle ran 23 days end to end. No gazette, no regulation, no consultation paper.** A comparable earlier cycle (MOH Cir 65/2025 → 69/2025) ran 21 days. The cadence is stable.

### 4.2.4 Disclosure of patient records to insurers — use the qualified version

**MOH Circular No. 02/2026 (9 January 2026)**, addressed to all HCSA licensees and all registered medical and dental practitioners, is directly on point. It requires **documented consent**; bars disclosure of information irrelevant to the request "regardless of the scope of patient consent provided"; addresses insurer access to raw records; and **prohibits access to the NEHR for insurance purposes absolutely** (para 7 describes accessing NEHR to prepare medical reports, memos or clinical summaries for any insurance purpose as an offence "under the Health Information Bill" — note the circular refers to it as a *Bill*, which it was at the date of the circular and which is another illustration of the commencement point). Confirmed by a written Parliamentary answer of 3 February 2026.

⚠️ **The strong reading of this circular is too absolute and should not be used.** An **MOH FAQ dated 5 June 2026** materially softens it: it **permits release of raw records (laboratory reports, imaging, operative notes) with irrelevant details redacted**; it says only that "[f]ree and unfettered access to all patient medical records in healthcare providers' systems including EMRs should not be permitted" — **not** that contractual audit rights are void; and it creates a **reciprocal duty on clinicians not to decline valid requests**.

**The accurate position as at 15 August 2026:** no unfettered access; redacted raw records are permissible; clinicians may not refuse valid requests; and NEHR access for insurance purposes is prohibited.

**The circular remains a striking instance of the bundle's central thread**, because it regulates disclosure "regardless of the scope of patient consent provided" — an administrative instrument operating on the scope of the patient's own consent. Note also the companion instrument in §6: an **MOH/MAS guidance note to insurers** was issued and has never been published.

### 4.2.5 Mandatory data-submission regimes operating administratively today

These are live obligations on licensees, imposed by licence condition or circular rather than by regulation:

- **Serious Reportable Events** → MOH via NQAS (Licence Conditions v2.0, 6 February 2024).
- **Laboratory-Developed Tests** → annual notification to MOH via HALP (Clinical Laboratory Services Licence Conditions v1.0).
- **HALP submission streams** — termination of pregnancy, drug practices, voluntary sterilisation, laboratory-developed tests (FAQ on Data Submission, 18 November 2022).
- **Nursing home abuse** → 72-hour notification and 30-day report via FormSG (MOH Cir 91/2023).
- **Laboratory isolates and samples** → National Public Health Laboratory (MOH Cir 13/2023).
- **Six-monthly medical examination results for female non-domestic work permit holders** → MOM portal (MOH Cir 13/Sep/2023).
- **Minimum Clinical Data Report** for the Diabetes Risk Profiling and Diabetic Foot Screening programmes (MOH Adv 04/2023).

### 4.2.6 🚩 The circular layer is where the rules live, and most of it is not public

**There is no public archive of MOH circulars.** There is no circular index on `moh.gov.sg` at all. What exists is a curated subset republished across four unconnected microsites. The largest was reconstructed in full: **115 unique circular-layer documents (2018–2026)** across 16 per-service collections on the HCSA portal.

**Roughly four out of five numbered MOH circulars in any recent year cannot be read by anyone outside the distribution list:**

| Year | Unique numbered documents on the portal | Highest circular number seen | Implied share published |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2019 | 2 | 56 | 3.6% |
| 2020 | 4 | 125 | 3.2% |
| 2021 | 12 | 193 | 6.2% |
| 2022 | 13 | 127 | 10.2% |
| **2023** | **24** | **95** | **25.3%** |
| **2024** | **17** | **91** | **18.7%** |
| **2025** | **20** | **79** | **25.3%** |
| **2026** (to 22 Jul) | **11** | **60** | **18.3%** |

**Read this honestly.** The circular sequence is a **whole-of-MOH** sequence, not an HCSA-licensing one — it also carries public-health, financing, manpower and public-institution circulars that have no reason to appear on a licensing portal. So the denominator is not "HCSA circulars", and the percentages are **not** "MOH suppresses 80% of its licensing circulars".

**What the table does establish, and it is the finding that matters:** between roughly 75% and 82% of the numbered instruments MOH issues in the circular series each year cannot be read by anyone outside the distribution list. **There is no public register naming them, no title index, and no numbered gap list.** For any given circular number in 2026 there is no way for a member of the public, a patient, a researcher or a lawyer to discover that the circular exists, let alone what it says. Of the 60 circular numbers issued in 2026 to 22 July, **11 are public**. Some of the remainder will be irrelevant to health data. Some will not be: MOH Cir 13/2025 — which sets out MOH's position on licensee liability for cyber breaches and the whitelisting assurance — would have been invisible had `healthinfo.gov.sg` not separately republished it.

**Availability degrades further inside the published subset.** Of the 115 documents, 67 link directly to a PDF, 46 link to a landing page, and **2 point at an internal resource token whose landing page returns HTTP 404**. One of those two is **MOH-MHC-0001-2026, the NRIC authentication guidelines** — a live, current data-protection instrument to which the portal has effectively broken the link.

**Numbering.** Two 2026 prefixes are in circulation — `MOH Cir nn/2026` and `MOH-MHC-00nn-2026` — and they are almost certainly **one running sequence, not two series**: MOH Cir 56/2026 (13 July) sits numerically between MOH-MHC-0051-2026 (29 June) and MOH-MHC-060-2026 (22 July), and MOH's own written direction refers to circular 0051 as "MOH Circular 51-2026". The `MOH-MHC-` prefix appears from 2 February 2026; the old style is still used alongside it. MOH also issues **unnumbered** circulars (the 4 November 2025 circular) and **date-numbered** ones (MOH Cir 13/Sep/2023).

**Status tagging.** The portal tags each entry Active (79) or Rescinded (36). That is the only public statement of currency for these instruments, and it is not always right at the document level.

### 4.2.7 The documented downgrade — the best evidence that the pattern is chosen

**MOH Circular No. 234/2020** stated that the 2018 genetic-testing Code of Practice would become **"CGGS Regulations"**. **MOH Circular No. 11/2025 (14 March 2025)** states instead that the Code "will be translated into either regulatory requirements prescribed as Licence Conditions (LCs) or guidelines" (MOH Cir 11/2025, para 3). That is a **recorded, dated decision to move an instrument down a class** — from gazetted subsidiary legislation to a licence condition or guideline — followed by no movement at all for 17 months, on top of an indefinite postponement of the whole regime since 26 March 2024. It is the strongest single piece of evidence that the pattern in §2.2 is chosen rather than accidental.

---

### 4.3.1 The status of the PDPA amendments — corrected

📌 **PDPA s 25 (the Retention Limitation Obligation) IS IN FORCE.** A widely repeated statement that s 25 of Act 40 of 2020 is uncommenced is **wrong**. S 60/2021 para 2 commenced Act 40 of 2020 "except sections 14, 24, 25, 39, 42, 44 and 45" on 1 February 2021 — **but para 3 of the same instrument commenced s 25 on 1 February 2021 at 12.01 a.m.** s 25 was carved out of para 2 only so that it could be given a distinct commencement *time*.

**The correct uncommenced set as at 15 August 2026 is ss 14 and 39 of Act 40 of 2020** — the other three excepted provisions have been deleted outright, not left pending. Only one further commencement notification has ever been made: S 767/2022, which brought s 24 into force on 1 October 2022.

⚠️ **A trap sitting on top of this.** "s 25 of Act 40 of 2020" (an amending provision) and "PDPA s 25" (the Retention Limitation Obligation in the principal Act) are different things. **Always write the citation out in full.**

**What is uncommenced, and why it matters:**

| Provision of Act 40 of 2020 | Effect |
|---|---|
| **s 14** | Inserts **Part VIB — DATA PORTABILITY, ss 26F–26J** |
| **s 39** | Inserts the **Twelfth Schedule — "Exceptions to Data Portability"** |
| ~~s 42~~ | **DELETED by Act 31 of 2022 s 24(3)(a) wef 1 Nov 2022** |
| ~~s 44~~ | **DELETED by Act 31 of 2022 s 24(3)(b) wef 1 Nov 2022** (it would have deleted HCSA s 59(8) — which the same omnibus provision deleted directly) |
| ~~s 45~~ | **DELETED by Act 31 of 2022 s 24(3)(c) wef 1 Nov 2022** |

**Consequence for health data: there is no statutory data portability right in Singapore, and there has not been one for five years and eight months.** Anyone advising on a patient-initiated transfer right under the PDPA should know there isn't one. What exists instead is the **Code of Practice for Data Portability v2.0** (§4.16), which operates by contract and certification.

⚠️ **Citation note.** The 2020 Act inserts "**Part VIB**" in Roman numerals; the 2020 Revised Edition renumbering renders it "**Part 6B**". Both are correct for their respective versions — say which you are citing. A drafting artefact confirms the position: **PDPA s 4 still forward-references Part 6B**, and the First Schedule cross-reference line still names the Twelfth Schedule, though neither exists in the consolidated text (the Parts run 6 → 6A → 7 and the Schedules run First to Eleventh).

📌 **A correction to a widely repeated statement about the health-specific limb.** It has been said that HCSA s 59(8) "remains in force today" because PDPA s 44 never commenced. **That is wrong.** **Act 31 of 2022 s 24** deleted **both** HCSA s 59(8) (s 24(1)) **and** ss 42, 44 and 45 of Act 40 of 2020 (s 24(3)), with effect from **1 November 2022**. So the consequential-amendment limbs of the portability package no longer exist, and neither does their target. **The uncommenced set of Act 40 of 2020 is ss 14 and 39 only**, and the whole of the remaining dormancy is the portability right itself. See §3A item 10 and §4.2.1.

### 4.3.2 Health data under the breach-notification regime — the nuance that matters

Health data **is** a prescribed data-breach category: **Personal Data Protection (Notification of Data Breaches) Regulations 2021 (S 64/2021), Schedule Part 1, items 17(d)–22**, prescribing categories deemed likely to result in significant harm. **But it is prescribed only for the listed conditions, not for health data at large.** That nuance is frequently lost and matters directly to whether a given incident is notifiable.

The scale threshold is **500 or more affected individuals**.

### 4.3.3 The public-agency carve-out and its consequences

**PDPA s 4(1)(c)**: Parts 3 to 6, 6A and 6B impose no obligation on a **public agency**. That is the carve-out that makes public-sector data governance a separate track (§4.4). **Part 9B (ss 48C–48F)** supplies the parallel public-sector offence regime, and it applies to **individuals**.

**S 90/2015 confirms rather than contradicts the position that the healthcare clusters sit outside the public-agency carve-out.** It is made under the definition of "prescribed healthcare body" in **PDPA s 2(1)** — **not s 2(2)**, which is the only public-agency designation power and is confined to statutory bodies. It calls the listed bodies "the organisations specified in the Schedule". It unlocks **PDPA Second Schedule, Part 3, Division 1, para 3(c)** — disclosure of patient data **to a public agency** for policy formulation or review, without consent. **Only a non-public-agency would need it.** See §4.4.4.

### 4.3.4 🔥 PDPC practical guidance to Singapore General Hospital on secure AI inferencing — 7 July 2026

**Type:** practical guidance / advisory ruling | **Issuing body:** PDPC | **Date:** published 7 July 2026 | **Status:** current | **Source:** `pdpc.gov.sg` regulatory guidance, tagged "Data Use" — **P**

**What it does.** It holds that **encrypted pathology patch files held in a cloud trusted execution environment are personal data to SGH but are NOT personal data to A\*STAR's Institute for Infocomm Research AI centre or to the Biomedical Data Hub** — because only SGH holds the keys. It is expressly conditional: if circumstances change such that those parties are able to identify individuals, they may be considered to be processing personal data.

**Why it matters.** It is a live, citable position on when encrypted health data ceases to be personal data in a third party's hands, and it sets a de facto safeguards standard for health AI in the cloud. **It is also party-relative — and none of the five research data access frameworks in §5 operates a per-recipient identifiability test.** This is the newest and most consequential regulatory position in the field and it sits entirely outside the access architecture.

Read it alongside the **Zuellig Pharma trusted execution environment guidance (7 December 2023)**: together they are becoming PDPC's de facto position on confidential computing for health data.

### 4.3.5 Enforcement — the complete corpus, and what it shows

The complete PDPC published corpus was enumerated from PDPC's own sitemap: **387 published Commission's Decisions and Voluntary Undertakings** spanning 21 April 2016 to 5 August 2026. Coverage of healthcare decisions is **verified, not sampled**.

**The leading health-data decisions:**

| Decision | Citation | Date published | Holding of note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **SingHealth and IHiS** | [2019] SGPDPC 3 | 15 Jan 2019 | The foundational health-data decision: medical data warrants a higher standard; controller / data-intermediary split |
| **Fullerton Healthcare and Agape CP Holdings** | [2023] SGPDPC 5 | 22 Jun 2023 | The leading vendor / data-intermediary and data-minimisation decision for health data |
| **Farrer Park Hospital** | [2022] SGPDPC 6 | 18 Nov 2022 | "As a hospital, the Organisation must be held to a higher standard"; email auto-forwarding; multi-factor authentication |
| **Institute of Mental Health** | **[2025] SGPDPC 1** | 31 Jul 2025 | **The only decision on secondary use of patient data for research recruitment.** Clinic signage amounted to implied consent; **s 15A deemed consent FAILED** for want of a communicated opt-out; express consent is best practice where medical information is used to select research subjects |
| **MRI Diagnostics / Clarity Radiology** | Case No. DP-1811-B2975 | 16 Oct 2020 | The teleradiology / SaaS decision: vendor versus data intermediary; ISO 27001 due diligence for radiology data |
| **Secur Solutions Group** | [2020] SGPDPC 8 | 24 Nov 2020 | The HSA blood-donor database; data intermediary of a statutory board; production data in test environments |
| **National Healthcare Group** | [2019] SGPDPC 46 | 9 Jan 2020 | Doctors' business contact information and SMC registration numbers are **outside** the Protection Obligation |
| **Cigna Europe Insurance** | [2019] SGPDPC 18 | 20 Jun 2019 | Health insurer; contractual safeguards with an offshore intragroup data intermediary satisfied ss 24 and 26(1) |
| **Actxa** | [2018] SGPDPC 5 | 18 Apr 2018 | Health and fitness IoT and app data; sensitive health-related data as an aggravating factor |
| **HMI Institute of Health Science** | [2024] SGPDPCS 5 | 29 Nov 2024 | Vendor-oversight standard for decommissioning health-sector IT systems; second offence |
| **Academy of Medicine Singapore** | [2024] SGPDPCS 4 | 2 Aug 2024 | Ransomware; unpatched FortiOS; RDP entry |

Health-sector **voluntary undertakings** include StarMed Specialist Centre (ECG and treadmill stress test data), Assisi Hospice (ICD disease classifications emailed to a wrong external address over nine months), SpeeDoc (laboratory results and photographs of rashes and wounds via a misconfigured S3 bucket), Widex Singapore (health card numbers and hearing-loss notes held on a sister company's server in Australia), Methodist Welfare Services / Bethany Nursing Home (vaccination records encrypted by ransomware), Manulife (medical reports on a lost unencrypted thumb drive) and Cantley LifeCare.

### 4.3.6 ✓ Verified negatives in this domain

- **No healthcare breach finding in 2025 or 2026 to date.** The most recent findings against healthcare respondents are HMI Institute (29 November 2024, $10,000) and Academy of Medicine (2 August 2024, $9,000). The only healthcare Commission's Decision in 2025 is the "no breach" decision in *Institute of Mental Health*.
- **Data Protection Appeal Committee decisions are not published.** PDPC publishes none and eLitigation returns zero. **There is no court judgment on appeal from PDPC involving health data.**
- **PDPA s 26(2) transfer exemptions are structurally unknowable** — s 26(3)(b) provides that they need not be gazetted (§4.18).
- **There is no publicly documented pending PDPC healthcare investigation** as at the retrieval date; PDPC does not publish a docket of open investigations.
- **The healthcare sector guidance predates the regime it will sit under.** The Advisory Guidelines for the Healthcare Sector (revised 20 September 2023) contain **zero** references to the NEHR or to the Health Information Bill or Act. The sector-specific PDPA guidance has not been updated for the statute that will govern the sector.

### 4.4.1 Public Sector (Governance) Act 2018 — what it does and what it does not publish

**Citation:** Act 5 of 2018 | **Status:** in force | **Source:** SSO `/Act/PSGA2018` — **P**

**ss 4, 6–8** authorise inter-agency data sharing by **ministerial direction** and create disclosure, misuse and re-identification offences. It binds MOH, HPB, HSA, the Communicable Diseases Agency and GovTech. The CDA was added as **First Schedule, Part 1, item 7A** with effect from 1 April 2025.

🚩 **Two structural transparency facts.** First, **the PSGA has no subsidiary legislation at all.** Second, its operative instruments are **directions**, which are not subsidiary legislation and carry no publication requirement — and **no data sharing direction in the health domain has been located by anyone.** So the mechanism by which health data actually moves between public agencies is, as a class, unreadable.

### 4.4.2 Public Sector (Governance) (Amendment) Act 2026 — what will change on commencement

**Uncommenced.** When commenced:

- **New s 4(5)** renames the instrument a **"data sharing and use direction"** and lets it authorise an agency to (a) share with another public sector agency; **(b) share with "any person that is not a Singapore public sector agency (called in this Act a non-public sector person), but only with the further authorisation of any Minister"**; (c) *use* information under its control; and (d) **re-identify anonymised information** under its control.
- **New s 4(6)** requires the direction to specify purposes, information, recipients and the anonymised information that may be re-identified — described "generally … or specifically (for example, by naming a particular non-public sector person)" per the explanatory statement.
- **New s 4(7)–(8)**: a "further authorisation" is a separate ministerial act narrowing the direction to specific purposes, information and named persons. **If the direction does not name which Minister may give it, any Minister may.**
- **New s 6(3)** retains the override: authority to share "overrides any obligation as to confidentiality under the common law", while **s 6(4)** preserves legal professional privilege and **contractual** confidentiality obligations.
- **New s 7(1A)** and **s 7(4A)** create offences for an employee or officer of a non-public sector person who discloses, or uses for gain or to another's harm or loss, shared information other than personal data. Penalty: fine up to **$5,000** or imprisonment up to **2 years** or both. "Officer" reaches directors, partners, chief executives, managers, secretaries, persons purporting to act as such, and committee members of unincorporated associations.
- **Clause 8 inserts a new PDPA s 48C(3)**: where a public agency shares personal data under a direction it "does not cease to have possession or control of the personal data merely by reason that" it shared it, and unauthorised downstream disclosure, use or re-identification is deemed unauthorised by both sender and recipient for PDPA ss 48D(1), 48E(1) and 48F(1). **This is the mechanism that makes the recipient's staff criminally liable under the PDPA for personal data, while PSGA s 7(1A)/(4A) covers non-personal information.**

**Health relevance.** Neither the Bill, the explanatory statement nor MDDI's 12 January 2026 press release names a health use case (the worked example is CDC Vouchers). But the mechanism is generic and MOH, HPB, HSA and the CDA are all Singapore public sector agencies. **The healthcare clusters and Synapxe are precisely "non-public sector persons".** So once commenced, this is the general route by which a health agency could be authorised to push identifiable health data to a cluster, to Synapxe, to a community care operator or to a private analytics vendor under statutory authority rather than contract. **The gating control is ministerial authorisation, not any independent or judicial body — and neither directions nor further authorisations are published.**

⚠️ **The Act also amends PDPA s 48C**, which makes it invisible to any title-based search for PDPA amendments. That is a good illustration of why the gazette sweep matters.

### 4.4.3 The institutional layer — who actually holds Singapore's public health data

| Body | Legal form | Inside the public-sector perimeter? |
|---|---|---|
| **MOH** | Ministry | Yes |
| **HPB, HSA, CDA** | Statutory boards | **Yes** — PSGA First Schedule; statutory secrecy duties (HPB Act s 39, HSA Act s 39, CDA Act s 35); OSA Schedule |
| **Synapxe Pte Ltd** (formerly Integrated Health Information Systems Pte Ltd) | **Private limited company**, Company Reg. No. 200814464H | **No** |
| **MOH Holdings Pte Ltd** and its subsidiaries (including MOH Office for Healthcare Transformation) | **Private limited companies** | **No** |
| **SingHealth (Singapore Health Services Pte Ltd), NHG (National Healthcare Group Pte Ltd), NUHS (National University Health System Pte Ltd)** | **Private limited companies** | **No** |
| **Agency for Integrated Care Pte Ltd** | **Private limited company**, a subsidiary of MOH Holdings — **not a statutory board** | **No** — it travels with the clusters, not with HPB and HSA |
| **HealthHub** | A platform operated by Synapxe | follows Synapxe |

⚠️ **Entity-name caution.** SingHealth's registered entity name appears as "**Singapore Health Services Pte Ltd**" in its website footer and in the form used in S 90/2015, but as "SingHealth Services Pte Ltd" in its own data protection policy. **Use the statutory form.** Separately, NHG now trades as "**NHG Health**"; the company name in the statute book remains **National Healthcare Group Pte Ltd** (S 90/2015, Schedule item 5) — the rebrand is a trading identity, not a change of legal form, and the effective date could not be confirmed from a primary source.

### 4.4.4 📌 What actually binds the clusters and Synapxe — the corrected position

**Do not write "they fall between two regimes".** They do not. The position, verified:

**What binds them:**
1. **The PDPA, as ordinary organisations** — admitted in their own published notices. Synapxe's notice says it is bound by the PDPA and may act as a data intermediary; SingHealth's policy characterises IHiS/Synapxe as its data intermediary.
2. **PDPA Part 9B, ss 48D–48F — criminal liability on individuals.** **PDPA s 48C(2) disapplies Part 9B only for *public officials*.** So ss 48D–48F ($5,000 / 2 years) **DO apply to cluster and Synapxe staff — precisely *because* they sit outside the public-sector regime.** Falling outside the PSGA *increases* individual exposure here. This corrects a widely repeated claim that there is an individual-liability gap.
3. **The Healthcare Services Act 2020 and its subsidiary legislation** — HCSA s 27 and General Regulations reg 38, and the licence-condition layer.
4. **The Cybersecurity Act 2018 — by designation.** SingHealth's electronic medical record system is designated critical information infrastructure.
5. **Contract, shareholder control and ministerial direction** — binding, and **unpublished**.
6. **Common law and professional regulation** — binding on the individuals.

**The two gaps that ARE real:**
1. **No statutory secrecy duty on any healthcare body.** The Statutory Bodies and Government Companies (Protection of Secrecy) Act 1983, Part 2, lists only "PSA Corporation Limited and its subsidiary corporations" — still true after S 120/2026. **No healthcare body carries a duty of the kind HPB Act s 39 and HSA Act s 39 impose on statutory boards.**
2. **Government Technology Agency Act Part 8 has never been used — for anyone.** The GTAA's "public entity" definition fits the clusters exactly, and Part 8 (ss 64–67) would let GovTech recommend, audit, advise and report. The phrase "designated public entity" appears across **the entire e-Gazette from 1998 to 2026 only in the GovTech Act itself**. The power has never been exercised.

**Corroborating evidence pointing the same way:** IMDA's Data Protection Trustmark register certifies **Synapxe, NHG, TTSH, IMH, Woodlands and AIC** against SS 714:2025, a standard whose stated aim is to demonstrate PDPA compliance — which a public agency, excluded from PDPA Parts 3–6, would have no reason to seek.

**What this means in practice.** Until the Health Information Act commences and the designations under it are made, **the clusters' and Synapxe's NEHR obligations rest entirely on contract and MOH direction.** The Act works by imposing duties on named statutory roles — System Operator (s 8), specified contributors (s 12), approved contributors (s 13), specified users (s 19), approved users (s 20) — rather than by reference to whether a body is a public agency. **Synapxe is the obvious candidate to be designated System Operator, but that is not yet a fact.**

### 4.4.5 The 2020 HealthHub transfer, and one live oddity

The national patient-facing health record portal is operated by **Synapxe**, a private limited company outside the public-sector perimeter, under a privacy policy last updated 26 July 2026. The NEHR itself "is owned by the Ministry of Health of Singapore and managed by Synapxe" — the ownership/operation split is stated on Synapxe's own programme page.

⚠️ **A published policy asserting compliance with a statute that is not in force.** The **AIC Data Protection Policy** states that AIC will handle personal data as required under the PDPA, the **Health Information Act 2026** and other relevant legislation. Two independent retrievals noticed this. It is a documented instance of the institutional layer running ahead of the statute book.

---

Amended by Act 11 of 2023 (wef 1 May 2023) and **Act 19 of 2025 (wef 5 Dec 2025)** | P |
