### 4.5.1 ⭐ HBRA s 13(4)–(5) — the one true statutory gateway for secondary use

This is the single most important secondary-use provision in the framework, and the only one currently in force.

**s 13(4):** a waiver under subsection (1) or (2) "has effect despite any obligation as to confidentiality or other restriction upon the disclosure or use of information imposed by law, contract or rules of professional conduct" (HBRA s 13(4)).

**s 13(5):** a researcher conducting research pursuant to a waiver "is not treated as being in breach of any obligation as to confidentiality" or other restriction imposed by law, contract or rules of professional conduct (HBRA s 13(5)).

**Three features make it load-bearing:**

1. **It operates on all three sources of duty at once** — statute, contract and professional codes. That matters given the structural finding in §4.7 that the practitioner's confidentiality duty is in none of the seven professional registration Acts but lives only in the professional codes. **s 13(4) reaches that duty directly.**
2. **It is exercised by a non-statutory body.** The HBRA gives a non-statutory, institution-appointed committee — the IRB — the statutory power to disentitle a patient of the confidentiality that would otherwise attach to their health information.
3. **It is currently the only such gateway in force.** Its two potential rivals are both uncommenced: the Health Information Act 2026, and the Public Sector (Governance) (Amendment) Act 2026's new s 6(3).

**The substantive test** is in the **Fifth Schedule, Part 2, paragraph 3**: the research cannot reasonably be done without identifiable form; obtaining consent would involve "a disproportionate amount of effort and resources relative to the research requirements"; no more than minimal risk; no adverse effect on rights and welfare; **and** the research "would reasonably be considered to contribute to the greater public good". **Paragraph 4** applies a four-limb test to health information obtained before **1 November 2017** and **omits the "greater public good" limb** — which is why practitioners speak of a "high bar" for post-1-November-2017 data.

📌 **"Greater public good" is defined only in MOH guidance, not in the statute.** MOH's *Guidance on the Requirement of Appropriate Consent* (17 May 2019, updated 29 October 2021) treats it as generally satisfied by epidemiology or population-wide studies at national or regional level, research with tangible measurable public benefit, or research with national, regional or international policy impact. **The operative gate for national health-data research therefore exists only in an advisory document.**

**⚠️ An unresolved point of construction — the most consequential in the domain.** **s 13(3)(b)** provides that a waiver "does not affect a person's duty to protect individually-identifiable information from unauthorised disclosure under sections 29 and 39 or imposed by law", and **s 13(3)(c)** provides that nothing in the section gives immunity to the custodian of individually-identifiable information for information disclosed otherwise than in accordance with the Act. On their face ss 13(3)(b)–(c) and ss 13(4)–(5) pull in opposite directions. **The natural reading — flagged as a reading, not a finding, and not resolved by any retrieved source — is that ss 13(4)–(5) protect the researcher acting pursuant to the waiver and remove the consent requirement, while s 13(3) preserves the custodian's separate s 29 / s 39 disclosure regime with its own gateways.** On that reading, **an IRB waiver does not by itself authorise a hospital to hand over identifiable records; the custodian still needs a s 29 gateway.** This should be flagged to a verifier, not stated as settled. It matters a great deal: if the reading is right, a data access committee approval is not merely the holder's commercial permission — it is part of how the holder satisfies itself that it is within a s 29 gateway.

### 4.5.2 The other operative HBRA data duties

- **s 27** — a duty on every holder of individually-identifiable information obtained for research to take all reasonable steps and safeguards, "including rendering information or material non-identifiable". Penalty: fine up to **$10,000** or 12 months.
- **s 28** — a bar on **re-identification** of information rendered non-identifiable, subject to seven gateways (consent; administration of the Act; court order; publicly available; a **Director-General public-interest opinion**; other written law; prescribed). Penalty **$20,000 / 2 years**.
- **s 29** — the same architecture for **disclosure** of research subjects' individually-identifiable information; **s 39** for tissue donors.
- **s 23(2)(c)** — every research institution must "formulate and implement appropriate standards, policies and procedures". **This is the statutory footing on which the cluster manuals, the university policies and, arguably, the institutional data access committees sit — but the Act says nothing about what those standards must contain.**
- **s 21(4)** — **there is no external appeal from an IRB refusal.** An appeal lies to the research institution, which may dismiss it, remit it, or send it to a second board, and "there is no appeal against the decision of the research institution or the second board".
- **s 35(2)(f)** and Tissue Banking Regulations **reg 15** — the only HBRA restriction on cross-border movement, and it is on **tissue**, not data. See §4.18.
- **Second Schedule, paragraphs 3 and 4, read with s 3(4)** — exclude **IDA s 59A national public health research** and **National Registry of Diseases collection** from "human biomedical research" **entirely**, so those pathways sit outside the HBRA's IRB and consent architecture. ⚠️ **This is the Second Schedule, not the First** — several files in the underlying research said First, and it is wrong. s 3(4): "Subsections (2) and (3) do not apply to such research, studies or activities that are specified in the Second Schedule." The carve-out was **widened** by **S 334/2019** (made 25 April 2019, in force 26 April 2019), which substituted the broader "National public health research as defined in and conducted in accordance with section 59A" for the narrower original.

### 4.5.3 The IRB layer in practice

MOH Circular 30/2025's distribution list names **54 research institutions and 22 IRBs**. But NUHS, HPB, HSA, AIC and others have **no IRB of their own** and run through the NHG Domain Specific Review Board. **The bulk of public-sector patient-data research therefore passes through SingHealth CIRB and NHG DSRB.**

**Data-only research qualifies for expedited review.** Human Biomedical Research Regulations **reg 20(2)(b)** permits expedited review of a research proposal involving "analysis of patient information without interaction with research subjects" where confidentiality measures satisfy the chairperson. **This is the single most-used door for secondary-use health data research.**

Since **1 April 2025**, collaborative studies across SingHealth, NHG, A\*STAR, NTU and NUS may use **Single IRB Review** under a five-party Memorandum of Agreement signed 18 March 2025 on an HBRA s 16 footing. **The Memorandum is unpublished** and was not even announced in SingHealth's own news feed (§6).

### 4.5.4 Two statutory silences that make the contractual layer inevitable

1. **HBRA s 40 codes of practice have never been issued** (§3E item 39). s 40(1)(b) expressly empowers the Director-General to issue codes "for the protection of the identity of individuals in relation to individually-identifiable human biological material and health information" — the exact gap. **Consequence: there is no national standard on identifiability of health research data.**
2. **The Health Information Act does not reconcile with the HBRA, and is uncommenced anyway.** Controlled term counts over the full text of Act 1 of 2026: **"research" 0 occurrences, "biomedical" 0**. There is no research carve-out, no HBRA carve-out and no consequential amendment to the HBRA. Meanwhile the Bioethics Advisory Committee **expressly parked de-identified-data governance with the Health Information Bill**, saying the governance of de-identified data is covered under that Bill and would not be discussed in its report. **So the question is deferred to a statute with no legal effect, while data access committees govern it by contract in the meantime.**

**And the BAC's own reports have not been accepted.** No MOH acceptance has been published for either the Big Data and AI report (21 February 2025) or the Human Nuclear Genome Editing report (23 October 2025), breaking the 2008 and 2010 pattern in which acceptance was followed by legislation. No BAC consultation is currently open; the BAC publications collection shows nothing after 24 October 2025.

### 4.5.5 The funder and institutional layer

| Instrument | What it is | How it binds |
|---|---|---|
| **NMRC Research Data Governance and Sharing Framework** (effective **1 January 2024**) | Funder framework | **By contract** — a term of the NMRC funding contract, incorporating the NMRC Research Grant Terms and Conditions v2.0 at cl 1.3; applies to listed schemes where NMRC funds **≥ S$250,000 direct cost** and the application was submitted on or after 1 January 2024 |
| **NMRC Data Request Form**, incorporating at Section V the **Terms of Use of the Final Research Data in the Research Data Repository** | **A published data use agreement** — publicly downloadable | By contract: acceptance "shall create a binding agreement between the Grantor, the Data Requestor and his Relevant Institution". 3-year Access Term; anti-re-identification; confidentiality; IP; publication controls; revocation; **Contracts (Rights of Third Parties) Act excluded** (cl 19) |
| **NMRC Research Data Repository** | National infrastructure, "developed by Synapxe Pte. Ltd." (Framework cl 6.2) | Minimum **10-year** retention; onward delivery via **TRUST** or **BRAIN** |
| **STEMPA** (Shorten Time to Execute Multi-party Project Agreements), **Guidebook Beta Version, May 2026, 52 pp** | National contracting framework | **By grant condition** — "selected STEMPA positions are mandated through grant conditions for applicable schemes". In force **1 April 2026** for NMRC OF-LCG and HHP MISSION Stream 1 |
| **Joint Statement on Research Integrity Relating to Scholarly Publications** (A\*STAR, NTU, NUS, SUTD, 22 November 2016) | Inter-institutional integrity standard | **By employment and disciplinary procedures.** Imposes a **10-year minimum research record retention** rule across all four institutions |
| **NTU Research Data Policy** | University policy | By employment or enrolment. **NTU owns all research data**; 10-year retention; mandatory data management plan before funds release; mandatory deposit; CC BY-NC default sharing licence |
| **NUS Research Data Management Policy** | University policy | By employment or enrolment. Minimum 10-year retention; data stored at NUS; no removal or sharing without the PI's permission. **Not public** — SharePoint, authentication required |

**STEMPA's data positions.** **PA3** allocates ownership of Foreground Data by collection, generation or enhancement — critically, "[s]hould there be a scenario where the ownership of data as defined under signed consent forms conflicts with the above principle, data shall be owned as stipulated by signed consent forms", so **the participant's consent form prevails over the national ownership rule**. **PA4** requires reference to the **STEMPA Data Sharing Guidebook**, which **is not published** (§6). **PA5** bars "contractual data-related indemnities, warranties, and liability caps", replacing contractual risk allocation with a **Data Incident Resolution Pathway** ending in a **Standing Escalation Committee** of institutional CEOs and Provosts and then the HHP EXCO Co-Chairs.

⚠️ **STEMPA's Guidebook is expressly a Beta Version and its Section 3.3 (Foreground Data Ownership) carries footnote 2: "This section will be updated in the next version." The single most legally consequential position is expressly provisional.**

⚠️ **STEMPA's scope excludes the whole industry and overseas story.** In scope: agreements between Singapore public sector institutions. Out of scope: agreements involving private sector or industry partners, foreign institutions or entities, and clinical trials. **So the national de-risking of research data contracting applies only to the public-to-public case** — the opposite of what "national framework" framing implies.

⚠️ **Two attribution cautions on STEMPA.** The Guidebook's governance diagram places two named individuals adjacent to a "Co-Chairs" label; **the association is not stated in words and they should not be cited as co-chairs**. And the STEMPA page moved: `a-star.edu.sg/bmsipo/stempa` now returns 404; the live page is `bmsipo.sg/stempa/`.

**STEMPA's PDPA breach-reporting allocation, analysed carefully.** The Data Responsibilities Framework provides that where a data incident is a personal data breach, the **Data Contributor** "takes the lead in reporting to the PDPC … including the 72-hour notification timeline, where applicable", and that if a participating institute is not subject to the PDPA (for example a statutory board) reporting is to be undertaken by the relevant PDPA-regulated Data Contributor.

**Read carefully, that allocates a task, not a duty.** "[T]akes the lead in reporting" and "where applicable" are coordination language. It does not purport to transfer another organisation's statutory obligation and does not release anyone from theirs. **The fair statement is that STEMPA allocates operational responsibility for performing the notification, not the statutory duty itself.** It would be an overstatement to say STEMPA reallocates the statutory duty by contract.

**But a contract cannot do more than that.** The PDPA's mandatory breach-notification obligation attaches to the organisation in respect of personal data in its possession or under its control. As between two institutes a contract can allocate who does the work; it cannot of itself extinguish or transfer the statutory obligation. **If a notifiable breach occurs in the hands of Institute A, the PDPC will look to Institute A.** ⚠️ **Verification note: the exact statutory addressee of the PDPA Part 6A duty was not read by any agent. Before this point goes into advice, read Part 6A and confirm the addressee. Do not cite section numbers for it.**

The fallback limb — a PDPA-regulated contributor reporting for a body that is not PDPA-regulated — is **private ordering filling a statutory perimeter gap**, and should be described as exactly that. And because **PA5 bars indemnities**, the institute that takes the reporting lead has **no contractual remedy** if the incident was another party's fault; the substitute is a body of institutional heads deciding liability among themselves, with no external review, and expressly aiming at "accountability and remediation, rather than punitive outcomes".

Finally, **STEMPA addresses only one of at least three notification channels**, and not the one with the shortest deadline: the CS/DS Essentials carry a **2-hour MOH notification** running concurrently with the CSA and PDPC channels, and MOH's incident-reporting framework does not launch until 2027 (§4.11). STEMPA's Guidebook also warns that an incident may require informing overseas authorities where data is stored or transferred abroad **without saying who does that**.

### 4.6.1 🔥 SG500K — the largest undertaking in the domain, with no located governing instrument

**What it is.** National Precision Medicine Phase III, operating as **SG-INSIGHT**, launched **14 November 2025** with a Memorandum of Understanding between PRECISE and NHG Health, NUHS and SingHealth. It runs **2025–2031**. It recruits a **further 400,000–450,000 participants on top of SG100K**, to exceed **500,000** — described by the Minister as roughly **10% of Singapore's local resident population** — with **whole-genome sequencing**. Funded via the MOH/NMRC Office and MOH Holdings under RIE2025.

**What has been searched, exhaustively:** the full 214-URL `npm.sg` sitemap; the A\*STAR and GIS data-access pages; the four NPM policy PDFs; three government sitemaps (MOH 11,144 URLs; npm.sg 214; Synapxe 440); the complete TRUST platform sitemap (125 URLs); and **every Hansard sitting day from 1 January 2024 to 15 August 2026 — 79 sittings, 7,209 sections, complete not sampled.**

**What was found:** no SG500K or SG-INSIGHT data access policy; no data access committee; no terms of reference; no participant information sheet; no consent form; no published Phase III IRB approval. **No governing instrument, no consent framework document, and no parliamentary scrutiny of it whatsoever — not one Parliamentary Question.** For the existing programme, **no documented withdrawal mechanics** were located despite a participant FAQ promising participants can opt out of future use at any time, and **no re-consent plan for Phase III** was located.

**What is being asserted, precisely.** Two independent agents using different methods — a sitemap and policy-document sweep, and an exhaustive Hansard sweep — searched the obvious primary sources and found nothing; a third agent, tasked specifically with this question, confirmed both negatives with high confidence and identified the **PRECISE–cluster MOU of 14 November 2025 as the only located instrument that could govern it, its text unpublished**. The correct statement is therefore: **the only located candidate instrument is an unpublished MOU, and no access policy, consent framework, data access committee or terms of reference has been located by any of three agents searching exhaustively.**

**Why it matters.** SG500K will feed the PRECISE-SG100K access pathway. So the access governance for a whole-genome dataset covering a tenth of the resident population would, on the current architecture, be **a non-statutory programme committee that publishes no terms of reference, no member list, no chair and no conflict-of-interest policy.**

**And the Genetic Information Bill would not reach it.** Verified independently: the Bill regulates **insurers and employers only**. It contains no research-access provisions and would not govern SG500K's collection, consent, access or use.

### 4.6.2 🔥 AI training on health data — a stated government position with no instrument

**5 August 2026.** Asked directly whether patients would be asked for **explicit consent** before their data is used to train AI models, and whether the scope included the NEHR, **Minister Ong Ye Kung declined both**, saying that scoping down the data and seeking everyone's consent would make it "a non-starter" (Hansard, sitting of 5 August 2026). He confirmed the pipeline runs on TRUST, "established in 2022 precisely for research, development and innovation efforts".

**12 January 2026**, Second Reading: Senior Minister of State Tan Kiat How stated that de-identified NEHR health information may be shared through established platforms such as TRUST for research purposes, and that this could include training for artificial intelligence models.

**It is already happening, and disclosed in a government instrument.** AIHGle 2.0 records that **Synapxe trained a deployed model on approximately 3.6 million derived NEHR records covering 2015–2021**.

**What governs it. On the evidence assembled: nothing binding.**
- The **Health Information Act 2026** is wholly uncommenced and contains **zero** occurrences of "research" or "biomedical".
- **AIHGle 2.0** is **voluntary and is not a licence condition** — it looks mandatory and is not.
- **There is no Singapore AI statute** — a verified negative (§4.12.4).
- What is left is the **TRUST data access committee's contractual conditions**, decided by a committee acting on a delegation from data contributors.
- **No instrument requires patient notification that their data was used for AI training.**

**The characterisation for the client.** National-record data is being used to train AI models; the responsible Minister has expressly rejected a consent requirement on the floor of the House; and the only instrument standing between that data and its use is a **contract**. This is the pattern in §2.2 in its purest form, on its highest-stakes application.

**The one published record of a SIMFONI data approval** is the TRUST approved-project entry "SIMFONI Pipe Cleaner 1 (Fine-tuning in enTRUST with Public Data Assets)", approved **11 May 2026**, active. It states no consent basis, no IRB reference and no reasons.

**Access statistics, from a written Parliamentary answer of 7 May 2026:** **40 approved TRUST applications — 34 public-sector and 6 private companies.** The part of the question asking for a public registry was not answered.

### 4.6.3 Clinical genetic testing — the regulatory position, stated properly

The strongest available statement of the position is **not** "no movement in 17 months". It is this:

**The regulation of Clinical Genetic and Genomic Service under the HCSA was postponed indefinitely on 26 March 2024 — "postponed to a later date until further notice" — and that remains the standing position 2 years and 5 months later.** It is the only announcement on the entire HCSA site not tagged [CLOSED].

Layered on top: MOH Circular 234/2020 said the 2018 Code of Practice would become **CGGS Regulations**; MOH Circular 11/2025 (14 March 2025) says it will instead become **Licence Conditions or guidelines**; and all three of the "2H 2025" commitments in that circular, plus the promised separate LGTS update, have never issued.

**So what actually governs clinical genetic testing today** is the **2018 Code of Practice** (updated 16 December 2020), plus the **licence conditions on PGT-M and PGT-SR** modified by the two-step in §4.2.3, plus the professional codes.

---

### 4.7.1 ⭐ The structural finding — confidentiality is a code duty, not a statutory one

**None of the seven professional registration Acts imposes a direct statutory patient-confidentiality duty on the practitioner.** The duty exists only in the professional codes, made enforceable **indirectly** through the disciplinary-findings provisions:

| Act | Disciplinary hook |
|---|---|
| Medical Registration Act 1997 | **s 59D(1)** |
| Dental Registration Act 1999 | **s 50(1)** |
| Allied Health Professions Act 2011 | **s 53(1)** |
| Nurses and Midwives Act 1999 | **s 19(1)(e)** |
| Traditional Chinese Medicine Practitioners Act 2000 | **s 19(1)(f), (i), (k)** |

This is the **same pattern** as the s 90 codes architecture and the HCSA circular layer: the operative norm sits below statute, enforced indirectly. Three independent instances, falling out of the sources rather than being imposed on them.

**The courts have judicially endorsed the codes**, which strengthens rather than weakens the layer: SMC ECEG Guideline 4.2.3.1 (2002 edition) and its 2016 successor C7 have been applied by the Court of Three Judges (§4.19).

### 4.7.2 Currency defects worth naming

- The **2016 SMC Handbook on Medical Ethics still cites the repealed Private Hospitals and Medical Clinics Act** as the operative statute governing disclosure of a deceased patient's information for clinical audit, education or research (HME C7.8). That is a live currency defect in the operative ethical guidance.
- The **TCM practitioners' code dates from January 2006** — twenty years old.
- The **SMC ECEG and HME revision has been live since 22 December 2020 with no edition published**; SMC's last published word on it is 15 January 2021.
- The confidentiality guidance now in force **predates the Healthcare Services Act 2020, the NEHR mandatory-contribution regime and the Health Information Act.**

**Verified negative:** no consultation, draft or announced revision from 2023 onwards was found for the SDC ECEG, the SNB Code, the SPC Code of Ethics, the AHPC Code of Professional Conduct, the TCMPB Ethical Code or the OOB Code of Professional Conduct.

### 4.7.3 TCM sits outside the national record entirely — state the negative

**Citation, settled:** *Singapore Parliamentary Debates, Official Report* (**12 February 2026**) **vol 96, sitting no 17, written answer 34** — question by Mr Cai Yinzhou, answered by Mr Ong Ye Kung. The answer states that **traditional Chinese medicine is not a licensable healthcare service under the Healthcare Services Act 2020**, and that data from TCM practitioners are not within the data types to be contributed to the NEHR. Combined with a code dating from January 2006, this means **a whole modality of care sits outside the national health record and is governed by a twenty-year-old code.** That deserves to be stated explicitly rather than left implicit.

📌 **A dating conflict, now settled.** One source recorded this as a sitting of **13 February 2026**. **There was no sitting on 13 February 2026** — the Hansard API returns HTTP 500 for that date and HTTP 200 with 86 sections for 12 February 2026. The 13 February citation is wrong and must not be used. Note also that "written answer 34" (the question's number within the sitting, as printed in Hansard) and an MOH newsroom "PQ 1393" reference are **not alternative citations for the same field**; use the Hansard convention and footnote the newsroom reference separately.

Related: from a written answer in the same sitting, **optometrists in HCSA-licensed institutions may be given NEHR access; those outside such institutions are not required to submit data.**

⚠️ **A gap in this domain, stated as a gap:** whether there is a **dental equivalent** of the TCM and optometry "proper records to be kept" regulations is **unresolved** — no such regulation was located, but the negative has not been verified.

Who may consent to treatment for, access the health information of, or receive health information about, a person who cannot decide for themselves — and the reporting regimes that override health confidentiality to protect vulnerable people.

### 4.8.1 The Advance Medical Directive Act — the strictest information regime in Singapore health law

It is worth naming for contrast with everything else in this bundle. **s 15(1) makes it an offence merely to ask** whether a person has made an advance medical directive. **s 6(3)** confines access to the register to a closed statutory list. **s 15(3)** protects the fact of having made one. MOH was asked in April 2026 to relax both the doctor-witness requirement and the s 15 prohibition and **declined**.

Set against the general pattern in §2.2, this shows that where Parliament has decided a category of health information deserves hard protection, it has legislated for it in unmistakable terms — and has recently refused to soften it.

### 4.8.2 The gap in the middle: advance care planning

**Advance care planning is policy, not law, and there is no proposal to change that.** myACP launched 19 July 2025; advance care plans are recorded in the NEHR and are viewable by healthcare workers and ACP facilitators; and the **Nominated Healthcare Spokesperson has no legal authority whatever**. So Singapore has a statutory instrument (the advance medical directive) hedged with criminal protections and effectively frozen, and alongside it a national programme with no statutory basis whose output sits in the national record.

Related: MOH declined to surface Lasting Power of Attorney status on the NEHR in answer to a Parliamentary Question of **7 July 2026**, on the basis that LPA data "is protected under the Mental Capacity Act".

### 4.8.3 Where confidentiality is overridden to protect vulnerable people

- **CYPA s 9** — compelled information with a privilege override; **s 12(2)** is the only mandatory report duty in the Act; **s 115** is permissive notification; **s 116** makes onward disclosure an offence. Child abuse reporting by healthcare professionals is **discretionary**, guided by the Sector-Specific Screening Guide and the Child Abuse Reporting Guide, not mandated.
- **VAA s 9(3)(a)** — power to examine and copy health records; **s 18(3)** permits treatment despite a donee's or deputy's refusal; **s 24** is a two-way inter-agency disclosure gateway.
- **Social Residential Homes Act 2025 s 42** — power to obtain information from a licensee, in force since 1 July 2026.

⚠️ **Health data in family and criminal proceedings is a real coverage gap in this bundle** — see §7.

### 4.9.2 The four verified confidentiality provisions

**Infectious Diseases Act 1976, s 25** — headed "**Protection of identity of person with HIV Infection or other sexually transmitted disease**" (use the full heading). A person aware in the course of functions under the Act that another has HIV infection or a sexually transmitted disease **must not disclose identifying information** except through **twelve** enumerated gateways (a)–(l): consent; administration of the Act; provision of information to a police officer under CPC s 22 or s 424; court order; treating or counselling practitioners and health staff; **a blood, organ, semen or breast milk bank** that has or will receive material from the person; statistical and epidemiological reporting where identity is not made known; **the victim of a sexual assault** by the person; **the Controller of Immigration** for Immigration Act purposes; **next-of-kin upon death**; any person to whom in the Director's opinion it is in the public interest; and where authorised by the Minister to publish for public health or public safety. **Penalty, s 25(2): fine up to $10,000 or imprisonment up to 3 months or both.**

The 2024 amendment touched s 25 only at (3)(b) ("below 16 years of age" → "a minor") and inserted a new s 25(4) defining "legal guardian". **It did not touch s 25(1), the (a)–(l) list, or the penalty.**

**Termination of Pregnancy Act 1974, s 7** — headed "Privilege against disclosure of matters relating to treatment to terminate pregnancy". A person concerned with keeping medical records in connection with, or participating in, treatment to terminate pregnancy must not disclose facts or information relating to the treatment unless the pregnant woman expressly consents, "except to such persons and for such purposes as may be prescribed". **Penalty, s 7(2): fine up to $2,000 or imprisonment up to 12 months or both.**

**Voluntary Sterilisation Act 1974, s 5** — headed "Privilege against disclosure of matters or treatment relating to sexual sterilisation", and **structurally identical to TPA s 7**. **Penalty, s 5(2): fine up to $10,000 or imprisonment up to 12 months or both.**

📌 **A clean, citable primary-text point:** TPA s 7 and VSA s 5 are identical in structure — same "is concerned with the keeping of medical records … or participates in …" formula, same express-consent gateway, same prescribed-persons-and-purposes exception — and **differ only in the fine: $2,000 against $10,000.**

**Human Organ Transplant Act 1987, s 28** — headed "Disclosure of information". A person must not disclose information whereby the identity of a donor, a person for whom consent or authority was given, or a recipient "may become publicly known", subject to six exceptions in s 28(2): administering and enforcing the Act; referring a complaint about a registered medical practitioner under Medical Registration Act s 39(1); **pursuant to a court order or when otherwise required by law**; **for the purposes of hospital administration or bona fide medical research**; with the consent of the person concerned; and where the disclosure is or would be privileged. **Penalty, s 28(3): fine up to $10,000 or imprisonment up to 12 months or both.**

📌 **HOTA s 28 is the weakest of the four as a confidentiality shield.** Its (2)(d) exception — "hospital administration or bona fide medical research" — is far wider than anything in the TPA or VSA, and (2)(c) already covers court orders and any other legal requirement. **It is also the only one of the four that the Health Information Act touches**, by s 113 adding a seventh gateway (§4.1.1).

**And all four are subject to CPC s 20(5)** (§2.5). None contains a clause preserving itself against the Criminal Procedure Code.

### 4.9.3 The compelled-disclosure counter-example

**Misuse of Drugs Regulations reg 19** requires clinicians to report suspected drug addicts to the Director of Medical Services **and to the Director of the Central Narcotics Bureau** within **7 days**, with no confidentiality protection attaching. This is the special category where the law **compels** disclosure rather than restricting it, and it belongs in the Limit 1 group in §2.3. ⚠️ **[VERIFY QUOTATION]** — proxy-derived and not re-verified.

### 4.9.4 The HIV viral-load regulations — do not compress the figures

**S 292/2024**, made and first published 8 April 2024, in operation **9 April 2024**, prescribes for the Infectious Diseases Act **s 23** undetectable-viral-load defence (introduced by Act 13 of 2024 s 23 — **not** s 25):

| Regulation | Figure | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| reg 2 | **6 months** | the period for which an undetectable viral load must have been maintained preceding the sexual activity |
| reg 3(a) | **9 months** | the period before the sexual activity within which the test must have been taken |
| reg 3(b) | **6 months** | the earlier test must have been taken no later than 6 months before the reg 3(a) test |
| reg 4 | **200 copies per millilitre of blood** | detectable at or above; undetectable below |
| reg 5 | — | a **clinical laboratory service licensee under the HCSA** is a "recognised laboratory" |

⚠️ **A shorthand such as "<200 copies/ml, 6 months, 9 months" is correct as a set of figures but ambiguous as to which is which** — "6 months" appears twice doing two different jobs. Do not compress them into a list.

### 4.9.5 A real protective gap affecting an identifiable population

The MOM six-monthly medical examination regime for migrant domestic workers requires clinics to submit **all** results — including HIV, VDRL, tuberculosis and pregnancy — and **employers can view them** through an MOM eService. The **Employment of Foreign Manpower (Work Passes) Regulations 2012 (S 569/2012)** make examination and, for migrant domestic workers, blood testing "for the purpose of detecting any infectious disease or testing for pregnancy" a work-pass condition.

**The gap:** Infectious Diseases Act **s 25 binds a person acting "in the performance or exercise of his or her functions or duties under this Act"**. A clinic conducting a work-permit medical is acting under MOM work-pass conditions, not under the Infectious Diseases Act. **So migrant workers' HIV results may reach MOM and their employers without the Act's criminal confidentiality protection attaching.** This was identified during the research as a live protective gap deserving a dedicated look, and **nobody looked**. It is stated here as an identified issue, not as a settled conclusion — the analysis has not been done. See §7.

### 4.9.6 The re-anchoring of "institution" definitions on 5 December 2025

The **Statutes (Miscellaneous Amendments) Act 2025 (Act 19 of 2025)**, in force **5 December 2025** (S 749/2025), amended **s 2 only** of the Termination of Pregnancy Act ("approved institution"), the Voluntary Sterilisation Act ("approved permanent premises"; "health institution"), the Human Organ Transplant Act ("licensee") and the National Registry of Diseases Act ("approved conveyance"; "approved permanent premises") — in each case **re-anchoring the definition to the Healthcare Services Act 2020 licensing perimeter**. It did **not** touch TPA s 7, VSA s 5, HOTA s 28 or the NRDA confidentiality provisions.

⚠️ **Consequence:** the 2020 Revised Edition definitions in those four statutes are superseded. **Any statement in this or any other bundle resting on those definitions needs re-checking against the post-5-December-2025 text.**

The same Act also **deleted sub-paragraph (a) of PDPA Second Schedule, Part 3, Division 1, paragraph 3** — a discrete, in-force change to a PDPA schedule that no other source in this project records, and whose effect has **not been characterised**. See §7.

---

## 4.10 DISEASE NOTIFICATION AND REGISTRIES

**The organising distinction, maintained throughout:** obligations to **REPORT** (duties, criminally sanctioned, running *inward* to the State) are structurally different from permissions to **DISCLOSE** (powers, discretionary, running *outward*). A third category — **override and immunity clauses** — is what makes the reporting duties bite through professional confidentiality.

- **REPORT:** IDA ss 6, 7(2), 10(1)(a), 55(1)(e), 59A(3); NRDA ss 6–7; Registration of Births and Deaths Act 2021 ss 7, 8, 22, 23; WSH (Incident Reporting) Regulations reg 7; TPA s 11(2)(a) regulations.
- **DISCLOSE:** IDA ss 25, 25A, 57A, 57B, 21B(2); NRDA ss 9, 10, 11, 12.
- **OVERRIDE / IMMUNITY:** **IDA s 10(4)**, **IDA s 67A**, **NRDA s 17(2)**, **Coroners Act 2010 s 17 (which overrides NRDA s 8 by name)**.

### 4.10.1 🔥 The contact-tracing safeguards have been repealed with no successor

**Infectious Diseases (Amendment) Act 2024 (Act 13 of 2024), s 48(e)–(f), with effect from 9 April 2024**, deleted **Part 11 ("Personal Contact Tracing Data")** of the COVID-19 (Temporary Measures) Act 2020, together with the **Sixth and Seventh Schedules**.

Those were the safeguards enacted after the 2021 TraceTogether police-access controversy: the **purpose limitation**, the **bar on police access**, and the **deletion mandate**. All gone.

- Verified: **"personal contact tracing data" returns ZERO matches across all in-force Acts.** Part 11 now reads "(Repealed)".
- The successor power is **IDA s 19A** ("Surveillance and contact tracing measures at premises"). It carries **no purpose limitation, no law-enforcement bar, no deletion duty and no misuse offence** in respect of the data it generates.

⚠️ **Two corrections to statements in circulation.** First, **s 19A and the s 2 definition of "contact tracing measure" were inserted by the Infectious Diseases (Amendment) Act 2019 (Act 5 of 2019), not by Act 13 of 2024.** Act 13 of 2024 s 2(c) merely *amended* the definition (inserting "or other at-risk individuals" after "contacts") and s 17 amended s 19A, adding subsections (1A), (1B), (2A) and (2B) with effect from 9 April 2024. **Do not say that the same Act both removed the safeguards and created the successor power.** Second, the correct repealing provision is **s 48(e) of Act 13 of 2024, with effect from 9 April 2024**.

📌 **And the framing must be corrected. The repeal WAS explained — it was simply not debated.** The Minister for Health devoted approximately **1,700 words** of his Second Reading speech to the repeal, including the reasons, the deactivation timeline, the deletion of the data, and a stated intention to return to Parliament if a future digital collection tool is deployed: *Singapore Parliamentary Debates, Official Report* (**Monday 7 March 2024**) **vol 95, sitting no 132** (14th Parliament, Second Session).

**What is true, and evidenced, is narrower and better:** *no Member of Parliament spoke to it.* "Part 11", "TraceTogether" and "SafeEntry" appear only inside the Minister's own opening speech; the two backbenchers who spoke addressed mental capacity, isolation limits and HIV. **No amendment was moved and no division was called.** Set against the enactment of those same safeguards — which followed a debate of some 212,926 characters opened by a ministerial apology — the contrast is the finding.

**The honest formulation is "explained by the Minister but not debated by the House", not "removed silently".**

### 4.10.2 The notification schema has left legislation

**S 189/2025**, in force 12 April 2025, replaced reg 3 of S 612/2008 so that notification under IDA s 6(1)–(3) must be given **through the electronic notification system**, or if unavailable by email to a CDA address, and replaced the prescribed statutory forms with **"the current version of the form required by the Director-General"**. **The notification data schema now sits outside legislation entirely.** It also updated the register URL and replaced "Director" with "Director-General of Health".

⚠️ **Date trap:** gazetted 24 March 2025, in force **12 April 2025**.

### 4.10.3 The Communicable Diseases Agency — a new body, and a notable split

**CDA Act 2025 = Act 4 of 2025**, commenced **in whole** (not in part) on **1 April 2025** by S 181/2025.

**The CDA Act confers no compulsory information power at all.** All compulsion remains with the **Director-General of Health** under IDA ss 7, 10 and 59A. The CDA did **not** take over IDA s 6 notification — only **vaccination-registry functions** moved. **So the National Immunisation Registry is now CDA's, not HPB's, while the power to compel the underlying data stays with the Director-General of Health.**

The CDA is inside every public-sector perimeter the clusters are outside: **PSGA First Schedule item 7A**; **Statutory Bodies and Government Companies (Protection of Secrecy) Act Schedule item 5A**; **Official Secrets Act Schedule item 5A**; and added to the PDPA Statutory Bodies Notification as item 9A by **S 217/2025**.

⚠️ **"Communicable Diseases Agency" appears zero times in the Health Information Act 2026.** MOH's "Our partners" page renders the name as "Communicable Disease Agency"; the Act and the PSGA Schedule use "Communicable Diseases Agency".

### 4.10.4 The NRDA — the best-structured onward-use regime in Singapore health law

This deserves saying, because the bundle should not read as uniformly critical.

- **s 8(2)** prohibits disclosure of registry information; **s 8(3)** makes breach an offence — **$10,000 / 12 months**.
- **s 9** makes anonymised disclosure the default.
- **s 10** permits identifiable disclosure only on the Director's approval, against **four mandatory considerations** in s 10(2).
- **s 12** permits identifiable disclosure for research only on **four findings**, including **"the requisite consent"** and a requirement that the research **cannot be carried out with anonymised information** (s 12(2)) — **the only consent requirement anywhere in the State-access field** (§4.21).
- **ss 12(3)–(4)** bind the researcher downstream.

⚠️ But **Coroners Act s 17(1)(ba) and (3) expressly override NRDA s 8 by name** in favour of a forensic pathologist, and the whole regime is subject to CPC s 20(5).

### 4.10.5 Registry gaps

- **Stroke is not a reportable disease** under the NRDA — the five reportable conditions are cancer, chronic kidney failure, acute myocardial infarction, and the two living-donor categories — **yet the National Registry of Diseases Office operates a Singapore Stroke Registry and releases data from it.** No stroke notification regulations exist. **The statutory basis for the Stroke Registry has not been established.**
- **No regulations exist under NRDA s 23.** Sections 11 and 12 both depend on consent "in the prescribed form and manner", and **none of the eight in-force sets of NRDA subsidiary legislation prescribes it.** The open question — **is NRDA s 12 a dead letter in practice?** — has not been answered.
- **The four NRDO data-request forms**, which contain the binding data-use conditions, were not retrieved.
- **Verified negative:** no subsidiary legislation was made under the NRDA in 2025 or 2026. The only NRDA items in the Gazette in the period are the appointment notices of 27 June 2025 (Registrar Dr Ruth Lim and Deputy Registrar Ms Davina Lim, both with effect from 23 June 2025).
- **Verified negative:** no subsidiary legislation was made under the Infectious Diseases Act in 2026. The last is S 638/2025 (30 September 2025), on maritime quarantine.

### 4.10.6 A compulsory transfer with no located legal basis

The CDA's Pandemic Preparedness and Response Framework states that laboratories are "**required**" to submit samples to the National Public Health Laboratory. **MOH Circular No. 13/2023** (7 March 2023) imposes compulsory specimen and isolate submission. But **no instrument imposing that duty has been located** — it is a compulsory transfer of human specimens resting, so far as the public record shows, on a circular.

Relatedly, **wastewater surveillance — on which the Framework expressly relies — has no located legal instrument at all**: no statute, no regulation, no published policy. Nobody has checked the Environmental Public Health Act, National Environment Agency instruments, or the Animals and Birds Act.


### 4.10.7 IDA s 59A — the compelled national public health research power, and how it changed

This is the State's compelled-data research power, and its history is a clean narrative that had not been told anywhere in the underlying research.

**Provenance.** **s 59A and Part 7A were inserted by the Infectious Diseases (Amendment) Act 2008 (Act 10 of 2008), s 32**, commenced 10 June 2008 — **not** in 2019, as some accounts say. ⚠️ Citation trap: that Act is **Act 10 of 2008** but was published as **Acts Supplement No. 11 of 2008**.

**Anonymisation was absolute for eleven years.** As enacted in 2008, **s 59A(4)** contained no override, and the third-party gateway in the then s 59A(5) reached **anonymised material only**. There was no subsection empowering the use of identifiable material at all.

**What changed in 2019.** The **Infectious Diseases (Amendment) Act 2019 (Act 5 of 2019), s 23** — again note the trap: Act 5 of 2019, Acts Supplement No. 8 of 2019 — did three things:

| Provision of Act 5 of 2019 | Effect |
|---|---|
| **s 23(c)–(d)** | converted the s 59A(2) factors from a cumulative checklist into factors, the Director "must consider the extent to which all or any of the following factors exist" — the Minister's stated purpose being to clarify that the section "allows national public health research to be conducted even when there is no imminent risk or crisis" |
| **s 23(h)** | inserted the words **"unless the Director directs otherwise"** into s 59A(4) — the anonymisation override |
| **s 23(i)** | substituted s 59A(5), **deleting the word "anonymised"** from the third-party gateway and widening its source from "received under subsection (3)" to "received under **this Act**"; and inserted the identifiability power as **s 59A(5A)** |

**Renumbering.** The 2020 Revised Edition renumbered s 59A(5A) as **s 59A(6)**, the former (6) as (7) and the former (7) as (8). So the identifiability override commonly cited as "s 59A(6)" was enacted as s 59A(5A).

**The structural asymmetry worth stating.** **s 59A(6) attaches a satisfaction test to the release of identifiable data; s 59A(4) attaches no criterion, threshold or record-keeping duty to the direction that disapplies anonymisation.** Whether s 59A(6) conditions a s 59A(4) direction is not resolved on the face of the section — s 59A(6) is expressed as a permission, not as a precondition.

**s 59A(3)(c)**, also inserted in 2019, repurposes material already in the Director-General's hands under **any** provision of the Act — surveillance data under s 7, notification data under ss 6 and 10, medical examination material under s 8 — for research. **No fresh compulsion, no fresh consent, and no notice to the individual.**

**"Third party" is not defined** and is not limited to public agencies, research institutions, Singapore-based recipients or bodies under IRB supervision. The only control is the s 59A(5)(c) power to impose conditions, breach of which is an offence.

**The re-identification penalty is the general one.** s 59A(7) prescribes no penalty of its own, so it falls to **IDA s 65 — a fine up to $10,000 or imprisonment up to 6 months** on a first offence. Neither Act 11 of 2023 nor Act 13 of 2024 amended s 65. **That is the maximum exposure for deliberate re-identification of national public health research data.**

**And the immunity that makes it work.** **IDA s 67A** (inserted by Act 5 of 2019) provides that no person commits an offence under any written law or any breach of confidence, incurs civil liability, or is liable to professional discipline, by virtue merely of disclosing information in good faith and with reasonable care in accordance with any requirement under the Act. **A s 59A(3) requirement is a requirement under the Act — so a clinician or laboratory complying with a s 59A requisition is immunised against breach of confidence, civil liability and professional discipline.**

⚠️ **A citation trap for anyone quoting the Infectious Diseases Act.** The 2020 Revised Edition reads "**the Director**". **Healthcare Services (Amendment) Act 2023 (Act 11 of 2023) s 28(8)** — note again, **Acts Supplement No. 15 of 2023 is not Act 11** — deleted that definition and directed that "Director" be replaced by "**Director-General of Health**" throughout a long list of provisions expressly including s 59A(1)–(8), with effect from **1 May 2023** (S 253/2023). **Any Infectious Diseases Act quotation using "Director" is pre-May-2023 text.**

**A verified negative:** no published framework, committee, approval body, application process or policy document governing s 59A was located, and it was searched for specifically. As at 15 August 2026, s 59A operates on the s 59A(2) statutory criteria, the s 59A(6) satisfaction test applied by the Director-General of Health, conditions under s 59A(5)(c), and whatever unpublished internal governance exists.

🔴 **And an asymmetry that should be flagged wherever s 59A is relied on as the safeguard.** **s 59A is a research power, not a surveillance power.** Routine genomic surveillance — sequencing every confirmed case to find clusters — is arguably the exercise of the Director-General's ordinary surveillance and outbreak-investigation functions under **IDA ss 6–10 and s 19A**, not "national public health research" under s 59A(1). ⚠️ **Which characterisation applies is not established in either direction**, and it is consequential: **if surveillance runs under ss 6–10, then none of s 59A's safeguards are engaged at all** — no anonymisation default, no s 59A(6) necessity test, no s 59A(7)(b) re-identification offence. **The safeguards this bundle treats as the controls on compelled infectious-disease data may simply not attach to the largest identifiable-data activity in the domain.** Nothing retrieved resolves it; the published record does not contain the answer.

### 4.10.8 📌 The National Public Health Laboratory is an HCSA licensee — and that makes it a compulsory NEHR contributor

**S 118/2026** (§4.2 table item 13; §4.10 table item 18) is on its face a naming exemption, but it discloses a fact of first-order importance: it calls the **Communicable Diseases Agency** "**the licensee**" and grants exemption from HCSA s 29(2A) "in relation to the use of the following names **for the provision of a clinical laboratory service**" — being "Communicable Diseases Agency" as the licensee's name and "**National Public Health Laboratory**" as its business name. **The drafting is unambiguous: CDA is an HCSA licensee providing a licensable clinical laboratory service, trading as the NPHL.**

**Three consequences.**

1. ⭐ **The HIA 2026 contribution duty reaches it.** **First Schedule Part 1 item 4** makes "[a]n HCSA licensee licensed to provide a clinical laboratory service" a **specified contributor** of "**Laboratory test reports**"; **s 12(1)** says a specified contributor "must contribute". Clinical laboratories sit in **Batch 1 — by 1 September 2027** (§3F item 56). **So Singapore's national public health genomics laboratory is, on the face of the Act, a compulsory NEHR contributor.**
2. **The whole HCSA regime applies to it** — Licence Conditions, s 27 and regs 38 and 45 obligations, and the HCSA data-security overlay — in the same way they reach a private laboratory. **That is a materially different position from treating NPHL as a bare government laboratory, and the bundle should not describe CDA as sitting outside health-services regulation.**
3. ⭐ **CDA sits on both sides of the bundle's central seam.** It is simultaneously a **public agency** (S 149/2013 Schedule item 9A, added by S 217/2025 wef 1 April 2025 — so PDPA s 4(1)(c) applies; PSGA First Schedule item 7A) **and an HCSA licensee**. Its PDPA position is that of a public agency; its HCSA position is that of a licensee.

🔴 **The unresolved question, and it is the most consequential in the pathogen-genomics domain: is a genome sequence part of a "laboratory test report"?** **"Laboratory test report" is not defined.** First Schedule Part 2 defines "adverse drug event history", "cardiac report", "dispensed medication", "medical device", "medication", "ordered (prescribed) medication", "referral memorandum", "surgical procedure notes", "Table of Surgical Procedures" and "visit event" — **and does not define "laboratory test reports"**; the whole of Part 2 was read in the gazetted PDF to confirm it. **If a genomic result is part of a laboratory test report, then from September 2027 NPHL is under a statutory duty to push sequence-derived results into the national health record, with the HIA's access, prohibited-use and opt-out machinery attaching. If it is not, the sequence stays entirely outside the Act.** It is resolvable by regulation under **HIA s 3(1)** or by amendment of the First Schedule under **s 111**, and nothing published resolves it. See §4.21.6 — **this is the same failure mode as the undefined "medical record"**.

*(Caveat, recorded because it is fair: the Order **presupposes rather than confers** the licence, and CDA's licence record on `elis.moh.gov.sg` was not independently retrieved. But an exemption Order made by the Minister under HCSA s 53, calling the body "the licensee" and referring to "the provision of a clinical laboratory service", is strong primary evidence.)*

### 4.10.9 ✅ The National Infectious Diseases Biorepository — the corpus's open question, answered

**This was recorded as an open question** — whether the NIDB rests on IDA s 59A or on HBRA tissue banking — **and it could not be answered as posed, because the framing is a false binary.** ⚠️ *(The NCID page at `nhghealth.com.sg` returns **HTTP 429** behind a Vercel security checkpoint and has done so to every agent that tried it, most recently on 18 August 2026. **The block is real and persistent — do not send anyone at it again.**)*

**The answer comes from a better source: CDA has published an NIDB Framework on its own site**, at `cda.gov.sg/professionals/research/cda-research-laboratory/`, **page last updated 31 March 2026**. **P**

**What NIDB is:** "a national resource for the systematic preservation of **microbial specimens and associated materials** to support public health efforts, outbreak preparedness and infectious disease research" — custodian of a national reference collection of bacteria, viruses and fungi of public health importance "**and associated clinical specimens**".

**The Framework is a composite, not a single gateway.** It states that "[a]ll collections will be subject to the applicable Singapore / international laws and ethical frameworks" and then names them: the **HBRA**; **Human Biomedical Research (Tissue Banking)**; the **PDPA**; the **PSGA**; the **IDA**; the **BATA**; "ethics approval by the relevant institutional review board(s)"; and "**inter-institutional agreements**, as applicable".

⭐ **And the two-collection split is the operationally decisive part — it is what makes the binary framing unanswerable:**

| Collection | Composition | Oversight |
|---|---|---|
| **Public health collection** | "[s]amples collected under the Infectious Diseases Act" | **Public Health Oversight Committee** — *not* an IRB |
| **Clinical / research collection** | Samples deposited from clinical and research networks, for use by the general clinical and scientific community "**in Singapore and abroad**" | **NIDB oversight committee** |

**Read against the statutes, the split is coherent.** Material collected under the **IDA** — including under s 59A(3), and including material the Director-General already holds and repurposes under s 59A(3)(c) — is, if it is s 59A national public health research, **outside the HBRA altogether** by **HBRA s 3(4) and Second Schedule para 3**; and **HBRA ss 34–37** are separately disapplied for tissue banking conducted **solely** for s 59A research by **S 704/2019 reg 2(1)(a)**. Material **deposited from clinical and research networks** is not IDA material and therefore stays within the HBRA and its tissue-banking regime, with IRB approval and inter-institutional agreements.

**Three limits, recorded rather than glossed:**

1. **It is a webpage, not a published document.** **No terms of reference, membership, criteria, application process or decision record is published for either committee**, and neither appears anywhere in the e-Gazette index ("biorepository" — **0 hits**). Added to the register at §6.6.
2. 🔴 **It says the clinical/research collection is available "abroad" and says nothing about how** — no transfer conditions, no material transfer agreement template, no country restrictions. See §4.18.1.
3. **It cites the PDPA without qualification**, although **CDA is itself a public agency** for which Parts 3–6B impose no obligation. The citation is presumably directed at depositors and recipients who are organisations rather than at CDA itself, **but the page does not say so.**

---

## 4.10A INTERNATIONAL HEALTH INSTRUMENTS

### 4.10A.1 The International Health Regulations (2005) and the 2024 amendments — Singapore accepted, by silence

**Singapore ACCEPTED the 2024 IHR amendments.** WHO's consolidated IHR text (explanatory note dated 19 June 2026), **Appendix 1**, gives Singapore's row as: `15 June 2007 | 11 July 2016 | 31 May 2024 | 19 September 2025 | 2014, 2022, 2024`. **There is no rejection, no reservation, no declaration and no statement from Singapore.**

**How acceptance happened: tacitly.** The rejection window under **Art 59.1 bis** ran ten months from the Director-General's notification of 19 September 2024 and closed on **19 July 2025**. Twelve States Parties rejected the amendments; **Singapore is not among them.**

⚠️ **Preserve a nuance rather than picking a number.** WHO's own news item of 19 September 2025 records **eleven** rejections; the consolidated text supports **twelve**. The two are reconcilable, because four States had until **19 March 2026** to act. Report both figures with their dates.

### 4.10A.2 ⚠️ The WHO Pandemic Agreement is not open for signature

**Nobody has signed it, because it cannot be signed.** **Art 31.2** gates signature on the adoption of the **Art 12 annex on Pathogen Access and Benefit-Sharing**, and **Decision WHA79(7) of 22 May 2026** extended the Intergovernmental Working Group's mandate to the World Health Assembly in 2027, "or … earlier by a special session … in 2026".

**Do not frame Singapore's non-signature as a Singapore choice.** It is not a choice; the instrument is not available to be signed.

### 4.10A.3 ⭐ Singapore implements the IHR almost entirely at executive level

This is the pattern in §2.2 appearing in the international layer, and it is verified by exhaustive search:

- The **Communicable Diseases Agency Act 2025**, the **Infectious Diseases (Amendment) Act 2024** and the **2021 Revised Edition of the Infectious Diseases Act** contain **zero** occurrences of "International Health Regulations" and **zero** of "Focal Point".
- A full-text sweep of the **entire e-Gazette index** for "International Health Regulations" returns **one hit, ever** — **S 492/2007**, a ship sanitation certificate form.
- **The only statutory gateway is IDA s 57A(1)(b)**, and it is a **power, not a duty**: it permits disclosure to the World Health Organization "if … necessary to comply with an international obligation of Singapore".
- The **CDA's National IHR Focal Point role rests on a ministerial statement**, not on any instrument: 14th Parliament, Second Session, vol 95, sitting no 148, **7 January 2025**.

**Open question:** the new **Art 4.1 National IHR Authority appears to be undesignated**, and **Art 4.2 bis** obliges States Parties to make the corresponding domestic adjustment. Nothing has been located.

📌 **Pathogen genomic data was formerly recorded here as an unfilled gap. It is now researched — see §4.10A.4.**


### 4.10A.4 ⭐ Pathogen genomic data — Singapore funds and hosts the world's largest platform, and no Singapore instrument mentions it

**Singapore hosts the GISAID Data Science Centre at A\*STAR's Bioinformatics Institute.** The arrangement is older and larger than the Minister's WHA79 statement disclosed — and every term of it comes from **GISAID's own website, not from any Singapore source**.

| Element | What is established | Source |
|---|---|---|
| **Origin** | A\*STAR and its Bioinformatics Institute "entered in **2014** into a long-term cooperation agreement with GISAID", scoped to the **FluSurver** (EpiFlu™) and **CoVsurver** (EpiCoV™) tools — i.e. software integration, not hosting | GISAID Public-Private Partnerships page · **P** |
| **Current commitment** | Under the heading "**Singapore (2025 - 2030)**": A\*STAR "has extended its long-term collaboration with the Initiative by allocating **$15 million SGD** of aggregate sourced funding over the next 5-years to fund the **GISAID Data Science Centre at A\*STAR's Bioinformatics Institute**" | GISAID Grants and Donations page · **P** |
| **What the Centre actually does** | **Nine of roughly fifty named EpiCoV curators are attributed to BII**, with two more to the Genome Institute of Singapore — the largest single-country contingent, alongside Institut Pasteur, Fiocruz and the Francis Crick Institute. Curation is active, not passive hosting: curators tag anomalous sequences and alert contributors before release. ⭐ **So A\*STAR staff review and annotate genomic submissions originating in other countries, in Singapore, before those submissions become visible to the world** | GISAID Data Curation page · **P** |
| **Governance link** | The **Director of the National Public Health Laboratory** is one of two named **Vice-Chairs of GISAID's Scientific Advisory Council** | GISAID Governance page · **P** |
| **The counterparty** | **Freunde von GISAID e.V.**, Munich; District Court Munich, Association Register **VR204844**; board constituted under § 26 BGB. **A German registered association — not an international organisation and not a treaty body** | GISAID Imprint · **P** |

⚠️ **Do not conflate Singapore's role with Germany's.** Germany is "the official hosting nation of the GISAID Initiative through a cooperation agreement", with technical hosting at its Federal Office for Agriculture and Food. **Singapore hosts a data science centre.** These are different arrangements.

🔴 **No agreement text is public.** The legal form of the arrangement (contract, grant, MOU or research collaboration agreement), whether a separate 2025 instrument exists or the 2014 one was amended, the Centre's terms of reference and governance, who employs its staff, who is data controller for curated third-country submissions, and what "aggregate sourced funding" means are **all unestablished**. Added to the register at §6.6.

📌 **And note where the documentation sits.** A\*STAR's own website does not surface the Centre at all — the full A\*STAR sitemap (4,077 URLs) contains no GISAID page, and `a-star.edu.sg/bii` returns zero occurrences of "GISAID" in rendered text or raw HTML. **The Centre's existence, funding and terms are documented on the German association's website and in a Singapore ministerial speech, and nowhere on the website of the Singapore agency that hosts it.** That is itself a finding.

**⭐ NO SINGAPORE LAW GOVERNS IT — established, not assumed.**

- **"GISAID" returns zero hits in the entire e-Gazette full-text index**, with controls confirming the method works: "International Health Regulations" returns 1 (S 492/2007, matching §4.10A.3); "genomic" returns 11, **none in a pathogen context**; "pathogen" returns 11, all in plant-health, food-safety and assisted-reproduction or cord-blood contexts; "Bioinformatics Institute" returns 3, **all three being National Day Honours and COVID-19 award notices**. "pathogen genomic", "genomic surveillance", "genome sequence", "whole genome" and "biorepository" each return **zero**.
- **"GISAID" and "PathGen" return zero hits across all 79 Hansard sittings from 1 January 2024 to 15 August 2026.** Neither has ever been named in the Singapore Parliament in that window.
- **The PDPA does not reach it.** **A\*STAR is a public agency** — **S 149/2013, Schedule item 2**, made under **PDPA s 2(2)** — so **PDPA s 4(1)(c)** removes Parts 3, 4, 5, 6, 6A and 6B in their entirety. ⭐ **The same exclusion-not-gateway point as §4.15.4 applies with equal force: s 4(1)(c) removes obligations and confers no authority to do anything.** What binds instead is **PSGA ss 7 and 8** (unauthorised disclosure; unauthorised re-identification), the **Government Data Security Policies / IM8**, and the A\*STAR Act 1990. **If a pathogen sequence held at BII were personal data, the PDPA would still not regulate BII's handling of it.**
- ⚠️ **A caveat recorded rather than smoothed over.** PSGA s 8 and the IM8 regime are directed at **Singapore-agency** data. **The Centre curates third-country submissions.** Whether Singapore's public-sector data regime is understood by A\*STAR to attach to data that is neither about Singaporeans nor collected by a Singapore agency is a question **no retrieved source addresses** — and it is a real one, because the PSGA is not a data-protection statute but an inter-agency sharing and offence statute keyed to information "under an agency's control".
- **The HBRA does not reach the sequence work, and the reason is textual.** HBRA s 2 defines "human biological material" as material obtained from the human body "**that consists of, or includes, human cells**". **A consensus viral genome is a string of bases, and a purified viral or bacterial isolate contains no human cells** — so s 3(2)(e) is not engaged by pathogen sequence work as such. ⭐ **The HBRA reaches pathogen genomics through the linked clinical data and through the original specimen. It never reaches the sequence.**

**⭐ THE GOVERNING INSTRUMENT IS A FOREIGN-LAW CONTRACT, AND ITS CHOICE-OF-LAW CLAUSE IS ASYMMETRIC.** What actually governs is the **GISAID EpiFlu™ Database Access Agreement** — a contract of adhesion accepted by every submitter and every downloader, and the whole of GISAID's legal architecture. Its governing-law provision splits:

> **Disputes involving a Data *provider* are governed by that provider's local law. Disputes involving a Data *user* go to ICC arbitration, seated in the Federal Republic of Germany, under German law, in English, with the tribunal deciding *ex aequo et bono* "without regard to the laws of any specific jurisdiction".**

⭐ **The consequence for Singapore, stated precisely: Singapore law survives on the submission side and is displaced on the download side.** A dispute about misuse of a Singapore-originated sequence is governed by Singapore law. A dispute in which a Singapore researcher is the *user* is arbitrated in Germany on equitable principles. **This is a live health-adjacent data flow, funded by a Singapore statutory board, whose operative instrument is a foreign-law contract that no Singapore instrument references.** Fuller treatment of the agreement in `BUNDLE-ADJACENT` §9.11.

⚠️ **Two limits on the published agreement, both worth carrying.** It is headed "EpiFlu™" and dated **16 March 2011**, and its recital confines "Data" to influenza — yet it is the agreement GISAID links from every page, including those describing EpiCoV™, EpiRSV™, EpiPox™ and EpiArbo, and **no separately published EpiCoV or EpiArbo agreement was located**. So **a user of GISAID's COVID-19 data cannot read, on the open web, the agreement that governs their use of it.** And it is **unilaterally amendable**: publication of a modification on the website "shall constitute adequate written notice", and continued use is acceptance.

📌 **The privacy architecture has a gap that should be stated plainly.** The agreement's "Privacy" clause, and the GISAID Privacy Notice (updated 22 May 2018), are **exclusively about the registrant's contact information** — name, employer, work address, email. **There is nothing in either document about the privacy of the individuals from whom the specimens were obtained. The word "patient" does not appear in the agreement**, although its recital expressly contemplates that "virological, clinical, epidemiological and demographic information (if available)" travels with the sequence. **It is a platform whose published privacy governance addresses the scientist and not the subject.**

**⭐ WHEN DOES A PATHOGEN GENOME BECOME PERSONAL DATA? Singapore supplies a method and has never applied it to pathogens.**

**The only usable Singapore test is the PDPC's relative-identifiability method** in its Practical Guidance to Singapore General Hospital of **7 July 2026** (§4.3.4): assess each holder separately, by reference to what **that** party "has or is likely to have access to". It is directly transferable. Applying it: a Singapore pathogen genome **is** personal data in CDA/NPHL's hands — NPHL sequenced every confirmed COVID-19 case and uses whole-genome sequencing to link individual cases to clusters — and, on the same reasoning, generally **is not** personal data in a downstream GISAID user's hands. **The same relative test appears in the HBRA's "individually-identifiable" definition**, so the regimes are at least coherent with one another.

🔴 **But the anterior question is unanswered by anything.** **Whether a virus genome is "data … about an individual" at all** — as opposed to data about a non-human organism — **is not addressed by the PDPA, by any PDPC guidance, or by any Singapore judgment.** Do not assert an answer. And note that the SGH guidance's **para 12 conditionality is doing more work than it appears** for genomic data, whose identifiability **grows as reference databases grow**.

**✅ A clean verified negative, and it matters.** **Singapore is neither a Party nor a signatory to the Nagoya Protocol** on Access to Genetic Resources and the Fair and Equitable Sharing of Benefits — verified against the CBD Secretariat's own list (142 Parties, 143 ratifications, 92 signatures; **Singapore appears in neither the Parties list nor the signed-but-not-ratified list**, unlike Brunei, Indonesia, Malaysia, the Philippines, Thailand and Viet Nam). ⭐ **Pair it with §4.10A.2: the PABS annex is unadopted, and Pandemic Agreement Art 12.4 expressly preserves that "nothing in this paragraph creates obligations under these instruments for non-Parties thereto".** So **nothing binds Singapore on pathogen access and benefit-sharing**, and the PABS annex, when it comes, will be the **first** such instrument to reach Singapore's pathogen holdings. *(Caveat: absence was verified for the Nagoya Protocol only. **No claim is made about the parent Convention on Biological Diversity.**)*

**The nearest things to benefit-sharing arrangements touching Singapore pathogen data are all non-statutory:** GISAID's own acknowledgement-and-collaboration model, which GISAID describes as answering governments' concern to "secure access to new vaccines or medicines" — **a contractual ABS regime operating where the treaty regime does not**; **PathGen's** announced-but-non-existent country arrangements; **WHO GLASS**, to which Singapore has contributed AMR data from sentinel healthcare facilities since **2019** through AMRCO as National Coordinating Centre, **with no Singapore instrument behind it**; and NPHL's network memberships, of which **WHO GISRS** is the most consequential.

📌 **PathGen, stated at its true weight.** A platform of the **Asia Pathogen Genomics Initiative**, led by the **Duke-NUS Centre for Outbreak Preparedness**, public preview **1 December 2025**, described as "federated" and "sovereign-by-design" and sharing "only the analytics". **It is a proof of concept**, with pilots from early 2026 and staged roll-out through 2027. ⚠️ **No data access policy, data use agreement, privacy notice or terms of use has ever been published** — its About page states five values ("Transparency", "Collaboration", "Sovereignty", "Federation", "Innovation"), **which is a statement of values, not a governance instrument**. Whether Singapore's own pathogen data is or will be inside any pilot is **not established in either direction**. *(Provenance disclosed: the Duke-NUS media releases for PathGen and for ADWANCE-Asia were read via **EurekAlert!** and **PR Newswire** respectively, because `duke-nus.edu.sg` sits behind an Imperva/Incapsula WAF returning a challenge to non-browser clients. They are the issuing institution's own releases, routed through wire services; a verifier with browser tools should re-read them on the Duke-NUS host.)*

⚠️ **A naming clash to avoid in any index or table of contents.** "**Genetic information**" in §4.13 and in the live MOH consultation (§3C.1) means **human** genetic information in insurance and employment. **It has nothing to do with pathogen genomic data.** Do not let the two run together.

**⭐ THE BOUNDARY ANSWER — use this formulation.**

> *"Pathogen genomic data is not classified by any Singapore health-data instrument. Where it is held together with identifiable patient or epidemiological data it falls within the PDPA (for organisations), the HBRA (where used in human biomedical research), and potentially the Health Information Act 2026 (where it forms part of a laboratory test report contributed to the national electronic records system). Standing alone and severed from linkage, its status is unresolved. The controls that apply are those attaching to the specimen, to the linked record, and to the holder — not to the sequence."*

**The supporting negatives, all from full-text search of the enacted texts:** the **Health Information Act 2026** contains **zero** occurrences of "genom", "pathogen" or "microb"; the **CDA Act 2025** contains **zero** occurrences of "genom", "sequenc", "laborator", "specimen", "sample", "biological" or "pathogen"; and the **Biological Agents and Toxins Act 2005** — Singapore's pathogen-material statute — contains **zero** occurrences of "genetic", "nucleic" or "sequence", defining "biological agent" by reference to micro-organisms and infectious substances only.

🔴 **State the consequence, because it is the sharpest line available in this domain: Singapore has a complete regime for moving a *vial* across a border and no regime at all for moving the *sequence* of what is in it.**

### 4.11.1 The consolidated incident-reporting picture — three channels, three deadlines, one of them with no form

A health data holder that is also a CII owner faces **three concurrent notification channels**:

| Channel | Instrument | Initial deadline | Follow-up |
|---|---|---|---|
| **CSA** (CII owners) | Cybersecurity (CII) Regulations 2018 **reg 5(1)(a)** | **within 2 hours** after becoming aware of the occurrence | **reg 5(1)(b): supplementary details within 14 days**; and per S 678/2025, a supplementary report within 72 hours of awareness and a final report within 30 days |
| **PDPC** | PDPA Part 6A and S 64/2021 | the statutory notification timeline | — |
| **MOH** | CS/DS Essentials (non-binding) and HIA Implementation Guide v2.0, Annex E, Table 12 | **within 2 hours of confirmation** | **detailed report within 14 days** |

📌 **The 2-hour CII deadline is now confirmed from the regulation itself** — reg 5(1)(a) of S 519/2018 — rather than from a press release, and the **14-day supplementary report under reg 5(1)(b) is half the obligation** and is frequently omitted.

⚠️ **The MOH channel has no form.** The Implementation Guide states, in place of a reporting link, that the link "will be added here in 2027, when the incident reporting framework is launched". **So the duty MOH describes exists on paper with nothing to file it on**, and it will not be a legal duty at all until the Act commences and the s 75 and s 79 regulations prescribe the periods (§4.1.5).

⚠️ **An unresolved question that must not go into the bundle as a finding.** It has been suggested that Health Information Act **ss 75(6), 79(5) and 64(4)** make HIA and PDPA duties **concurrent**, with no PDPA disapplication clause — even though the 2023 consultation stated the Act would supersede the PDPA. **That analysis was performed on the Bill, not the enacted Act, and the verification against Act 1 of 2026 was assigned and never delivered.** If it holds, regulated parties face two concurrent regimes where they were told they would face one. **It is stated here as an open question and should not be advanced as a finding until verified.** See §7.

### 4.11.2 The health-sector incidents that produced the instruments

| Incident | Date | What it produced |
|---|---|---|
| **SingHealth cyber attack** | 27 June 2018 | The Committee of Inquiry report (10 Jan 2019); Government acceptance of all findings and recommendations (15 Jan 2019); the MOH CISO office as healthcare sector lead; database activity monitoring; **and the continued deferment of mandatory NEHR contribution** |
| **HIV Registry leak** | disclosed 28 January 2019 | 14,200 diagnosed individuals plus 2,400 contacts. Ministerial Statement 12 February 2019. The origin of MOH's registry download controls |
| **HSA blood-donor exposure** | disclosed 1 April 2019 | More than 800,000 donors, via vendor Secur Solutions Group. Produced the HSA Board Committee and **the convening of the PSDSRC** |
| **Eye & Retina Surgeons ransomware** | 25 August 2021 | The first MOH public statement on a **private** licensee's breach; more than 73,000 patients; announced the **Healthcare Cybersecurity Essentials** |
| **DDoS on public healthcare institutions** | 1 November 2023 | 9.20am–4.30pm outage across all three clusters; **no data compromise**; CSA investigation |
| **Academy of Medicine ransomware** | 2023 | [2024] SGPDPCS 4; 6,574 individuals; unpatched FortiOS; RDP entry |

**The only published official statistic on threat volume** is from a written Parliamentary answer of 22 November 2023: Synapxe blocks about **3,000 malicious emails a day and 1.7 million firewall bypass attempts a month**.

### 4.11.3 ✓ Verified negatives in this domain

- **The CCoP for CII 2026 contains zero occurrences of "health".** The binding code that governs healthcare CII owners does not mention healthcare.
- **No Standard of Performance is currently in force** under the Cybersecurity Act.
- **No subsidiary legislation was made under the Cybersecurity Act in the health sector specifically.** The 2025–2026 cybersecurity subsidiary legislation is sector-neutral.
- **Parts 3A and 3B are in force without a civil-penalty alternative to prosecution**, because s 20 of Act 19 of 2024 is uncommenced (§3D.1).
- **CSA published no press release, publication, consultation or code of practice between 1 and 15 August 2026.** Its latest press release is 22 July 2026; its activity in the window was alerts and advisories only.
