### 4.12.1 📌 S 66/2026 — what the Order actually says, and four things it does NOT say

**Exact title:** Health Products (AI Standalone Mobile Application — Exemption) Order 2026 | **Made:** 5 February 2026 by the **Health Sciences Authority** under Health Products Act s 70, signed Benjamin Ong, Chairperson | **First published:** 12 February 2026 at 5 pm | **In operation:** 13 February 2026 | **Length:** 5 pages | **Reference:** `[401:04/04-000; AG/LEGIS/SL/122D/2025/1]` | **Source:** e-Gazette Subsidiary Legislation Supplement — **P**

**What it does.** It creates the AI-medical-device exemption sandbox: it exempts **Class A and Class B AI standalone mobile applications developed by a "public healthcare entity"** from the manufacturer's licensing requirement (Health Products Act s 12(1)) and the product registration requirement (s 15(1)).

**"Public healthcare entity" is defined precisely** and is worth quoting because it settles a naming question elsewhere in this bundle: a public healthcare institution; a cluster headquarters — **National Healthcare Group Pte Ltd; National University Health System Pte. Ltd.; Singapore Health Services Pte Ltd**; **Synapxe Pte. Ltd.**; or **MOH Office for Healthcare Transformation Pte. Ltd.** 📌 Note the statutory form **"Singapore Health Services Pte Ltd"**, which settles the SingHealth entity-name question in §4.4.3 on 2026 drafting, not merely on the 2015 notification.

**The conditions in the Order:**
1. the device is **Class A or Class B** under the Third Schedule to the Health Products (Medical Devices) Regulations 2010;
2. manufacture is **supervised by a clinician employed in a public healthcare institution holding the position of consultant or higher**;
3. **notification to the Authority (HSA)** — paras 3(d) and 4(3)(d);
4. **endorsement by the chairman of the medical board or the chief executive officer** of the institution (para 4(3)(e)); where the entity is the Health Promotion Board, the Chief Executive of HPB.

**⛔ Four widely circulated conditions are NOT in the Order.** They came from law-firm alerts and were checked against the gazetted text:

| Claimed condition | Verdict |
|---|---|
| **ISO 13485 self-attestation** | ❌ **NOT IN THE ORDER.** "ISO" appears 0 times; "13485" 0 times; "attest" 0 times |
| **Patient notification** — that patients must be told when a sandbox AI application is used in their care | ❌ **NOT IN THE ORDER, and materially misleading.** "Patient" appears **0 times**. The Order's only notification duties run **to HSA**, not to patients |
| **$20,000 fine / 12 months' imprisonment** | ❌ **NOT IN THE ORDER.** "20,000" appears 0 times; "penalty" 0 times; "offence" 0 times. **It is an exemption Order; it creates no offence.** Any penalty would arise under Health Products Act s 12(1) or s 15(1) themselves, which the Order disapplies in the stated circumstances |
| **Annual attestation** | ❌ **NOT IN THE ORDER.** "Annual" appears 0 times |

**This is a substantive correction, not a citation slip.** Anyone advising a public healthcare entity on the sandbox's conditions on the basis of the law-firm account would be advising on obligations that do not exist.

**What is true:** the exemption relaxes *device licensing and registration*, not health-data governance. The internal governance controls for public healthcare institutions, including cyber and data security requirements, continue to apply.

**Pipeline:** the 12-month monitoring period ends around **February 2027** (§3F item 61).

### 4.12.2 AIHGle 2.0 — voluntary, and it looks mandatory

**It is not a licence condition.** It was circulated to all HCSA licensees and all healthcare professionals by **MOH Circular MOH-MHC-0024-2026** of 13 March 2026, which is what makes it look mandatory. The circular itself states that AIHGle "is a living document that will be periodically updated"; a feedback channel is open.

**What it covers on data:** training and test dataset independence; PDPA compliance for training data; privacy-enhancing technologies; synthetic data (directing developers to PDPC's Guide on Synthetic Data Generation while requiring that **real-world data be prioritised** for training and evaluation); continuous-learning data disclosure risk; generative AI data disclosure; and permissible input data types.

**🔥 The disclosure inside it that the client will want:** AIHGle 2.0 records that **Synapxe trained a deployed model on approximately 3.6 million derived NEHR records covering 2015–2021**. That is a concrete instance of national health record data being used for AI training, disclosed in a government instrument.

**Table 6 of AIHGle 2.0 lists every instrument MOH and HSA consider relevant to AI in healthcare.** It names **five statutes** — the PDPA 2012, the HCSA 2020, the professional Acts, the Civil Law (Amendment) Act 2020 s 37 and the Health Products Act 2007 — plus the Health Products (Medical Devices) Regulations 2010 and the HBRA 2015. **No AI statute appears, because there is none.**

### 4.12.3 ⚠️ Two reporting regimes that contradict each other on patient identifiers

- **Drug and CTGTP pharmacovigilance** (CIOMS I form, per HSA's Post-marketing Vigilance guidance v6) **requires an identifiable patient** — name, date of birth or patient ID number all qualify.
- **Device adverse-event reporting** (Form MDAR1, §VII) **forbids patient identifiers.**

**The same incident involving a drug-device combination product is therefore subject to opposite instructions.** This is a genuine, citable conflict on the face of two HSA instruments and the client should see it.

### 4.12.4 ✓ VERIFIED NEGATIVE — there is no Singapore AI statute

**No AI Act or AI Bill has been introduced, announced or consulted on.** This was checked specifically:

- AIHGle 2.0's own Table 6 (§4.12.2) lists five governing statutes, none AI-specific.
- MDDI's press release of 22 January 2026 launching the Model AI Governance Framework for Agentic AI contains no reference to legislation or regulatory mandate.
- PDPC's own position is that the PDPA is "broad-based legislation" applying to all collection and use of personal data, "including the collection and/or processing of personal data to develop, test and monitor AI Systems" — i.e. **AI is brought inside the existing statute, not given its own.**
- The complete Bills Supplement for 2026 (B 1–B 18, to 4 August 2026) contains no AI Bill.

**Nothing is in the pipeline to track.** Re-verify at each Parliament sitting and each Committee of Supply.

**Two further negatives worth stating:**
- **Continuous learning and generative AI sit outside the streamlined change pathway.** GN-37-R1 §1.3 carries an express carve-out. Those devices must instead go through the GL-04-R4 Change Notification route, and AIHGle 2.0 directs generative-AI device developers to a **pre-market consultation** rather than to any published pathway. **This is a live regulatory gap, not a finished framework.**
- **EHR software with no processing or analysis is not a medical device at all** (GL-07-R2) — a scope boundary that determines whether any of this applies.

---

### 4.13.1 The moratorium — two editions, and both are operative

📌 **Both editions are in force, for different books of business, and any statement that "the moratorium dates from 2021" or "from 2025" alone is wrong.**

| | **2021 Moratorium** | **2025 Moratorium** |
|---|---|---|
| Exact title | "Ministry of Health (MOH) – Life Insurance Association (LIA) Moratorium on Genetic Testing and Insurance" | "Amended and Restated Moratorium on Genetic Testing and Insurance Between The Ministry of Health ('MOH') And Life Insurance Association ('LIA')" |
| Entered into | **27 January 2021** | not stated on its face; MOH describes it as "amended and re-signed in 2025" |
| Commencement | para 11: commences 27 January 2021 | para 11: takes effect **30 June 2025** |
| When the core prohibitions bit | **27 October 2021** — the predictive-test prohibitions applied only "on and from the date which is nine months after the Commencement Date" | 30 June 2025, no transition |
| Policies covered | signed 27 October 2021 to 29 June 2025 | signed on or after 30 June 2025 |
| Still operative? | **Yes, residually.** 2025 para 11: "the 2021 Moratorium shall continue to apply in respect of insurance policies that were signed or entered into prior to the Commencement Date" | Yes — the current operative version |

⚠️ **The 27 January versus 27 October 2021 date trap.** They are not in conflict. The 2021 Moratorium *commenced* 27 January 2021, but its predictive-genetic-test prohibitions were deferred nine months, to 27 October 2021 — which is why MOH's page keys the 2021 edition to policies signed "from 27 October 2021". **Use 27 January 2021 for "entered into and commenced" and 27 October 2021 for "from when it bit".**

**Legal character — the most important characterisation.** It is a **voluntary, non-statutory bilateral instrument between MOH and an industry association**. Not legislation, not subsidiary legislation, not a MAS notice, and not issued under any statutory power. **Its only enforcement mechanism is associational: compliance is a condition of LIA membership** (2025 para 10), backed by annual member compliance reports to LIA, an annual consolidated report from LIA to MOH, and complaint reporting to MOH. **There is no statutory penalty, no regulatory sanction and no individual right of action.**

⚠️ **A definitional asymmetry with a real consequence.** Para 1(m) defines "Insurer" by reference to carrying on life business under the Insurance Act, but the instrument binds only through **LIA membership**. **A licensed insurer that is not an LIA member is not bound.** That is precisely the gap the proposed Bill closes by applying to all MAS-licensed insurers.

### 4.13.2 What the moratorium does and does not do

**The core architecture is the diagnostic / predictive split** (recital C):

- **Diagnostic genetic tests** (para 1(g): a test "that confirms or rules out a diagnosis based on existing symptoms, signs or abnormal non-genetic test results") — **insurers MAY access and use these**, subject only to paras 2, 3 and 8.
- **Predictive genetic tests** (para 1(s): a test "that predicts a future risk of disease in individuals without symptoms or signs of a genetic disorder") — **prohibited**, subject to the para 4(b) financial-limit exception.
- **Direct-to-consumer and other non-clinical tests** — **absolutely prohibited** (para 7), even where para 4(b) is satisfied.

**Paragraph by paragraph:**

| Para | Effect |
|---|---|
| **2** | Insurers must not require or pressure an individual to undertake **any** genetic test to obtain cover, and must not **request** a **Resident** Individual to take a predictive test |
| **3** | Insurers must not request or use the result of **any** genetic test conducted in the context of **biomedical research**, "regardless of where or when the research was conducted or the nature of the research", even where para 4(b) is satisfied. **This is the provision protecting participants in PRECISE and SG100K-type research** |
| **4(a)** | No requesting or using predictive results in underwriting a Resident Individual, including tests "previously taken or which may be taken in the future" |
| **5** | Carve-back: predictive results must not be used at all — even above the limit — where the cover is **health insurance, an Integrated Shield Plan or any type not listed in Table 1**; or the test was taken after cover started (unless consented to before); or the test is **of another individual, "such as a blood relative"** |
| **6** | Where a predictive result reaches the insurer "whether accidentally, voluntarily or otherwise", it may be used **only if to the individual's benefit** |
| **7** | Absolute prohibition on direct-to-consumer results |
| **8** | **NEW in 2025.** No requesting or using the result of **any** genetic test for familial hypercholesterolaemia — diagnostic or predictive — where the individual was enrolled with an MOH-approved facility under the National FH Genetic Testing Programme and the test was of the **LDLR, APOB or PCSK9** gene |
| **9** | Each insurer must tell each individual, before the application completes, what must and need not be disclosed |

**The exception — para 4(b) and Table 1.** All three conditions must be met: an Approved Insurance Type; the **aggregate** of payouts under the applied-for policy and all cumulative insurances of the same type from **all insurers in Singapore** exceeds the Approved Financial Limit; and the test is an Approved Predictive Genetic Test.

| Approved Insurance Type | Approved Financial Limit (aggregated per life) |
|---|---|
| Life Insurance | higher of **S$2,000,000** or the 99th-percentile sum assured at the time of underwriting |
| Total Permanent Disability Insurance | higher of **S$2,000,000** or the 99th percentile |
| Long-term Care Insurance, Type A (inability to perform **2 or more** activities of daily living for at least 6 months) | higher of **S$3,000 monthly benefit** or the 99th percentile |
| Long-term Care Insurance, Type B (inability to perform **1** activity of daily living) | higher of **S$3,000 monthly benefit** or the 99th percentile |
| Critical Illness Insurance | higher of **S$500,000** or the 99th percentile |
| Disability Income Insurance | higher of **S$10,000 monthly benefit** or the 99th percentile |

**The limits are identical in both editions.** The only textual change is "and" to "or" in the Table 1 formula; substantively the same test.

**Approved Predictive Genetic Tests:** only the **HTT gene test (Huntington's disease)** and the **BRCA1 and BRCA2 gene tests (breast cancer)**. ⚠️ **Table 1's third column uses merged cells that flatten in the PDF text layer, so the per-row mapping of test to insurance type cannot be stated reliably. Do not assert one.** The safe statement is: only Huntington's and breast cancer predictive tests are ever available to insurers, only above the limits above, and only for the six listed policy types.

**What it does NOT do:**
- **It does not apply after acceptance.** Per a written Parliamentary answer of 24 February 2026, once a policyholder has been accepted the moratorium does not apply, so medical history and diagnoses may be submitted in support of claims and used in claim assessment, with no secondary underwriting.
- **It does not touch employment at all.**
- **It does not restrict diagnoses or family history.**
- **It does not bind non-LIA insurers, general insurers outside LIA, or reinsurers outside LIA.**
- **It creates no offence and no remedy.**

**What changed between the editions:** FH protection added (new para 8); the nine-month transition removed; direct-to-consumer tests promoted to a standalone paragraph with a "notwithstanding para 4(b)" override; recital C added, formally splitting clinical from non-clinical tests; terminology simplified; and para 11 gained both the self-termination-on-legislation clause and the back-book savings clause. **Financial limits, approved tests, compliance mechanism and consumer-information duty are unchanged.**

### 4.13.3 The employment side — and what is missing from it

The picture on the employment side is the mirror image of the insurance side: **there is no restraint at all on an employer's use of health information**, and two regimes actively push it to them.

- **WSH (Medical Examinations) Regulations 2011 reg 9(1)** *requires* the designated workplace doctor to report medical examination results **to the employer**.
- **The MOM six-monthly medical examination regime** requires clinics to submit all results, and employers can view them.
- **Employment Act s 89** contemplates certification by "a medical practitioner not appointed by the employer" but imposes no confidentiality constraint.
- **The Workplace Fairness Act 2025 has no genetic protected characteristic** and is **uncommenced** in any event.
- **TAFEP guidance is voluntary** and addresses application forms, not use of information already held.
- **No agent covered employer-side health-data handling as a domain.** See §7.

**The Genetic Information Bill would be the first instrument to restrain employers** (§3C.1).

---

### Summary table

| 2 | **MediShield Life Scheme (Disclosure of Information) Regulations 2017** | Subsidiary legislation | **S 458/2017** | Made 11 Aug 2017; **in force 30 November 2017.** Prescribes the "public schemes" and "healthcare-related public schemes" for Part 5 disclosures — **all MOH-administered subsidy schemes, with an open catch-all** | P |
| 3 | MediShield Life Scheme (Private Medical Insurance Scheme) Regulations 2015 | Subsidiary legislation | **S 623/2015** | In force 1 Nov 2015. Constitutes the Integrated Shield Plan framework. ⚠️ **Contains no data provisions** | P |
| 4 | **MediShield Life Scheme (Amendment) Act 2024** | Act | **Act 40 of 2024** | **In force 1 April 2025** (S 230/2025). **s 9 added a SECOND purpose — debt recovery — to the s 28 means-information gateway, and deliberately confined the opt-out to the subsidy purpose only** | P |
| 5 | **CareShield Life and Long-Term Care Act 2019** | Act | CLLTCA 2019 | In force. **Part 8 (ss 31–34) "Handling of Restricted Information"** — health information from healthcare institutions and practitioners for eligibility and claims; **s 34(2) permits class-wide certifications**; **s 5(3) requires the Administrator to be "a person other than a public authority"** | P |
| 6 | CareShield Life and Long-Term Care (Amendment) Act 2025 | Act | **Act 18 of 2025** | In force **1 January 2026** (S 804/2025) | P |
| 7 | **MOH webpage listing approved schemes** | Agency webpage — **the only public trace of the certifications** | none | last updated **29 October 2024**. Names six approved schemes: **CHAS, Screen for Life, Home Caregiving Grant, IDAPE, ElderFund, CareShield Life** | P |
| 8 | Statutes (Miscellaneous Amendments) Act 2025, s 7 | Act | Act 19 of 2025 | **In force 5 December 2025.** Permits employers to deduct wages on a MediShield or CareShield recovery body's declaration | P |
| 9 | Statutory Bodies and Government Companies (Protection of Secrecy) Act 1983 (Amendment of Schedule) Notification 2026 | Subsidiary legislation | **S 120/2026** | In force **18 March 2026.** Adds the National Council of Social Service | P |
| 10 | National Council of Social Service (Amendment) Act 2026 | Act | **Act 3 of 2026** (Acts Supplement No. 6 of 2026) | Moves NCSS into **PSG Act First Schedule Part 1 as a Group 1A public body** with effect from **1 August 2026**. ⚠️ **Act 3 of 2026 is correct — do not "fix" it** (§1.4(a)) | P |
| 11 | MediShield Life Scheme (Amendment No. 2) Regulations 2026 | Subsidiary legislation | **S 542/2026** | In force 1 August 2026 (same day as item 10). Slaves the definition of "approved voluntary welfare organisation" to NCSS "sector member" status | P |
| 12 | Medical and Elderly Care Endowment Schemes Act 2000 (Medifund) instruments | Subsidiary legislation | S 19–20, S 82–83, S 223–224, S 308–309 of 2026 | In force. Medifund committees and approved institutions | P (index) |
| 13 | **Social Residential Homes Act 2025** | Act | **No. 14 of 2025** | **In force 1 July 2026** (S 449/2026). s 38 restriction on publishing information about residents; s 42 power to obtain information from a licensee | P |

### 4.14.1 🔴 The hard negative — the certifications that authorise population-scale health data flows have never been published

This was established exhaustively: **every gazette item ever made under both Acts was enumerated — 41 under the MediShield Life Scheme Act and 18 under the CareShield Life and Long-Term Care Act — and full-text phrase searches were run** (the phrase "certifies, under the Minister's hand" returns **0 hits**).

**No certification or approval under MediShield Life Scheme Act Part 5 or CareShield Life and Long-Term Care Act Part 8 has ever been published.** The only public trace anywhere is a bulleted list on an MOH webpage (last updated 29 October 2024) naming six approved schemes.

🔴 **And the detail that makes it bite: MSLA s 30(2) and CSLA s 34(2) expressly permit *class-wide* certifications.** So **a single unpublished certificate can authorise a population-scale data flow.** This is the same doctrinal mechanism as §2.2's Interpretation Act point, with the widest possible reach. See §6.

### 4.14.2 CareShield Life Part 8's onward-disclosure limbs have never been operative

**ss 31(2)(b), 31(2)(c) and 32(1) all turn on a "prescribed social or healthcare-related public scheme"** — a phrase that appears in the e-Gazette **only in the Act and Bill text**. Nothing has ever been prescribed.

⚠️ **Do not mistake S 869/2019 and S 851/2020 for the prescribing instruments.** They are made "For the purposes of section 39(1)(c)" — **Part 10, the Long-Term Care Support Fund** — a different thing entirely.

**Part 8 commenced 2 January 2020 and the frame is still empty, six and a half years later.** MediShield's equivalent went the other way: **S 458/2017** prescribes all MOH-administered subsidy schemes with an open catch-all.

### 4.14.3 ⭐ A refinement that strengthens the clusters analysis

**CareShield Life and Long-Term Care Act s 5(3) requires the Administrator to be "a person other than a public authority."**

So the body holding CareShield disability assessments — the Agency for Integrated Care Pte Ltd — sits **outside the Public Sector (Governance) Act by statutory command, not by accident of corporatisation.** That is materially stronger than saying these bodies happen to be companies, and §4.4.4 should be read with it.

### 4.14.4 A narrowed opt-out, and a health-financing fact routed to employers

- **MediShield Life Scheme (Amendment) Act 2024 (Act 40 of 2024) s 9**, in force 1 April 2025, added a **second purpose — debt recovery — to the s 28 means-information gateway and deliberately confined the opt-out to the subsidy purpose only.** A narrowed opt-out is squarely on the consent-architecture theme in §4.1.1.
- **Statutes (Miscellaneous Amendments) Act 2025 s 7**, in force 5 December 2025, lets **employers deduct wages on a MediShield or CareShield recovery body's declaration** — a health-financing fact routed to an employer. ⚠️ **This is unreconciled with Health Information Act s 38(5)**, which makes access or collection of accessible health information for an employment purpose the most heavily punished offence in that Act. The two have not been read together by anyone.

### 4.14.5 A coordinated package worth naming

Three instruments moved together on the National Council of Social Service: **S 120/2026** (statutory secrecy, 18 March 2026); **Act 3 of 2026** (PSG Act First Schedule, Group 1A, 1 August 2026); and **S 542/2026** (the same day, slaving the MediShield Life Regulations' "approved voluntary welfare organisation" definition to NCSS sector-member status). Read together they show the social-sector perimeter being built out in the same way the health perimeter was.

⚠️ **A partial disagreement to carry:** **Bill 17/2026 clauses 44 and 51 do have a data dimension**, notwithstanding a contrary reading elsewhere, because **MediShield Life Scheme Act s 21 and CareShield Life Act s 47 give investigators compulsion powers over claim records.**

⚠️ **A named gap:** the five MOH claims instruments — the **MediShield Life Claims Rules**, the **Table of Surgical Procedures Booklet**, the **MediSave and MediShield claims Manual**, and the relevant **MOH Finance Circulars** — sit behind the HPP professional login. **That is the layer specifying what clinical data actually leaves a hospital, and it is unretrieved.** See §7.

## 4.15 TRANSPLANT, BLOOD AND TISSUE

### Summary table

Re-identification of an infant donor on abnormal or incidental findings (reg 19); **linkage records (reg 20)**; cross-border transfer written agreements (reg 21(2)(f)); reg 22 two-way transplant information flow. ⚠️ **Correction: reg 20 no longer requires linkage records to be kept "indefinitely".** S 389/2023 para 10 **replaced reg 20 in its entirety** with effect from 26 June 2023 and **deleted the word "indefinitely"**; the replacement imposes confidentiality, anti-aggregation and safeguards duties but **no retention period at all** | P |

Donor-identification records and confidentiality (reg 31); **two-way donor-to-recipient information flow to the transplanting clinician (reg 25)**, expressly subject to other written law (reg 25(5)) | P |

### 4.15.1 🚩 MTERA has no register, no confidentiality provision and no offence provision — at all

All 17 sections were read. **The much-referenced "MTERA pledge register" is wholly administrative** — it is the Singpass-based Organ Donor Registry — and is protected **only** by the PDPA and public-sector rules.

**The only statutory register in this domain is HOTA's register of objections (s 9)**, and even that is protected merely by **s 9(2)** ("not open to inspection by the public"): **no gateway list, no disclosure offence.**

**HOTA s 28 is textually unchanged** — it is absent from the "Director → Director-General" replacement list in Act 11 of 2023, unlike s 9.

### 4.15.2 Blood-donor confidentiality is recent, and sits in HCSA regulations

The duty is **reg 20(3)–(6) of the Healthcare Services (Blood Banking Service) Regulations 2021, as inserted by S 388/2023 reg 12 with effect from 26 June 2023.**

📌 **Cite the amending instrument, always.** **Reg 20(3) is not in the 2021 principal Regulations.** As made, reg 20 had only paragraphs (1) and (2), and **S 1038/2021 contains zero instances of the word "confidential"**. A citation to "reg 20(3) of the Blood Banking Service Regulations 2021" without the amending instrument is incomplete, and **a careful reader will find nothing at the pinpoint.**

The duty itself is **unqualified**: the inserted text contains **no "except", no "unless authorised by law" and no carve-out of any kind** — which is what makes §4.15.4 bite. Penalty **$20,000 / 12 months** under reg 24, plus a continuing daily fine. **reg 20(4) extends confidentiality to extracts and aggregated compilations**, which is unusually broad and worth flagging.

⚠️ **And it is subject to CPC s 20(5)** (§2.5).

### 4.15.2A 🔴 The sharpest gap in this domain: donor records have no retention period and no disposal point

**There is no blood-banking-specific and no cord-blood-banking-specific Licence Condition in existence.** MOH's HCSA portal lists 9 Licence Conditions for blood banking and 7 for cord blood banking, and **every one is a cross-service instrument** that also applies to outpatient clinics and hospitals. **Only human tissue banking has service-specific Licence Conditions** — 6 of its 15.

**Retention for all three services therefore runs through a single generic instrument** — the **Licence Conditions on the Retention Periods of Patient Health Records (v1.0)**, 19 July 2022 (§4.2.2) — **which is drafted entirely around the *patient* and contains no category for donor records.**

**The result:** blood-bank donor records (Blood Banking Regulations reg 20), cord-blood linkage records (Cord Blood Banking Regulations reg 20, as replaced by S 389/2023) and tissue-bank donor-identification records (Human Tissue Banking Regulations reg 31) sit under a **statutory duty to keep them confidential and secure, with no stated retention period and no stated disposal point.** And because "medical record" is undefined (§4.21.6), it is unclear whether Bill 17/2026's carve-out would protect them from a private-sector auxiliary authorised officer at all.

### 4.15.3 ⚠️ The citation trap — republication went both ways

- **HOTA subsidiary legislation WAS republished** in the 2025 Revised Edition (in force 2 June 2025) and must be cited to it.
- **The HCSA blood, cord-blood and human-tissue-banking Regulations were NOT** republished and must still be cited **by S-number**.

**Getting this backwards produces wrong citations in either direction, and it is the commonest citation error available in this area.**

### 4.15.4 ⭐ The blood-donor Healthpoints linkage — no published legal basis, and an affirmative statutory obstacle

From **27 June 2026**, blood donors receive **200 Healthpoints via the Healthy 365 application** per donation. That is an **identified-donor data linkage** running from the blood service to the Health Promotion Board, and onward to a private rewards programme — **yuu Rewards Club**, the commercial loyalty scheme operated by **DFI Retail Group**, which pays **80 yuu Points** per donation at People's Association blood drives until **31 December 2026**. **Every link in the chain was checked against retrieved primary text. There is no published legal basis for it.**

**Why each candidate basis fails:**

| Candidate | Why it does not work |
|---|---|
| **PDPA s 4(1)(c)** — the public-agency exclusion. HSA and HPB are both public agencies, via **S 149/2013, Schedule items 16 and 17** (all seven amending notifications were retrieved; **neither entry was ever deleted**) | ⭐ **This is an exclusion, not a gateway.** It removes obligations; it confers no power to disclose. **Anyone citing s 4(1)(c) as "the legal basis" is misreading an exclusion as a permission.** Say so expressly |
| **PSGA s 6** | Authorises sharing only "to the extent permitted by the data sharing direction" — and **s 9(2)** provides that a direction "does not have legislative effect". A **462-hit full-text gazette search for "data sharing direction" returned no gazetted direction, ever** — only Acts using the phrase. See §6.4(a) |
| **Health Promotion Board Act s 12(e)** | Confined to information **"of a statistical nature"**. Identified per-donor records are not (§4.17.3) |
| **Health Sciences Authority Act** | **Has no information-sharing power at all** |
| **HPB Act s 39 and HSA Act s 39** | Both are **prohibitions on disclosure**, not authorisations |

⚠️ **THE CAVEAT THAT MUST TRAVEL WITH THIS FINDING — it changes its character rather than weakening it.** The most plausible mechanism for the HSA→HPB leg is **a data sharing direction under PSGA s 4**, and **s 4 contains no publication requirement of any kind** — the word "Gazette" does not appear in it, and s 44's parliamentary-laying duty reaches only subsidiary legislation. **The absence of a gazette hit is therefore structurally guaranteed, not evidentiary.** The gap is **structural, not necessarily an oversight**: the scheme running on an unpublished direction is at least as consistent with the evidence as its running on nothing.

📌 **State the consequence as a general rule, because it governs every inter-agency flow in this bundle: "no gazette hits" can never by itself establish that an inter-agency health-data flow is unauthorised.** Write **"no *published* legal basis"** and **"no *located* authorisation"**. **Never write "unauthorised" or "no legal basis".** The only route to an answer is a parliamentary question (§7.3).

⭐ **Two things survive the caveat, and they are what make the finding hold.**

1. **A PSGA direction could not cure the reg 20(3) problem even if one exists.** **s 6(1)** authorises sharing "**despite any obligation as to confidentiality under the common law**" — **common law only**. **s 6(2)** expressly preserves confidentiality obligations arising from legal privilege or contract. And **s 11(1)** provides that "**a direction under section 4 or 5 must not be inconsistent with this Act or any other written law**". **Blood Banking Service Regulations reg 20(3) is written law.** So the most plausible candidate basis is, **on its own terms, incapable of curing a statutory confidentiality problem.**
2. **The caveat cannot reach the yuu leg at all** — see immediately below.

**⭐ THE ASYMMETRY — this is the centre of the entry.** The rewards operator's **collection and use** of donor data is squarely authorised without consent by **PDPA s 17(1)** and **Second Schedule, Part 1, paragraph 1** (data "disclosed by a public agency"). But **the disclosure *to* it has no located authorisation whatsoever**: PSGA s 6 as currently in force reaches only "another Singapore public sector agency", and the rewards operator is not one. The power to share with a **"non-public sector person"** was created by **Public Sector (Governance) (Amendment) Act 2026 s 4(5)(b) — which is not in force** (§3A item 2).

> **So the statute book authorises the private company to receive the data while authorising nobody to send it.**

⭐ **And this is the leg the caveat above cannot rescue — which is why the yuu leg, not the Healthpoints leg, is the sharpest live exposure in the domain.** Every analytical route that softens the HSA→HPB transfer runs through the **public sector**: the PDPA public-agency disapplication (s 4(1)(c)) and the PSGA data-sharing regime, which by its own terms authorises sharing only "with another Singapore public sector agency" (s 6(1)). **yuu Rewards Club is neither.** It is a full PDPA "organisation", and **a data sharing direction — published or unpublished, existing or not — could not authorise disclosure to it under the law as in force.** The unpublishability caveat therefore has no work to do here at all.

**⭐ THE AFFIRMATIVE OBSTACLE.** **Blood Banking Service Regulations reg 20(3)** imposes an **unqualified, criminally enforced** (reg 24(1)) confidentiality duty over **exactly these donor records**. The full inserted text was read: **there is no "except", no "unless authorised by law", and no carve-out of any kind.**

**The only instrument capable of overriding it is Health Information Act 2026 s 50** ("Despite any other written law…") — and it cannot help, for two independent reasons: its **Fourth Schedule contains exactly four use cases**, none involving the Health Promotion Board, blood donation or rewards (§4.1.1); and **the Act is not in force**. Scripted term counts across the full enacted text return **zero** occurrences of "Healthpoint", "Healthy 365", "Health Promotion Board", "donation", "donor", "reward" and "People's Association".

**Two further gaps, recorded rather than glossed:**

- **The Singapore Red Cross is placed inside the scheme** by both HSA's own page and the Minister's speech — yet it appears in **neither the PDPA Statutory Bodies Schedule nor the PSGA Schedules**. It is therefore a **full PDPA "organisation" with full consent obligations**, and its role in the scheme has **no published treatment at all**.
- **The only parliamentary scrutiny of the scheme concerned donor motivation, not data.** *Singapore Parliamentary Debates, Official Report* (**6 May 2026**) **vol 96, sitting no 30, written answer 41**. **The data-protection question was never put.** And the published opt-out is an **in-person, pre-donation request to counter staff to suppress *point issuance*** — it is not a consent mechanism over the data flow.

⚠️ **A false lead, correctly rejected — do not resurrect it.** **S 422/2026** was gazetted 26 June 2026, one day before the 27 June go-live, which looks suggestive. **It is unrelated:** it only replaces item 62A (Skills and Workforce Development Agency) and deletes item 67.

---

## 4.16 STANDARDS, INTEROPERABILITY AND TELEHEALTH

### ⭐ The organising finding

**Every published Singapore Standard on health informatics is voluntary** — SS 719:2025 says so on its own face (NOTE 2). **What actually binds is a contractual certification framework operated by a government-owned company.**

The **HIA-Compliant Certification ("HCH") Framework**, run by **Synapxe**, has three limbs: **NEHR connectivity**; **Cyber Essentials for Health Information Management System providers**; and **data portability**. All three are **contractual today** — through Healthier SG and SmartCMS participation and grant eligibility — and become **statutory** on the Sept 2027 / Sept 2028 / Mar 2030 batches. ⚠️ **Limbs 2 and 3 are satisfied by self-declaration, not audit.**

This is the cleanest instance of the pattern in §2.2: the binding norm is a certification framework operated by a company, not a standard and not legislation.

The national terminology standard: NHDD, LOINC, SDD, SMST, SNOMED CT. ⚠️ **Expressly excludes HL7, FHIR and CDA** | P |

### 4.16.1 ✓ Verified negatives in this domain

- **There is no national de-identification standard.** PDPC's Guide to Basic Anonymisation (24 July 2024) does **not** fill the gap left by the unissued HBRA s 40 codes. See §5.
- **There is no health-data residency or localisation requirement.** Independently confirmed twice. See §4.18.
- ⚠️ **Do not attribute to the Singapore Government** a claim that Synapxe leads an effort to mandate HL7 FHIR nationally — it rests on an industry-survey self-report by an unnamed respondent.
- ⚠️ **Do not state that ICD-10-AM is mandated by any identified instrument.** Say that MOH *uses* it for billing, subvention and reporting. No instrument mandating it was located.

### 4.17.1 🚩 The vaccination-reporting duty points at the wrong body, and has since 1 April 2025

This is the sharpest single defect in the domain and it is fully documented.

- The duty is created by **IDA s 48(2)(b)** and made operative by **reg 7 of S 1082/2021**.
- The **CDA Act 2025**, commenced in whole on 1 April 2025, transferred the **National Immunisation Registry Branch** out of HPB (Schedule, Part 1, item 2) and by **s 54(4)** replaced "the Board" with "the Communicable Diseases Agency" in **IDA ss 48(2)(b)(i), 49, 50(4) and 63(1)**.
- **S 1082/2021 was never amended.** Its reg 7 still requires the practitioner to notify **"the Director or the Chief Executive"** by submitting through a website at an `nir.hpb.gov.sg` address.
  - "Chief Executive" is defined in reg 2(1) as the Chief Executive of **"the Board"**, and "Board" is defined in IDA s 2 as **the Health Promotion Board**.
  - **"The Director" is not a defined term in the current text of the IDA at all** — the definition was deleted by Act 11 of 2023 with effect from 1 May 2023, and the Act now uses "Director-General of Health" throughout.
  - The `nir.hpb.gov.sg` host now **301-redirects to the CDA**.
- **Verified exhaustively:** a full-text search of the entire e-Gazette for the string `nir.hpb.gov.sg` returns **exactly one document — S 1082/2021 itself.** No amending instrument has ever touched it.

**So the only operative reporting duty in Singapore's national immunisation system names the Chief Executive of the wrong statutory board and a web address that no longer serves it — sixteen and a half months after the transfer.** The CDA Act s 55 saving power runs only to 1 April 2027 and no such regulations have been made.

### 4.17.2 🚩 The prescribed duty covers two diseases; the registry covers twenty-five

**IDA s 48(2)(b)** is expressed universally — notify "within the prescribed period after carrying out **any vaccination**". But the duty bites only where a period and manner have been prescribed, and **reg 7 of S 1082/2021 is confined to a child vaccinated "against diphtheria or measles under these Regulations".**

All ten current instruments under the Infectious Diseases Act were enumerated, and the only other vaccination instrument (S 611/2008) deals solely with WHO international certificates.

**No instrument prescribes the manner or period for notifying any vaccination other than a child's diphtheria or measles vaccination**, and none prescribes the manner for the s 48(2)(a) record-keeping duty either. Meanwhile the National Immunisation Registry covers the **14-disease childhood schedule and the 11-disease adult schedule**.

**Consequence: National Adult Immunisation Schedule and HPV data reach the National Immunisation Registry by practice, not by duty.** ⚠️ *This is the source agent's own reading of the statutory position, flagged as such, and is worth a second look before it is relied on.*

### 4.17.3 🚩 HPB has no statutory power to collect individual-level health data

The Health Promotion Board Act confers wide **programme** functions but a strikingly narrow **information** power:

- **s 12(e)** — the Board may "collect, analyse, compile, publish or disseminate information **of a statistical nature**".
- **There is no power to require information from anyone**, no compulsion provision, and **no statutory disclosure gateway**.
- **"Confidential" appears zero times** in the Act; **"personal data" zero times**. The only secrecy provision is **s 39**, penalty $2,000 / 12 months.

Contrast the **CDA Act s 6(2)(p)**, which permits collection of information "of a statistical nature **or otherwise**", and **s 6(2)(q)**, "to set up and administer systems for the recording and collection of information".

**Consequence: every individual-level HPB collection in this domain — Healthy 365, hiSG, Healthier SG Screening, the National Population Health Survey, school health records — rests on consent and programme terms of use, not on statute.** In this domain the pattern in §2.2 is close to universal.

### 4.17.4 🚩 hiSG — a 24-month wearable cohort governed by a website privacy notice

**Health Insights Singapore** issues participants a wearable device, monitors them for a **minimum of two years**, and collects physical activity, sleep, nutrition and mental-wellbeing data plus meal logs and questionnaires, expressly to develop programmes "powered by artificial intelligence engines for our population".

**Its published governance consists of a generic Government Agency Privacy Statement and a Terms of Use whose clause 7 obtains consent to HPB disclosing identifiable data to "companies/organisations/institutions" *without further notice to you*.** The substantive study terms and conditions sit behind registration and were **not retrieved**. Whether the 10,000-person "SG100K" arm connects to PRECISE-SG100K has **not been established**.

### 4.17.5 ✓ Verified negatives

| Proposition | How verified |
|---|---|
| **No gazette instrument in Singapore's history is titled with the word "immunisation"** | Title-restricted search of the full e-Gazette index: **0 hits** |
| "National Immunisation Registry" appears in the gazette in **only two documents** — the CDA Act 2025 and its Bill | Full-text gazette search: 2 hits |
| **There is no subsidiary legislation under the Health Promotion Board Act 2001** | SSO browse, current subsidiary legislation, title "Health Promotion": **0 results** |
| Compulsory childhood vaccination is limited to **diphtheria and measles** | IDA **Fourth Schedule** read verbatim: two items only |
| The National Childhood and Adult Immunisation Schedules are **not legal instruments** and appear nowhere in legislation | Gazette title and full-text searches; MOH's own press release calls the adult schedule "guidance" and says its vaccinations "are not mandatory" |
| **"School" appears 0 times in the Health Information Act 2026** | Full-text count |

⚠️ **Two gaps:** **school health records** are unowned as a domain; and while the Infectious Diseases Act **s 57B** permits disclosure of communicable-disease status to specified recipients, **no s 57B "specified recipient" notice has been located**, so whether schools, employers or dormitory operators are in fact designated is **unknown**.

## 4.18 CROSS-BORDER TRANSFER AND DATA LOCALISATION

### ✓ THE HEADLINE — a verified negative

**There is no health-data localisation or residency requirement in Singapore law.** Independently confirmed from two directions and corroborated by two MOH primary documents. What governs an outbound transfer of health data is the general **PDPA s 26** Transfer Limitation Obligation, and nothing health-specific.

- The **Health Information Act 2026** contains **no transfer or localisation restriction**; its security duties are location-neutral and expressly reach health data intermediaries wherever situated.
- The **Cybersecurity and Data Security Essentials** expressly contemplate health information stored "in Singapore or overseas" and route the question to PDPA s 26.
- The **HIA Implementation Guide v2.0** provides cloud-vendor templates with **no hosting-location requirement**.
- The **NEHR Guidelines (13 March 2026)** contain **no cross-border or hosting-location provision**.
- **HBRA restricts cross-border transfer of tissue only** — s 35(2)(f) and Tissue Banking Regulations reg 15 — **not data**.
- The **National Registry of Diseases Act** confidentiality and disclosure regime (ss 8–12) contains **no territorial restriction on the recipient**.

### Summary table

| # | Instrument | Type | Citation | Status @ 15 Aug 2026 | P/S |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| 2 | **Personal Data Protection Regulations 2021, Part 3 (regs 9–12)** | Subsidiary legislation | **S 63/2021** | In force 1 Feb 2021; current version in force from 2 Mar 2026. Prescribes the conditions and permitted mechanisms | P |
| 3 | **Personal Data Protection (Amendment) Regulations 2026** | Subsidiary legislation | **S 86/2026** | **In force 2 March 2026.** Adds **Global CBPR** and **Global PRP** as specified certifications under reg 12(2) | P |

Federated analytics; **data remains local, only aggregated results move**. TRUST is testing HDR UK federated-analytics tooling (news item 26 February 2026) | P |

### 4.18.1 What actually constrains an outbound transfer

**PDPA s 26 plus PDP Regulations Part 3.** The permitted mechanisms are the ordinary ones: legally enforceable obligations (including the ASEAN Model Contractual Clauses), specified certifications (**APEC CBPR and PRP; and since 2 March 2026 the Global CBPR and Global PRP**), and the statutory exceptions.

⚠️ **A structurally unknowable exception.** **PDPA s 26(2)** permits the Commission to exempt a transfer, and **s 26(3)(b) provides that such an exemption need not be gazetted.** So the set of granted exemptions cannot be established. See §6.

🔴 **Two outbound pathways for human-derived material and identifiable infectious-disease data that no part of the cross-border analysis has owned.** Both are recorded here because a reader looking for "how does health data leave Singapore" will not find either by looking at PDPA s 26.

1. **The NIDB clinical/research collection.** CDA's published NIDB Framework states that deposited clinical and research samples are for use by the scientific community "**in Singapore and abroad**" (§4.10.9). **No transfer conditions, no material transfer agreement template and no country restrictions are published**, and the overseeing committee's terms of reference do not exist publicly. **This is a named, live, cross-border pathway for human-derived material with unpublished terms.**
2. **IDA s 59A(5)(b).** The power to send or provide material to a "**third party**" is **undefined and not confined to Singapore-based recipients**, and **s 59A(6) permits identifiable material** where the Director-General is satisfied it is necessary. **So the compelled national public health research power carries an outbound limb on its face** (§4.10.7).

⚠️ **Neither is caught by PDPA s 26 in the ordinary way**, because the holders are public agencies for whom Parts 3–6B impose no obligation (§4.4). **State them as pathways, not as breaches — nothing establishes that either has been used to transfer identifiable data abroad.**

### 4.18.2 The parliamentary record on cross-border health data — a real gap, stated as one

A complete sweep of every sitting from 1 January 2024 to 15 August 2026 found **no Parliamentary Question on cross-border transfer of health data**. The only cross-border health-data material in the whole record is a Second Reading question about records generated overseas — prompted by the Johor–Singapore Special Economic Zone and a December 2025 memorandum on health cooperation — and the answer that **"the HIB only applies within Singapore"**: patients may bring overseas records to a Singapore provider, who may incorporate them into their own records, and once incorporated they are contributed to the NEHR.

**There is no inbound or outbound cross-border health data regime in the parliamentary record at all.** The client should be told that is a gap, not given a thin answer.

### 4.18.3 ⚠️ What this bundle does not cover on the international side

Cyber conventions and cyber-norm instruments — the Budapest Convention, the UN Cybercrime Convention (Hanoi, October 2025), the Counter Ransomware Initiative, ASEAN cyber norms and the ASEAN Framework on Digital Data Governance — **are not covered.** They were assigned and never delivered, and the one attempt produced only an AI-generated search summary that was correctly refused. ⛔ **Do not report any Singapore position on the UN Convention against Cybercrime — it is unverified.** See §7.

### 4.19.1 ⭐ THE STRAIGHT ANSWER — there is no doctor–patient privilege in Singapore

Verified by full-text count: **"medical", "physician" and "doctor" appear ZERO times in the entire Evidence Act 1893.** Further:

- **Rules of Court 2021 O 11 r 9** expressly bars withholding production on confidentiality grounds.
- **CPC s 20(5)** overrides any other law on production (§2.5).
- **CPC s 235(4)** provides that the court's production-summons power "does not affect any provision of the Evidence Act 1893" — so the Evidence Act is the *only* source of protection against a s 235 summons, and it contains no medical privilege.
- **CPC ss 21 and 22** compel a doctor's attendance and truthful answers, the only excuse being **self-incrimination of the doctor** (s 22(2)) — not the patient's confidentiality.

**Legal professional privilege survives; medical confidentiality does not.** The one qualification is *Rahimah*, where **litigation privilege** protected psychiatric reports — but that is privilege attaching to the litigation, not to the doctor–patient relationship.

### 4.19.2 Live and unresolved points of law

**(a) The materiality standard for pre-action production is unresolved between three co-ordinate first-instance decisions.**

| Decision | Formulation |
|---|---|
| *Gillingham* [2023] SGHCR 13 at [17] | material to "the applicant's determination of whether there is a cause of action which can be fashioned into a viable claim" |
| *Yeap Seng Yen v Singapore General Hospital* [2026] SLR (StC) 70 at [20(c)] | material to "the issues in the putative case" |
| *Tumyu* [2026] SGHCR 27 at [66]–[68] | expressly prefers the *Gillingham* formulation and proposes a **two-stage test** — threshold requirements first, then countervailing considerations |

*Tumyu* at [66]: the previous requirement of necessity "should now be viewed through the lens of materiality" (*Tumyu Limited v TLC Northcote Development Limited* [2026] SGHCR 27 at [66]).

**Why it matters for health data: the only located Singapore decision on pre-action production *against a hospital* is on the losing side of this disagreement.** A claimant seeking a hospital's records before commencing a medical negligence claim faces an unsettled standard. **There is no appellate ruling.**

**(b) No Singapore appellate decision has yet applied the *I-Admin* framework to medical information.** *I-Admin* changed the cause of action in 2020, but every located Singapore case applying it concerns commercial or employment information. **The application of the reversed burden of proof to a hospital or doctor who discloses patient information is, as at 15 August 2026, untested.**

**(c) The Health Information Act will displace parts of this, but is not in force.** Until commencement, the equitable duty of confidence and the professional codes remain the operative norms without statutory displacement.

### 4.19.3 ✓ Verified negatives, and citation cautions

- **There is no reported Singapore PDPA private action about health data.**
- **There is no court judgment on appeal from PDPC involving health data**, and **Data Protection Appeal Committee decisions are not published** (§6).
- 📌 **The private right of action is PDPA s 48O, not s 32.** The section is commonly mis-cited; *Piper* reproduces s 48O verbatim.
- ⛔ **Do not cite** an unnamed SMC case described as involving "six charges and an 18-month suspension". It is uncorroborated and no Grounds of Decision has been located. **The instruction to treat it as unreliable must survive.**

| 6 | Spam Control Act 2007; PDPA Part 9 (Do Not Call Registry, ss 36–48) and S 709/2013 | 2020 Rev Ed; S 709/2013 | In force | Marketing calls and messages to patients; the Eighth Schedule "ongoing relationship" carve-out | P |
| 7 | **Online Criminal Harms Act 2023** | **Act 24 of 2023** | Fully in force 24 Jun 2024 | First Schedule item 6 = all Computer Misuse Act offences; item 44 = public-sector information-disclosure offences | P |
| 8 | OCHA First Schedule amendment orders | S 561/2025; **S 257/2026** (in force 1 May 2026, adding Tobacco and Vaporisers Control Act offences); **S 559/2026** | S 559/2026 gazetted **14 August 2026**, **in force 17 August 2026** — i.e. **not in force as at the retrieval date** | Health-adjacent extension of the specified-offence list | P |
| 9 | Protection from Harassment Act 2014 | 2020 Rev Ed | In force, as amended | **ss 3(1)(c), 4(1)(c) doxxing limbs** — reach health facts only where they are *identity* information. **s 6 protects public service workers, which includes healthcare workers** | P |
| 10 | **Online Safety (Relief and Accountability) Act 2025** | **Act 23 of 2025** | **Partly in force 29 Jun 2026.** ⚠️ **s 11 — non-consensual disclosure of private information, whose statutory illustration is a health condition — is NOT in force** (§3A item 15) | The health-facing provision is the one left out | P |
| 11 | Electronic Transactions Act 2010 | 2020 Rev Ed | In force | ss 6–9 recognition of electronic records and signatures; s 9 electronic retention. ⚠️ **No medical exclusion** | P |
| 12 | Evidence Act 1893, ss 32(1)(b), 116A | 2020 Rev Ed | In force | Business-records exception and electronic-record presumptions — **the route by which health records are admitted** | P |
| 13 | Broadcasting Act 1994 as amended by Act 38 of 2022; Codes of Practice for Online Safety (Jul 2023) and for App Distribution Services (in force 31 Mar 2025) | — | In force | "Egregious content" includes content obstructing a public health measure or creating a public health risk | P |
| 14 | Protection from Online Falsehoods and Manipulation Act 2019 | **Act 18 of 2019** | In force | ss 4(b), 7(1)(b)(ii) — falsehoods prejudicial to public health | P |
| 15 | **Government Technology Agency Act 2016** | **Act 23 of 2016** (Acts Supplement No. 20 of 2016) | In force | Establishes GovTech, operator of Singpass and Myinfo. ⚠️ **Never mentions identity, Singpass or a national digital identity.** 📌 **The Act number is 23, not 20** — see §1.4(a) | P |
| 16 | Myinfo data catalogue — Government Scheme category | — | Live | ⚠️ **Exposes a `chas` field — Community Health Assist Scheme, sourced from MOH** | P |
| 17 | Record-retention duties in general law | Companies Act 1967 s 199(2) (**5 years**); Income Tax Act 1947 s 67(1)(a) (**5 years**); Employment Act 1968 s 95 with **S 148/2016** reg 5 (**2 years / 1 year after**) | In force | **Conflict with PDPA s 25 disposal duties and with the HCSA retention Licence Conditions** — unreconciled (§4.2.2) | P |
| 18 | **Scams (Countermeasures) and Other Matters Bill** | **Bill No. 16/2026** | **NOT PASSED** (§3B item 20) | Would override PDPA ss 48D–48F **and "rule of professional conduct"** for ordered disclosures | P |
| 19 | Trusted Data Sharing Framework; Data Regulatory Sandbox | IMDA and PDPC | Live; **voluntary, no statutory power**; the sandbox is **advisory only and confers no immunity, waiver or exemption** | Sample Data Sharing Agreements used for health data collaborations | P |
| 20 | SS 584:2020 (MTCS); Government on Commercial Cloud; Green Data Centre Roadmap; Second Data Centre Call for Application | — | Current. GCC hosts up to "Confidential" classification, derived from IM8; DC-CFA2 closed 31 Mar 2026, outcome not published | Where sensitive health workloads may physically sit | P |
| 21 | PDPC Guide on Synthetic Data Generation; IMDA PETs Adoption Guide; PDPC Guide on Federated Learning | — | Sep 2024; 14 Jul 2025; **20 Jul 2026** | Re-identification risk; privacy-enhancing technologies for health analysis | P |

**✓ Verified negative:** **there is no Singpass or national digital identity statute.** An exhaustive title search of the e-Gazette Acts and Bills Supplements across all years confirms it. The only place "Singpass" appears in the Singapore statute book is **Computer Misuse Act s 8B and the First Schedule**, inserted by Act 16 of 2023 and amended by Act 21 of 2025.
