## 4.21 THE STATE'S OWN POWERS TO ACCESS HEALTH DATA

This domain was assigned late and delivered a set of findings that belong at the front of any advice on health data confidentiality. **Thirteen statutes and one Bill were read in full from gazette PDFs.**

### 4.21.1 The organising point

**CPC s 20(5)** — set out at §2.5 — lets a police officer or authorised person exercise the s 20 production power "despite any provision in any other law relating to the production of, or the giving of any access to, any document or thing, or data". The power it overrides for is a **written order from a police officer of or above the rank of sergeant**, or an authorised person, where the officer "considers that any document or thing … is necessary or desirable" for an investigation or proceeding. **s 20(1)(b)** reaches a document "contained in or available to a computer" by ordering a person "believed to have power to access" it to produce it or give access to a copy; **s 20(1A)** does the same for **"data"** and additionally requires the recipient **"to authenticate the data"**. Those two limbs are how an electronic medical record system is reached without anyone entering the premises.

**Sanction — s 20(7):** non-compliance is an offence; a body corporate up to **$10,000**; an individual served with an order stating it is for an arrestable offence, up to **$5,000 or 6 months**; otherwise up to **$1,500 or one month**. **Immunity — s 20(8):** no liability lies against a person acting in good faith and with reasonable care in complying.

### 4.21.2 The recurring statutory formula

Across thirteen statutes, **exactly one** medical-record carve-out was found — and it is in an unpassed Bill, and it applies only to *private-sector* auxiliary officers. Everywhere else the legislation **expressly names medical records and expressly disapplies patient consent**. The recurring formula is "**even though the prior consent of such person has not been obtained**", and it appears in:

**HBRA s 45(2)(b)** · **HCSA s 41(2)(b)** · **PHMCA s 12(2)(f)** · **NRDA s 14(1)(b)(ii)** · and, in the health-information register, **HIA 2026 ss 93(2)(d) and 94(2)(d)**.

**It is not an oversight. It is a deliberate, repeated drafting choice.**

**And the privileges these statutes recognise protect the person compelled or their lawyer — never the patient.** Self-incrimination is preserved in HCSA s 42(2), HBRA s 45(10) and new s 45B(2), HIA s 95(2), PDPA Ninth Schedule para 1A(2), CPC s 22(2) and new HCSA s 36A(4). Legal privilege is preserved in Cybersecurity Act s 23(3)(a), HIA s 84(4)(a) and CPC s 235(4). **The patient is a third party to the entire transaction and has no standing in any of it.**

### 4.21.3 The four answers, stated plainly

| Question | Answer |
|---|---|
| **Medical-record carve-out?** | **One** — new HCSA s 41A(2)(a), (3)(b)–(c) and its HBRA twin, in an unpassed Bill, for private-sector auxiliary officers only — **and already defeated on its own terms by new HCSA s 36A** (§3B.1(g)). Every regime in force expressly *includes* medical records, and five expressly disapply the patient's consent by name |
| **Proportionality?** | Genuine tests exist in **four** places: **Cybersecurity Act s 20(3)** (severity threshold); **HIA s 84(1)** (a "critical event" that is or is likely to be a serious and imminent threat to public health) and **s 94(2)(g)(ii)** (necessity of seizure); **NRDA ss 10(2) and 12(2)** (enumerated criteria and an anonymisation-necessity test); and **IDA s 59A(2)** (three mandatory factors, though defeasible under s 59A(4) and (6)). **Everywhere else the test is the officer's own view that the material is "necessary", or "necessary or desirable", or merely relevant to administering the Act** |
| **Judicial authorisation?** | **One** route — **PDPA Ninth Schedule para 3**, a warrant with a one-month life and a **3-month retention cap** (para 3(12)). Every other power in this domain, including every power that reaches identifiable clinical records, is exercisable **without a warrant**. The Coroner is a judicial officer, but Coroners Act s 16(1) confers the same directing power on the Public Prosecutor |
| **Patient notification?** | **NONE. Not one regime, in force or pending, requires that the individual whose medical record is inspected, copied, seized or exported be told.** Notice obligations exist, but they run to the *occupier* (PDPA Ninth Schedule para 2(2) — **2 working days' written notice** stating subject matter and purpose; Cybersecurity Act s 20(2)(d)) or to *the person from whom a thing was seized* (HCSA s 41(4)(a); HBRA s 45(6)(a)). **The patient is never a notice recipient in any provision read** |

**The structural reason is worth stating.** These statutes are drafted on a **licensee/occupier model**: the regulated person is the clinic, the hospital, the research institution, the system operator. The patient is not a party to the regulatory relationship and appears in the drafting only as the subject of the records — which is precisely why the only time consent is mentioned is to disapply it.

### 4.21.4 Onward use — and the one regime that gets it right

| Regime | Restriction on the regulator's onward use? |
|---|---|
| **HCSA s 51** | **Yes — the most detailed in force.** s 51(2) bars the Director-General and authorised officers from disclosing medical information or information relating to any individual's condition, treatment or diagnosis obtained under the Act — subject to gateways: administering or enforcing **twelve** named Acts; making a complaint or providing information under the disciplinary Parts of **seven** professional registration Acts; and any other purpose **with consent**. **s 51(1)** also makes them **not compellable** in proceedings to give evidence about or produce such material, except in criminal proceedings under the Act. **No retention limit stated** |
| **NRDA ss 8–12** | **Yes — the strongest and best-structured in Singapore health law.** See §4.10.4 |
| **PDPA s 59** | Yes, framed around access-refusal categories and informant identity. **s 59(5)(b) permits disclosure to the Public Prosecutor and police** where the Commission considers there is evidence of an offence. **Ninth Schedule para 3(12): 3-month retention cap** |
| **Cybersecurity Act s 43** | **Yes for ss 19–20 material** — secrecy duty, $10,000 / 12 months, with gateways including disclosure **to any police officer** of information disclosing a Computer Misuse Act offence. ⚠️ **s 43(3): NOT for s 23 material. The most coercive power in the Act produces the least protected data** |
| **Health Products Act s 66** | ⚠️ **NO for patient data.** s 66(1) protects only health-**product** confidential information. **There is no restriction whatever on onward use of clinical or patient information obtained under s 49**, and s 66(2)(c) permits disclosure to assist any public officer in investigating or prosecuting **any offence under any written law** |
| **IDA s 59A(5)** | ⚠️ **NO — the opposite.** An express power to send information or samples to a third party for testing, and to provide them to a third party engaged in national public health research, subject only to conditions the Director-General thinks fit |
| **HBRA s 49** | Only for **research-side** information — it protects the institution's research interests, not patients' records |
| **Coroners Act; MRA s 60A** | **None.** "Confiden*" appears zero times in the Coroners Act; MRA s 47 restricts onward use of performance and fitness-assessment material only, not s 60A investigation material |
| **HIA 2026 ss 91–95** | 🚩 **NOTHING** — see §4.1.1 |

**Three consequences the client should see:**

**(a) HCSA s 51(2)(b)(iii) is the statutory pipe from MOH regulatory inspection into professional discipline.** It permits disclosure of medical information for the purpose of making a complaint or providing information under Part 7 of the Medical Registration Act. **Every MOH clinic audit is therefore, by statutory design, a potential SMC referral, and patient records collected for one purpose lawfully travel into disciplinary proceedings against the doctor. The patient is not told and has no say.**

**(b) The Health Information Act 2026 is the least constrained regime of all on onward use — and it is still fixable.** Widest inbound powers, no outbound restriction (§4.1.1). Given that the Act is uncommenced and commencement is targeted for early 2027, **this is the single most actionable legislative gap in the bundle.**

**(c) Concurrent-regime exposure.** A regulator that lawfully obtains a health record under one Act can, under an express gateway, feed it to another agency: **HCSA s 51(2)(a)** (twelve Acts); **Health Products Act s 66(2)(c)** (any offence under any written law); **PDPA s 59(5)(b)** (the Public Prosecutor and police); **Cybersecurity Act s 43(7)** (any police officer, for Computer Misuse Act offences). **The practical effect is that the twelve-Act HCSA gateway, plus CPC s 20(5), plus IDA s 10(4), plus Cybersecurity Act s 23(3), leaves no statutory route by which a Singapore health record can be withheld from the State once any one agency has a lawful basis to hold it.**

### 4.21.5 The *Ling Chia Tien* question — what authorised the 2016 MOH clinic audit?

*SMC v Ling Chia Tien* [2024] SGHC 283 records that an **MOH audit obtained 16 patient medical records** from a clinic. **The judgment names no statutory power.**

⚠️ **The answer below is reasoned from the statute in force on 1 November 2016, not cited from the judgment, and must be carried with that caveat.** On that reasoning the power was **Private Hospitals and Medical Clinics Act s 12(1)(b) with s 12(2)(f)** — the audit-and-copy power, with consent expressly disapplied — and onward transmission to the SMC ran through **PHMCA s 13(2)(b)**, the Medical Registration Act Part 7 gateway. **No medical-record limit and no notification requirement attached.**

### 4.21.6 "Medical record" is undefined — and that is the weak point of the one safeguard

**"Medical record" is defined nowhere in Singapore health legislation.** Ten Acts and eight sets of regulations were swept: the term appears in six instruments and is defined in none of them. **And there is no general definition to fall back on — the Interpretation Act 1965 contains zero occurrences of the term.**

**The six instruments, named, so the sweep can be checked:**

| Instrument | Where "medical record" appears | Defined? |
|---|---|---|
| **Healthcare Services Act 2020** | **s 41(2)(b)** inspection power; **s 51(2)** confidentiality duty | No |
| **Human Biomedical Research Act 2015** | **s 45(2)(b)** inspection power | No |
| **Medical Registration Act 1997** | **s 60A(4)** | ⚠️ **Only the reverse way round** — it defines "**record**" as including "the medical record of any person", and only for s 60A |
| **Health Information Act 2026** | **s 3(5)(b)** — inside the Act's own central definitional provision | No |
| **Private Hospitals and Medical Clinics Act 1980** (repealed) and its Regulations | s 12(1)(f) audit-and-copy power; s 13(2) regulator confidentiality | No |
| **Bill 17/2026** | **9 occurrences**, including new HBRA ss 45(2)(b), 45A(2)(a), 45A(3)(b)–(c) and new HCSA ss 41A(2)(a), 41A(3)(b)–(c), plus the Explanatory Statement twice | No — **and the Bill does not amend HCSA s 51(6), HCSA s 3(1) or HBRA s 2(1) to insert one** |

⭐ **The diagnosis, and it explains the whole defect: this is a 1980 PHMCA term carried through two statutory generations.** When MOH modernised the record-keeping duty in 2021–2023 it **switched the operative noun** to "**patient health record**", defined it, and moved the content into the regulation itself. But the **Act-level** enforcement and confidentiality provisions **kept the old, undefined 1980 noun**. The two vocabularies have run in parallel ever since — **and Bill 17/2026 builds its new safeguard on the older, undefined one.**

**The only defined cognate anywhere is "patient health record" in reg 2 of the Healthcare Services (General) Regulations 2021 (S 1035/2021)** — and that definition is expressly confined to those Regulations, so it does not reach the HCSA itself, the HBRA, or Bill 17/2026.

📌 **HCSA s 51(6) shows the drafting problem in miniature: the Act defines the contents and leaves the container undefined.** s 51(2) prohibits disclosing "any **medical information** contained in **the medical record** of any individual" — and s 51(6) defines "medical information" while saying nothing about "medical record". **Even that definition is confined to s 51.**

⚠️ **The HBRA position is worse than the HCSA position.** The HCSA at least has a *related* defined term (s 51(6)) and a *regulation-level* cognate (reg 2) that a court could reach for by analogy. **The HBRA has neither.** Its own defined term is "health information", which is *wider* than any plausible reading of "medical record". **So in a research setting the carve-out is anchored to nothing.**

⚠️ **A citation trap to correct, because an earlier research lead pointed the wrong way.** **S 387/2023 is an Order made under HCSA s 56 amending the Act's Schedules, and it contains no record provision at all** — do not cite it for one. **Reg 37 was amended by S 414/2023**, and **the "patient health record" definition sits in reg 2, not reg 37**; reg 37 prescribes record *content*.

Yet the entire safeguard barring private-sector auxiliary officers from patient data rests on the undefined term.

⭐ **The same failure mode appears in the newest statute in the bundle, and it should be presented as a pattern rather than as two coincidences.** The **Health Information Act 2026 First Schedule Part 1 item 4** makes an HCSA clinical-laboratory licensee a specified contributor of "**Laboratory test reports**" — and **"laboratory test report" is not defined either.** First Schedule Part 2 defines ten terms (adverse drug event history, cardiac report, dispensed medication, medical device, medication, ordered (prescribed) medication, referral memorandum, surgical procedure notes, Table of Surgical Procedures, visit event) **and not that one**; the whole of Part 2 was read in the gazetted PDF to confirm it.

**The consequence is the same shape as the "medical record" defect, one statutory generation later:** the scope of a **compulsory contribution duty**, rather than of a protective carve-out, turns on an undefined phrase. **Whether a pathogen genome sequence forms part of a "laboratory test report" determines whether Singapore's national public health genomics laboratory must push sequence-derived results into the national health record from September 2027** (§4.10.8, §4.10A.4). ⭐ **Both defects are curable by the same mechanism — a definition — and in the HIA's case the mechanism is expressly available at s 3(1) and s 111. Neither has been used.**

🔴 **A consequence that matters for the transplant, blood and tissue domain.** Because the Bill 17/2026 carve-out is keyed to "medical record", and a blood, cord blood or tissue bank's **donor** records are neither "patient health records" within reg 2 nor obviously "medical records", **it is genuinely unclear whether the Bill's central safeguard protects donor records at all** from a private-sector auxiliary authorised officer. A safeguard whose scope depends on an undefined phrase, in a Bill whose Explanatory Statement already misdescribes the clauses it explains, is a safeguard worth testing at second reading.

---

# 5. DATA ACCESS PATHWAYS — who governs access to Singapore health and genomic data

## 5.1 Orientation

**There is no single body governing access to Singapore health and genomic data.** There are **five distinct access-governance models**, plus narrower gateways, plus a statutory ethics layer cutting across all of them, plus a national contracting framework sitting upstream of the lot.

**The five models differ on the most basic question of all — who decides:**

| Model | Who decides |
|---|---|
| **1. TRUST Data Access Committee** | One central committee, MOH-chaired, deciding for all contributors |
| **2. PRECISE-SG100K DAC** | A programme committee, deciding for one programme's dataset |
| **3. NPM DAC** | A programme committee under GIS, A\*STAR, deciding for one legacy dataset |
| **4. Biomedical Data Hub** | **Nobody centrally** — the data host institute's own committee decides; BDH provides governance and infrastructure |
| **5. NMRC Research Data Governance and Sharing Framework** | **The contributing institution** decides; the funder checks eligibility only; a funder committee hears appeals |

Models 1 and 5 are opposites. TRUST **centralises** the decision by taking a delegation from contributors; NMRC **decentralises** it by imposing on every grantee institution a standing obligation to run its own committee. Model 4 does neither.

**The second organising fact: all five rest on contract, policy or grant condition. None rests on a statute.**

**The third, and the one most likely to mislead — two of the names are the same word:**

| | **enTRUST** | **TRUST** |
|---|---|---|
| Expansion | "**en**abling **T**rusted **R**esearch and real-world data **U**tilisation and **S**haring **T**ech" | "**T**rusted **R**esearch and **R**eal world-data **U**tilisation and **S**haring **T**ech" |
| What it is | **Infrastructure** — GovTech's shared secure data platform | The national health data governance framework and platform |
| Operator | **GovTech**, Data Programme | **RIE TRUST Office** (moved from the MOH Office for Healthcare Transformation to the National Research Foundation, hosted by A\*STAR, on **1 April 2026**) |
| Decides access? | **No** — it delegates to sector authorities | **No, itself.** The **TRUST DAC** decides |

**The TRUST Members' Portal is hosted at `entrust.gov.sg`**, which is why the two run together in practice and in memory.

⚠️ **enTRUST does not rest on the Public Sector (Governance) Act.** Its own terms cite no statutory basis, and no retrieved source states one. **Do not assert one.** And in any event the Public Sector (Governance) (Amendment) Act 2026 is wholly uncommenced.

⚠️ **Do not cite "RIDES" as current.** Multiple secondary descriptions name a "Research and Industry Data Exchange Subcommittee" as TRUST's steering body, and TRUST's own site once carried such a path. **Every such URL now returns 404**, and the live governance page names the **RIE Data System Steering Committee** instead. The succession is unresolved.

## 5.2 The comparison table

| | **1. TRUST DAC** | **2. PRECISE-SG100K DAC** | **3. NPM DAC** | **4. Biomedical Data Hub (federated)** | **5. NMRC Framework** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Governing body** | TRUST Data Access Committee. Chair: **Deputy Secretary (Development), MOH**. 14 named members from MOH, MDDI, the Department of Statistics, A\*STAR, CRIS, Duke-NUS, NTU, NHG, NUHS, NUS and SingHealth, plus ethics, legal and lay members. Secretariat of 6 | PRECISE-SG100K Data Access Committee, under **PRECISE** (hosted by CRIS). Works with the **Scientific Committee** on merit | NPM Phase I Data Access Committee, under the NPM Programme Coordinating Office at **GIS, A\*STAR**. Registered at the European Genome-phenome Archive as DAC **EGAC00001001322** | **No central body.** "Access requests are managed and approved by the data owner's Data Access Committee (DAC) or a central DAC" | **The Data Contributing Institution's own** committee or representative. NMRC checks requestor eligibility only |
| **Body above it** | **RIE Data System Steering Committee**, chaired by the Permanent Secretary (National Research and Development) | PRECISE **Board Oversight Committee**; industry requests screened first by the **Business Development Team** | **Precision Medicine Steering Committee** — may impose additional conditions and introduce fees. **Composition not published** | Hosted at A\*STAR; controlled by MOH and NMRC through grant **MOH-001850** | **NMRC Data Access Oversight Committee** — appeals only |
| **Legal basis** | **Contract.** The DAC "has thus been delegated the authority to approve data access on behalf of the Data Contributors" (DAC Annual Report FY2024, p 6). **No Act, regulation or gazetted instrument creates TRUST, and no statutory power is claimed on any retrieved page** | **Policy plus participant consent.** A non-statutory programme committee resting on opt-in broad research consent that expressly covers "working with industry" | **Policy plus contract.** A published Data Access Policy, a **non-negotiable institutional Data Access Agreement**, and platform Terms of Use. Ethics runs on **pre-existing IRB approvals** | **Grant condition plus contract.** Funded and controlled through NMRC grant MOH-001850; access granted "under the terms of the data use agreement" | **A term of the funding contract.** The Terms of Use state that acceptance "shall create a binding agreement between the Grantor, the Data Requestor and his Relevant Institution", and expressly **do not supersede** any institutional policy or applicable law |
| **What data** | ~45–50 **anonymised** datasets: population, clinical (diagnosis, medication, laboratory, radiology, healthcare financing), lifestyle and wearables, chronic disease screening, plus strategic research datasets including **genomic** and longitudinal cohorts. **Not a repository** — contributors retain ownership; data is deleted on completion | PRECISE-SG100K **research phenotype and genomic data only** (~100,000 participants; 30X whole-genome sequencing; questionnaires, physical measurements, biochemical assays). **EHR-linked clinical data is outside its remit** | **SG10K_Health** — whole genomes of **10,323** consented healthy individuals, with methylation, an imputation panel and structural variants; clinical fields minimal (year of birth, gender, ethnic group). **SG10K_Pilot** — 4,180 genomes | A governance and services layer over **two** A\*STAR repositories — BII's Biomed DAR and GIS's **RAPTOR** — under "one governance and services layer", ISO 27001 and ISO 38505 | **"Final Research Data"** and metadata from NMRC-funded projects, deposited into the **NMRC Research Data Repository** (developed by Synapxe). Robustly anonymised before deposit. **Minimum 10-year retention** |
| **Who may apply** | "bona-fide researchers" from the **public sector and publicly funded research institutions**; the institution must hold a **Data Request Agreement** with TRUST | Lead investigators with a **primary appointment in a local publicly funded institution**; or the Pharma Pre-competitive Consortium route | "Researchers from academic settings in Singapore", with the DAC prioritising Singapore-based applicants and key strategic collaborations | **No published eligibility criteria at all.** A contact form only | An employee with a primary appointment in a **Singapore Public Institution** at a level equivalent to a Principal Investigator. **Graduate students and postdoctoral researchers may not submit** |
| **Industry eligible?** | **Published answer: no. Actual answer: already yes, selectively.** Published eligibility is public-sector only; the FAQ says TRUST "is beginning selective collaboration with industry and overseas entities"; **yet the FY2024 annexes already list industry-collaborative approvals, all approved 20 December 2024.** The framework, criteria, commercial terms and IP rules are **unpublished** | **Two contradictory channels for the same dataset.** The 2026 Call for Proposals: "No, direct or indirect industry partnerships are not permitted." The **Pharma Pre-competitive Consortium** is expressly a pharma channel (Alnylam, Bayer, Boehringer Ingelheim, Novo Nordisk from 1 Dec 2025; **GSK from 5 May 2026**). **Both are live** | **Yes, but not as of right** — "on a case-by-case basis" | **Yes, contractually**, through a service agreement or research collaboration agreement. **Terms unpublished** | **No — a hard textual exclusion.** An "Affiliate" does not include an employee of any institution in Singapore other than a Singapore Public Institution, or any institution outside Singapore |
| **Overseas eligible?** | **Not under the published route.** No published application pathway exists for an overseas applicant | **Only via a Singapore-led application**; case-by-case for early access, with an expectation of demonstrable return of benefit to Singapore | **Case-by-case.** SG10K_Pilot is separately distributed through the European Genome-phenome Archive | **Not addressed.** Its privacy statement carries a GDPR section for EU and EEA users, which implies anticipated European engagement but is not an eligibility rule | **No — the same exclusion clause** |
| **Approvals required** | (1) **IRB approval, IRB waiver, or an institution-endorsed "Review Not Required" determination** — accepted since 26 January 2024 — *before* submitting. (2) Two-stage DAC review: secretariat preliminary assessment, then DAC final assessment on legitimacy, **public benefit**, relevance and risk. (3) **For sensitive datasets, a separate MOH gate: access only through an MOH-certified Micro-Access Laboratory** | (1) IRB approval, waiver or "Review Not Required". (2) PRECISE-SG100K DAC. (3) Scientific Committee. (4) **Additionally the TRUST DAC if EHR or clinical linkage is required.** (5) Business Development screen if industry is involved | (1) NPM DAC, assessed **against the existing IRB approvals**. (2) Institution signs the **non-negotiable Data Access Agreement**. (3) Platform Terms of Use. (4) **Mandatory onboarding training.** (5) Countersignature by an Institution Representative | (1) **The data host institute's DAC.** (2) The governance and contracting process it initiates. (3) A **data use agreement — unpublished**. IRB requirements not published | (1) NMRC eligibility check. (2) **The contributing institution's DAC — discretion expressly unfettered.** (3) Separate **IRB approval**. (4) PDPA and HBRA compliance. Upstream, the **Data Sharing Plan must be IRB-endorsed before work commences** |
| **Conditions on approved access** | **No download** — only aggregated research insights may be exported. **Small-cell suppression: any analysis involving fewer than 5 subjects will not be exported.** **Output vetting** — drafts to TRUST before publication for re-identification checks. Virtual desktop with internet separation and copy-paste disabled. No re-identification. Time-limited; data deleted on completion. **Personal Undertaking carrying personal liability and indemnification.** **Charging Framework in force from 1 January 2025 — schedule not public** | No downloading of datasets. Pre-publication review by **both** committees. **Linkage to external datasets not permitted.** **Exclusivity periods — 24 months after R1; 12 months after R2.** PRECISE claims no IP unless it made inventive contributions | Aggregated download only, as a general principle. No re-identification. Annual progress report. **Destroy the data at end of study or on demand.** Pre-submission manuscript review **including preprints**, expressly including evaluation for intellectual property. Technology disclosure to A\*STAR if IP is generated. Consent to public listing of the request. **Enforcement: the DAC reserves the right to block manuscript submission and may contact journals** | **Not published.** Governed by the unpublished data use agreement. BDH claims the **Five Safes** and **FAIR** principles | **3-year Access Term**, extension at NMRC's sole discretion. No attempt to identify; on discovery, immediate notification and suspension. Confidentiality, including a **publication gag on the obligations themselves** absent prior written consent. May patent but must consult the contributing institution first. Acknowledgement and possible **co-authorship**. **Public posting of a 300-word Research Use Statement** plus requestor and institution name. Completion report within 3 months. Revocation on breach. **Unilateral variation clause.** Singapore law; **Contracts (Rights of Third Parties) Act excluded** |
| **Appeal / finality** | "TRUST DAC's decision is final" | **Not published** | Appeals lie to the DAC itself; its decisions on appeals "will be regarded as final". **No external review** | **Not published** | Appeal to the Data Access Oversight Committee, whose "decision … shall be final" |
| **Governing documents public?** | **Partly — the best of the five on governance transparency, the worst on the operative contract.** PUBLIC: the **DAC Annual Report FY2024** (named members, criteria, two-stage process, timelines, caseload statistics, and an annex listing all 37 FY2024 approvals); the **approved-projects register** (92 items at retrieval); the Five Safes application; eligibility pages. **NOT PUBLIC:** the **Data Requestor Agreement**, Organisation Undertakings, Terms of Use, **Personal Undertaking**, contributor pre-agreements and the **Charging Framework schedule** | **Partly.** PUBLIC: access principles and process, eligibility, Call for Proposals terms, consent scope. **NOT PUBLIC:** no terms of reference, no member list, no chair, no appointing authority, no conflict-of-interest policy | **Yes for the rules — the most transparent of the five on its own instruments.** The **SG10K_Health and SG10K_Pilot Data Access Policies** and the **Data Access Form (Annex B, v3.1)** are publicly downloadable, and a **register of approved applications** is published. **NOT PUBLIC:** DAC terms of reference and membership; the Precision Medicine Steering Committee entirely. ⚠️ The historic portal host `npm.a-star.edu.sg` **does not resolve** (DNS failure, verified twice on 15 August 2026) — any citation to it must be checked | **Barely.** The fullest public account is a **19-slide A\*STAR deck of 9 February 2026** marked "ARES PUBLIC", found on another body's site. **NOT PUBLIC:** the data use agreement, the access policy, eligibility criteria, the application form and the dataset inventory. The metadata catalogue was "Coming Soon" in February 2026 and, as at 15 August 2026, `bdh.sg` still shows no inventory and no application route | **Yes — and uniquely so.** The **Framework**, the **Data Request Form containing the complete Terms of Use** (a full data use agreement), the Data Sharing Plan templates, the Instructions to Applicants, reviewer guidance and a PI-engagement Q&A are all **publicly downloadable**. **NOT PUBLIC:** the Data Access Oversight Committee's terms of reference and membership; the contributing institutions' own committees; and no public researcher-facing repository portal or metadata catalogue was located |

📌 **The count is at least five and arguably seven.** Beyond the five above: the **STCC Specimen and Data Access Committee** governs the Singapore Translational Cancer Consortium's **Cancer Data Commons** (oncology clinical records from all three clusters and their cancer centres, **2010 to 2024**, linked to biospecimen and multi-omic data) — and where an analysis links Cancer Data Commons data with MOH TRUST data, **TRUST DAC approval is additionally required**; its terms of reference, membership, application route and data use agreement are all unpublished. And the **federated biorepository route** runs through the public, searchable **Bio Repositories Catalogue** on the Clinical Trials Singapore portal, where "all sample requests are subjected to respective institutional review and approval" — approval stays at institution level.

## 5.3 The decision map — "I want X. Which pathway, and what must I obtain?"

**Read top to bottom. The first question is always *what data*, not *who are you*.**

### Step 1 — identify the data

| If you want… | Pathway | What you must obtain |
|---|---|---|
| **EHR-linked clinical real-world data** — diagnoses, medications, laboratory results, radiology, healthcare financing, death and registry data | **TRUST DAC** | Institution's Data Request Agreement with TRUST → TRUST membership → **IRB approval, waiver or "Review Not Required"** → TRUST Data Request → two-stage DAC review → Personal Undertakings from every analyst → analysis inside the TRUST environment → **output vetting before publication** |
| The same, but the dataset is flagged **sensitive or rare disease** | **TRUST DAC plus a second MOH gate** | All of the above, **plus** access through an **MOH-certified Micro-Access Laboratory**. Approval takes **minimally 6–8 weeks** rather than 4–6 |
| **PRECISE-SG100K genomic or research phenotype data, with no EHR linkage** | **PRECISE-SG100K DAC** | IRB approval, waiver or "Review Not Required" → PRECISE-SG100K DAC → Scientific Committee merit review → analysis in the secure environment (hosted on TRUST) → **pre-publication review by both committees**. Note the **exclusivity periods** |
| **PRECISE-SG100K data *linked to* EHR or clinical data** | **PRECISE-SG100K DAC *and* TRUST DAC** | Both approvals. PRECISE's own materials state the split: TRUST DAC if EHR clinical data is required; PRECISE-SG100K DAC for research phenotype and genomic data only. **Check first whether PRECISE's "Umbrella approval" already covers the project** |
| **SG10K_Health or SG10K_Pilot genomes** | **NPM DAC** | Application to the NPM DAC (assessed against **existing** IRB approvals) → institution signs the **non-negotiable Data Access Agreement** → platform Terms of Use → **mandatory onboarding training** → account. **Aggregated download only.** Manuscripts to the DAC before submission, preprints included |
| **Other biomedical research data** — omics, imaging, cohorts, assays held in A\*STAR's Biomed DAR or RAPTOR | **Biomedical Data Hub (federated)** | Identify the **data host institute**, apply to **its** committee, then contract on BDH's data use agreement. **There is no published application form, no published eligibility rule and no published policy** — the practical first step is the contact form |
| **Data deposited by an NMRC-funded project** (grants ≥ S$250,000; applications from 1 January 2024) | **NMRC Framework** | Submit the **Data Request Form** → NMRC checks eligibility → routed to the **contributing institution's committee, whose discretion is unfettered** → separate **IRB approval** → accept the **Terms of Use** (3-year Access Term). Appeal, if refused, to the Data Access Oversight Committee — whose decision is final. Onward delivery is through **TRUST** or **BRAIN** |
| **Oncology clinical records linked to biospecimens** | **STCC SDAC** — and **TRUST DAC in addition** if linked to MOH TRUST data | Route not published; approach STCC |
| **Human biological material or specimens** | **Institution level** | Search the public **Bio Repositories Catalogue**; requests are "subjected to respective institutional review and approval". For tissue, the HBRA and Human Tissue Framework apply, and for export **HBRA s 35(2)(f)** and Tissue Banking Regulations reg 15 |

### Step 2 — check who you are (this can end the enquiry)

| You are… | Where you can go | Where you are shut out |
|---|---|---|
| **A principal investigator at a Singapore public institution** | All five pathways | — |
| **A graduate student or postdoctoral researcher** | May analyse under an approved project | **Cannot be the requestor** on the NMRC route |
| **A Singapore private-sector researcher** | Biomedical Data Hub (through a service or collaboration agreement); the NUH Tissue Repository (separately priced) | **The NMRC repository — expressly excluded.** TRUST — **no published route**. STEMPA — out of scope |
| **An overseas academic** | Only as part of a **Singapore-led** PRECISE application; case-by-case at the NPM DAC; SG10K_Pilot through the European Genome-phenome Archive | **The NMRC repository — expressly excluded.** TRUST — **no published route** |
| **Industry or pharma wanting SG100K** | The **Pharma Pre-competitive Consortium** is the *only* channel that expressly admits you | The **Call for Proposals route bars you outright**, including indirect partnerships |
| **Industry wanting EHR real-world data** | **Nothing published.** Industry-character projects have been approved by the TRUST DAC and an industry framework is a live workstream — **but the terms are unpublished** | Everything published says public-sector only |

### Step 3 — the ethics gate you cannot skip

**Every one of the five pathways requires an IRB determination, and none of them provides it.** The IRB layer is statutory and sits *outside* the access structure: **HBRA Part 4 and the Human Biomedical Research Regulations 2017**. See §4.5.3.

The routes are **IRB approval**, an **IRB waiver of consent under HBRA s 13(1)(b)** (Fifth Schedule, Part 2), or — since **26 January 2024** on the TRUST route, and on the PRECISE route — an institution-endorsed **"Review Not Required"** determination. **Data-only research qualifies for expedited review** under reg 20(2)(b). **There is no external appeal from an IRB refusal** (HBRA s 21(4)).

### Step 4 — check whether STEMPA has already decided something for you

If the project is a **multi-party public-sector** research collaboration funded under NMRC OF-LCG or HHP MISSION Stream 1, **STEMPA's pre-aligned positions bind through the grant conditions and will have fixed data ownership and the Data Contributor and Data User roles *before* any access request is made** (§4.5.5). If any party is **industry or foreign**, or the project is a **clinical trial**, STEMPA does not apply and the parties are in open-ended bilateral negotiation.

## 5.4 The legal-basis analysis — and why it matters more than it looks

**Not one of the five bodies exists under, or draws authority from, any Act of Parliament.** Their decisions are contractual decisions made by unincorporated committees whose constitutions, in three of the five cases, are not published.

**Three qualifications stop that being an overstatement:**

1. **"Not statutory" is not "outside the law".** Two statutory constraints run through every pathway regardless of any approval: **HBRA s 27** (a duty on every holder of individually-identifiable research information to take all reasonable steps and safeguards, "including rendering information or material non-identifiable"; $10,000 / 12 months) and **HBRA s 28** (the re-identification bar; $20,000 / 2 years), with **s 29** and **s 39** doing the same for disclosure. **No committee approval can license a s 28 or s 29 contravention.**
2. **There is a statutory hook for the institutional layer, but it prescribes no content.** **HBRA s 23(2)(c)** requires every research institution to "formulate and implement appropriate standards, policies and procedures" — the footing on which the cluster manuals, university policies and institutional committees sit. **The Act says nothing about what those standards must contain, and no code has ever been issued under HBRA s 40 to fill the gap.**
3. **The public-sector route is a live but unrealised possibility.** Even if enTRUST or TRUST were to rest on the Public Sector (Governance) Act — which no retrieved source states — **the Public Sector (Governance) (Amendment) Act 2026 is wholly uncommenced.**

### 5.4.1 The one true statutory gateway, and why it is doing less work than it appears

**HBRA s 13(4)–(5)** (§4.5.1) is real, powerful and **narrow**. Two gates run in series, and neither substitutes for the other:

| | **HBRA s 13 waiver** | **Data access committee approval** |
|---|---|---|
| Question answered | *May this be done at all, notwithstanding confidentiality?* | *Will the holder permit it, and on what terms?* |
| Decided by | An IRB, exercising a **statutory** power | A committee exercising a **contractual** delegation |
| Effect of refusal | The research cannot proceed in identifiable form | The research cannot proceed on **this** data |
| Effect of a grant | Legal impediment removed | Access granted, subject to contractual conditions |

**A s 13 waiver removes a prohibition. It does not create an entitlement.** Nothing in s 13 obliges a data holder to disclose anything. **The holder retains an unfettered discretion to refuse — and that discretion is precisely what the access committee exercises.**

**And the operative conditions come only from the contractual layer.** Everything that constrains a researcher day to day — no download, aggregate-only export, five-subject suppression, output vetting, data destruction, IP consultation duties, co-authorship requirements, exclusivity periods, revocation and journal-contact powers — comes from the access contracts, not the statute. The HBRA's only affirmative data controls are **s 27** and **s 28**. **Strip out the contractual layer and the operative controls on Singapore health-data research largely disappear.**

**The decisive qualification: most of this data is not identifiable, so the HBRA is disengaged.** The HBRA bites through **s 3(2)(f)** — research involving "the use of any individually-identifiable health information". **Research on non-identifiable health information is outside the Act entirely**, and MOH's own guidance confirms that use of de-identified health information or human biological material does not require appropriate consent under the framework. What flows through these pathways is de-identified or pseudonymised at source in every case.

> **The conclusion to give the client, stated plainly.** Singapore's statutory gateway for secondary use of health data — HBRA s 13(4)–(5) — is real, powerful and narrow. It applies only to research on individually-identifiable health information. **The moment data is de-identified, the statute steps back and the entire governance of access passes to a set of contractual committees whose constitutions are largely unpublished and whose decisions are, by their own terms, final.** The contractual layer is not an optional overlay on a statutory scheme. Over most of the field it **is** the scheme.

**And the point that sharpens it into a risk: the threshold that decides which regime applies — *identifiability* — has no national standard.** There is no national de-identification standard; the PDPC's Guide to Basic Anonymisation sets no health-specific threshold; and the HBRA s 40(1)(b) codes have never been issued. **The NMRC Framework itself points at two different named benchmarks** — "the Framework on Anonymisation Standards for the Public Healthcare Sector or the Personal Data Protection Commission anonymisation guidelines" — and asks only for something "equivalent to or more robust than" them. TRUST applies its own two-level hashing. BDH claims the Five Safes. **The single question that determines whether the criminal law or a contract governs your data is answered differently by every actor in the system.**

⚠️ **The "Framework on Anonymisation Standards for the Public Healthcare Sector" is a named, load-bearing, apparently unpublished standard that nobody has retrieved.** See §6.

### 5.4.2 An unresolved point of construction — the most consequential in the domain

**HBRA s 13(3)(b)–(c) and s 13(4)–(5) pull in opposite directions on their face.** The natural reading — **flagged as a reading, not a finding, and not resolved by any retrieved source** — is that ss 13(4)–(5) protect the *researcher* acting pursuant to the waiver and remove the consent requirement, while s 13(3) preserves the *custodian's* separate s 29 and s 39 disclosure regime with its own gateways.

**On that reading, an IRB waiver does not by itself authorise a hospital to hand over identifiable records; the custodian still needs a s 29 gateway.** If it is right, an access committee's approval is not merely the holder's commercial permission — it is part of how the holder satisfies itself that it is within a s 29 gateway. **This should be put to a verifier, not asserted.**

## 5.5 Where the pathways overlap, conflict, or leave ungoverned ground

**Double-gating happens and nothing sequences it.** PRECISE-SG100K plus EHR linkage requires two committees. STCC Cancer Data Commons plus TRUST data requires two. TRUST sensitive datasets require a committee **and** a separate MOH certification of the physical Micro-Access Laboratory. NMRC-deposited data requires an eligibility check, the contributing institution's committee and a separate IRB, then delivery through TRUST or BRAIN, which may pull in TRUST's own conditions.

⚠️ **No published instrument sequences these, allocates precedence, or says what happens if one body approves and another refuses. Nor does anything say whether conditions imposed by one — PRECISE's exclusivity periods, NMRC's IP-consultation duty — bind inside another's environment.**

**Four direct conflicts:**

| Conflict | The two positions |
|---|---|
| **Industry access to PRECISE-SG100K** | The 2026 Call for Proposals bars direct or indirect industry partnerships; the **Pharma Pre-competitive Consortium** is expressly a five-company pharma channel into the same dataset. **Both are live** |
| **Industry access to TRUST — rule against practice** | Published eligibility is public-sector only and the industry framework "will soon enable" collaborations. **But industry-collaborative projects were approved on 20 December 2024.** The published rule and the actual practice diverge, and the framework governing the practice is unpublished |
| **Funder posture against infrastructure posture** | The **funder** instruments systematically exclude industry and overseas parties; the **infrastructure** instruments are being built to include them on bespoke, unpublished commercial terms. **A commercial client's exposure sits exactly in that gap, and it is governed entirely by unpublished agreements** |
| **Identifiability is party-relative, and no access framework reflects it** | PDPC's guidance to Singapore General Hospital (7 July 2026) holds that the same encrypted pathology patch files **are personal data to SGH but are not personal data to A\*STAR's AI centre or to the Biomedical Data Hub**, because only SGH holds the keys — and is expressly conditional on that remaining so. **None of the five frameworks operates a per-recipient identifiability test.** This is the newest and most consequential regulatory position in the field and it sits outside the access architecture entirely |

**And one structural point that runs across every layer: at every level, the decision is final and there is no external review.**

| Layer | Finality |
|---|---|
| IRB refusal | **HBRA s 21(4)** — "there is no appeal against the decision of the research institution or the second board" |
| TRUST DAC | "TRUST DAC's decision is final" |
| NPM DAC | Appeal decisions "will be regarded as final" |
| NMRC | "The decision of the NMRC Data Access Oversight Committee shall be final" |
| STEMPA data incidents | Standing Escalation Committee → HHP EXCO Co-Chairs for final determination |

⚠️ **This is not an assertion that these decisions are immune from supervisory jurisdiction** — that is a question no retrieved source addresses, and a "final" clause in a private contract cannot of itself oust a court. But **as a matter of the published architecture there is no appellate route out of any of these bodies, and in three of the five cases the deciding body's membership is not published either.** That combination is worth putting in front of the client explicitly.

## 5.6 What is public and what is not

**Public and readable today:** the TRUST DAC **Annual Report FY2024** (named members, criteria, process, timelines, caseload, and an annex listing all 37 FY2024 approvals); the TRUST **approved-projects register** (92 items); the **NPM SG10K_Health and SG10K_Pilot Data Access Policies** and Data Access Form, plus a published register of approved applications; **PRECISE-SG100K access principles**, eligibility rule and Call for Proposals terms; **the entire NMRC suite**, including a complete data use agreement as a downloadable form; the **STEMPA Guidebook (Beta, 52 pp)** and an 8-page public extract of its Data Sharing Guidebook; the **Bio Repositories Catalogue**; the **SmartCMS Subscription Agreement**; and the HBRA, its five sets of Regulations and MOH's HBRA guidance suite.

**Not public, and load-bearing:** every contract that actually governs access (TRUST's four instruments and its charging schedule; BDH's data use agreement and access policy; STCC's), the constitutions of three of the five deciding bodies, the **STEMPA Data Sharing Guidebook** in full, the **Public Sector Master Research Collaboration Agreement**, the **five-party Single IRB Memorandum of Agreement**, and the **NEHR contractual stack**.

📌 **The pattern: Singapore publishes its *governance narratives* — annual reports, principles pages, frameworks — and withholds its *operative contracts*.** The clean exception is the NMRC route, where the actual data use agreement is a public downloadable document. **That exception is worth putting in front of the client as the model of what the rest could look like.**

---

# 6. WHAT YOU CANNOT READ — the unpublished but operative instruments

## 6.1 Why this section exists, and the doctrinal answer

**An unpublished instrument is a finding, not a silence.** The bundle is stronger for naming these than for glossing over them.

And there is a **doctrinal answer to why they are unpublished**, set out at §2.2: **Interpretation Act s 23(1)(a)** requires subsidiary legislation to be gazetted; **s 2** defines subsidiary legislation as an instrument "having legislative effect"; and five statutes bearing on health data expressly place the operative normative instrument **outside that definition**. The Public Sector (Governance) Bill's own Explanatory Statement says it in terms: a direction "does not have legislative effect. **It is therefore not subsidiary legislation.**"

## 6.2 ⭐ The distinction that turns a complaint into an argument

**Unpublished contracts and unpublished public-law instruments are not the same objection, and they should not be run together.**

| | **Unpublished contracts** (the NEHR stack; TRUST's agreements; BDH's data use agreement; the STEMPA Data Sharing Guidebook; NHG and NUS policies) | **Unpublished public-law instruments** (PSGA data sharing directions; MediShield Life and CareShield Life certifications; PDPA s 26(2) transfer exemptions; HIA s 90(8) code waivers) |
|---|---|---|
| Nature | Bilateral, negotiated at least in form, and voluntarily entered | **Unilateral.** Made by a Minister or official; the affected party is not a party |
| The objection | **Third-party effect** — the patient whose data moves is bound by a contract they never saw; and **pre-contractual asymmetry** — the prospective party cannot read the terms before initiating the relationship | **Rule of law.** The instrument determines rights, duties and in some cases **criminal liability**, and no statute requires anyone to publish it |
| The sharpest instance | The **NEHR Data Share Agreement** — a private clinic cannot read the terms before applying; onboarding is not self-service | **PSGA s 7(1)** makes disclosure a criminal offence carrying up to **$5,000 or 2 years** unless authorised by a direction that **no statute requires anyone to publish** |

## 6.3 🔴 THE NEHR CONTRACTUAL STACK — corrected. It is six instruments, not one.

**Every account in the underlying research names a single instrument, the "NEHR Accession Agreement". That is incomplete in a way that matters.**

**The Accession Agreement is an execution deed acceding to a master instrument called the *NEHR Data Share Agreement*** — a name that appears **nowhere in the research corpus** — and accession is **at premise level**, not per legal entity. **The counterparty is MOH**, with Synapxe administering.

Established from **two independent primary sources**: the public API definition of Synapxe's "Request to participate in NEHR" form, whose own field text states that the onboarding process "involves acceding to the **NEHR Data Share Agreement**"; and the public **NEHR Briefing deck** (27 pp) at p 8, which states that "MOH requires the institution to accede to our **Data Sharing Agreement at premise level**".

**The corrected stack for a private clinic contributing to the NEHR:**

| # | Instrument | Public? |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **NEHR Data Share Agreement** — the master instrument, acceded to at premise level | **No** — and not previously named anywhere |
| 2 | **NEHR Accession Agreement** — the accession deed; the form collects only the signatory's name, email and contact number to be printed on it | **No** |
| 3 | **NEHR account provisioning form** | **No** |
| 4 | **Early Contribution Incentive Letter of Agreement** — **or** | **No** |
| 5 | **Voluntary Data Contribution Agreement** | **No** |
| 6 | On the vendor side: the **HIMS Provider Agreement** and an **NDA**, which gate release of the Onboarding Pack containing the interface specifications | **No** |

*(Source for items 3–5: MOH and AIC's primary-care circular "15th EDM on Digital Enablement for Healthier SG", 17 May 2024, which sets out the onboarding sequence and notes that signing the Voluntary Data Contribution Agreement is not required where the Early Contribution Incentive Letter of Agreement is already signed.)*

⚠️ **A group practice with multiple clinics accedes multiple times.** And **onboarding is not self-service**: the form states that the agreements will be prepared and the applicant contacted within three days. **A prospective contributor cannot read the terms before initiating a relationship with the operator.**

**Scope note:** the route covers Centre Based Care, Home Nursing, MSF Assisted Day Homes, Home Therapy, Community Case Management, Integrated Community Care Provider and **Retail Pharmacy**, with non-HCSA-licensed applicants requiring endorsement from AIC, SG Enable or HSA.

*(The **SmartCMS Subscription Agreement**, by contrast, **is public** — 19 pp with an NDA template, on an open content-delivery network. It was assumed unobtainable and was not.)*

## 6.4 🔴 Statutorily unpublishable public-law instruments

### (a) PSGA data sharing directions

**The operative instruments of the Public Sector (Governance) Act are directions.** They are not subsidiary legislation (s 9(2)), so the Interpretation Act gazetting duty does not reach them, and s 44's parliamentary-laying duty reaches only subsidiary legislation. **The PSGA has no subsidiary legislation at all beyond its 2018 commencement notification.**

🔴 **And they carry criminal liability. PSGA s 7(1) makes unauthorised disclosure an offence of up to $5,000 or 2 years where "the disclosure is not authorised by any data sharing direction". Whether a public official's conduct is criminal turns on an instrument nobody outside the recipient agency can read.**

**No data sharing direction in the health domain has ever been located.**

⚠️ **And read that negative correctly — this is the epistemic point the whole section turns on.** The directions are given under **PSGA s 4**, which was read in full: **it contains no publication requirement, and the word "Gazette" does not appear in it.** So a nil return from the gazette is **structurally guaranteed and evidentially worthless** on the question whether a direction exists. **"No gazette hits" can never by itself establish that an inter-agency health-data flow is unauthorised** (§2.4). What *can* be said with confidence is narrower and more useful: **s 6(1)** lifts only confidentiality "under the common law", **s 6(2)** preserves legal privilege and contract, and **s 11(1)** provides that a direction "must not be inconsistent with this Act or any other written law" — so **no direction, published or not, can authorise a disclosure that a written law forbids, and none can reach a recipient outside the Singapore public sector.** Worked through in full at §4.15.4.

### (b) MediShield Life and CareShield Life ministerial certifications

**Every gazette item ever made under both Acts was enumerated — 41 and 18 respectively — and full-text phrase searches run. No certification or approval under MediShield Life Scheme Act Part 5 or CareShield Life and Long-Term Care Act Part 8 has ever been published.** The only public trace is a bulleted list on an MOH webpage (last updated 29 October 2024) naming **CHAS, Screen for Life, the Home Caregiving Grant, IDAPE, ElderFund and CareShield Life**.

🔴 **And MSLA s 30(2) and CSLA s 34(2) expressly permit *class-wide* certifications — so a single unpublished certificate can authorise a population-scale data flow.**

Both Acts use "by notification in the Gazette" and "by order in the Gazette" freely elsewhere, and both require regulations to be presented to Parliament after gazetting. **The Part 5 and Part 8 certifications that actually authorise health data to move from hospitals to the CPF Board and to private insurers carry no publication requirement of any kind.** They are made under the Minister's hand and that is all.

### (c) PDPA s 26(2) transfer exemptions

**s 26(3)(b) provides that they need not be gazetted.** The set of granted exemptions is therefore **structurally unknowable**.

### (d) HIA s 90(8) code waivers

**s 90(7) makes compliance with a code mandatory; s 90(8) lets the Minister waive it "to any person" with no publicity requirement at all — while s 110 requires an exemption from the Act to be by order in the Gazette.** Within a single statute, exemption from the Act is gazetted and waiver from the mandatory code is invisible.

### (e) Data Protection Appeal Committee decisions

**PDPC publishes none, and eLitigation returns zero.** **There is no publication duty**; publication is purely discretionary. There is consequently **no court judgment on appeal from PDPC involving health data**. *(The Data Protection Appeal Panel's membership was gazetted between 2015 and 2017 and nothing since.)*

## 6.5 📌 Instruction Manual 8 — the corpus position is superseded and must not be repeated

**Do not write that "no public version of IM8 exists".** The accurate position, verified:

**What is NOT published, on the Government's own statement.** The PSDSRC Report of November 2019, **Annex F para 20**, records that "the Government does not publish the IM8 PDM in the public domain". **That finding drove Recommendations 4.5 and 4.6** — publish the personal data protection policies, and publish an annual update — **and the published extracts below are the implementation of that recommendation.** So the publication position is a documented, traceable policy decision, not an omission. The manual itself sits behind TechPass and `.gov.sg` authentication; the IM8 portal is not publicly resolvable.

**What IS published, all verified:**

| Source | Content |
|---|---|
| **`info.standards.tech.gov.sg`** | A live GovTech-operated public portal publishing **approximately 250 individually-numbered controls across 26 domains** — including Access Control, Application Security, Cryptography, Data Protection, Generative AI, Logging, Network Security and Secure Development, plus the Digital Service Standards and WCAG domains. Each control carries a **Control Statement, Control Recommendations or Guidance, and a Risk Statement.** GovTech's own former IM8 page redirects here, and the portal's control text refers to "relevant IM8 SSPs". **Eight System Security Plans** are also published (low, medium and high-risk cloud and on-premises, generative AI, and sandbox) |
| **`github.com/GovTechSG/tech-standards`** | Official GovTech organisation, **MIT licence**. The file `catalogs/im8-reform.json` (296KB) carries OSCAL metadata whose title is literally **"Instruction Manual 8 Reform"**, version `2025.05.13`, created by "Workstream 1A (Content), IM8-reform Executive Committee" — **15 domain groups and 137 controls** with full prose. ⚠️ **Carry the README's own caveat:** it is "a public reference for industry partners, providing access to **similar** control requirements" — a published mirror, **not** a certified verbatim reproduction |
| **`file.go.gov.sg`** | Three clause-numbered extracts: the **Government Data Security Policies** (22 pp, 61 clauses); the **Government Personal Data Protection Policies** (44 pp, 51 clauses, July 2021); and **Key Policies of the Government's Third-Party Management Framework** (5 pp, 6 clauses, April 2020). ⚠️ **All three carry the caveat that the Government may update the policies "without publishing such updates"** |

**What could not be found — an absence of evidence only.** No public table of contents for the *full* manual. The reform catalogue covers cybersecurity and digital service standards, with **no public counterpart for the procurement, project-lifecycle, human-resource or classified-information chapters**.

**And no Parliamentary answer ever gives a reason for confidentiality.** On 4 February 2020 a Nominated Member asked whether the Instruction Manuals would be made public and the Minister of State **declined to answer substantively**. ⚠️ **That is a deflection, not a justification — say it that way.**

📌 **A legal hook worth using.** In a ministerial statement of **6 March 2025**, the Minister for Digital Development and Information stated that ACRA's failure "contravened the Government's internal rules on data management, namely, IM8", which it "was required to comply with **under the Public Sector (Governance) Act**". **That is the clearest available statement linking IM8 compliance to a statutory obligation.** The fullest public description of IM8's contents ever given in Parliament is from 6 May 2019. **Note the scale of the parliamentary record: "IM8" appears in exactly four sitting reports in the entire Hansard corpus, 1955 to the present.**

⛔ **Reject a vendor claim that IM8 "consists of 27 domains and 53 controls" and "maps to 669 other frameworks".** It is uncorroborated by any government source and is **positively contradicted** by GovTech's own artefacts. **Do not adopt those figures from any source.**

⚠️ **One health-specific point survives all of this:** the published **Government Data Security Policies** glossary defines "Agency" so as to **exclude the healthcare clusters** (§4.4).

## 6.6 The register of unpublished but operative instruments

| # | Instrument | Class | What is known | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|
